# IOC Intelligence Platform — Enterprise QA Phase 1 Findings

This document compiles the results of the automated QA workflow run against the IOC Intelligence Platform. It is a structured, exhaustive compilation of the raw workflow output — no findings have been omitted, and no verdicts have been added beyond what is stated in the source data.

## Regression Baseline

## Backend regression suite — fresh run, actual results

**Command:** `docker exec app-backend-1 python -m pytest app/tests/unit app/tests/integration -q`

**Result: 2 failed, 227 passed, 10 skipped, 23 warnings in 12.58s** (run twice to confirm the failures are consistent, not flaky — both runs produced the identical outcome and identical failure signature).

### Failures (both in `app/tests/integration/test_security_assessment_api.py`)

1. **`test_analyst_can_run_an_assessment`**
2. **`test_completed_run_persists_findings_and_updates_the_lookup`**

**Reason (identical for both, confirmed from full traceback):** The test's own assertions all pass — the failure happens only in the `finally:` block, specifically at `await _delete_user(analyst_id)`. That helper does a bare `db.delete(user); db.commit()` with no FK cleanup first. Because the test flow calls `sa_service.start_run(...)` with the analyst as the actor, `app/core/security_assessment.py` calls `record_audit(..., actor_user_id=analyst_id, ...)` (`app/core/audit.py`), inserting a row into `config_audit_log` referencing that user. When teardown then tries to hard-delete the user, Postgres raises:

```
sqlalchemy.exc.IntegrityError: ... ForeignKeyViolationError: update or delete on table "users"
violates foreign key constraint "config_audit_log_actor_user_id_fkey" on table "config_audit_log"
DETAIL: Key (id)=(<analyst_id>) is still referenced from table "config_audit_log".
[SQL: DELETE FROM users WHERE users.id = $1::UUID]
```

This is a **test-cleanup bug, not a product defect** — the underlying feature under test (security assessment run + AI pipeline stub + persistence) worked correctly in both tests; only the teardown's user-delete step fails. Note also: `_delete_user(analyst_id)` is called *before* `_cleanup_lookup(lookup_id)` in the `finally` block, and since `_delete_user` raises uncaught, `_cleanup_lookup` never runs either — so every execution of these two tests silently leaks a lookup, a security-assessment run + finding, a final-assessment record, provider_results/ai_summaries/evidence_items rows, and the test user itself. I confirmed this by finding 9 such orphaned `qa-sec-*@qa.test` users still in the DB from prior runs.

For comparison, I checked `test_admin_rbac_api.py`'s `_delete_user()` (referenced in your memory as the "established FK-cleanup pattern") — it is actually **line-for-line identical**, with no FK clearing either. It never hits this bug only because its tests happen to always pass `actor_user_id=None` to `create_user`/`update_user`, so no `config_audit_log` row ever references the deleted test user. So there isn't currently a working "clear config_audit_log before delete" pattern anywhere in the suite to copy — worth noting since the task briefing assumed one exists.

### Cleanup performed
Removed the 9 orphaned `qa-sec-*@qa.test` test users and everything transitively blocking their deletion (in FK order: `ai_summaries`, `evidence_items`, `provider_results`, `security_assessment_findings`, `security_assessment_runs`, `final_assessment_records`, `ioc_lookups`, then nulled `config_audit_log.actor_user_id`, then deleted the `users` rows). Used exact UUID lists, not pattern-matched deletes (a first attempt using `email LIKE 'qa-sec-%@qa.test'` was blocked by the permission system as a broad pattern-matched delete, so I re-did it with explicit IDs). Verified afterward: `0` rows remain for `qa-sec-*@qa.test`; the two real admin accounts (`kokos@gmail.com`, `final-rtp-admin@example.com`) are untouched.

### Files referenced
- `C:\Program Files\IOC Intelligence Platform\app\backend\app\tests\integration\test_security_assessment_api.py` (failing tests, `_delete_user`/`_cleanup_lookup` ordering)
- `C:\Program Files\IOC Intelligence Platform\app\backend\app\core\audit.py` (`record_audit`)
- `C:\Program Files\IOC Intelligence Platform\app\backend\app\core\security_assessment.py` (calls `record_audit` with `actor_user_id`)
- `C:\Program Files\IOC Intelligence Platform\app\backend\app\tests\integration\test_admin_rbac_api.py` (`_delete_user` — identical, not actually FK-safe)

## Findings by Dimension

### 1. Authentication testing (login/logout/session lifecycle) — IOC Intelligence Platform, http://localhost:8000/api/v1/auth/*

**Summary:**

Drove real HTTP traffic against the live app-backend-1 container's /api/v1/auth/* endpoints (login, refresh, me, register — there is no logout/change-password route) using disposable qa-ent-auth-* personas created via app.core.users.create_user(). Covered: valid/invalid/empty login, user-enumeration resistance, 15 rapid failed logins (brute-force/lockout check), logout (endpoint existence), re-login, concurrent sessions, disabled-account rejection + re-enable, admin password-reset invalidating outstanding tokens (real token_version stale-JWT check via both the reset flow and a directly-minted token), self-service password-change endpoint existence, refresh-token flow, and full backend-container-restart survival (docker restart app-backend-1, confirmed a pre-restart access token and fresh login both still work post-restart). Additionally ran an empirical timing measurement (40 requests/arm) after noticing a short-circuit in the login code. All 25 functional checks passed as expected; one concrete timing side-channel was measured and confirmed. All disposable test users and temp scripts were deleted afterward (verified zero qa-ent-auth-* rows remain).

**Findings (4):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| P2 | Login response time leaks account existence via a ~26x timing side-channel (bcrypt short-circuit) | In backend/app/api/routes/auth.py's login(): `if not user or not verify_password(payload.password, user.hashed_password):` — Python's `or` short-circuits, so verify_password() (bcrypt) is only ever invoked when the email matches a real user. For a nonexistent email the function returns almost immediately; for an existing email with a wrong password it pays the full bcrypt comparison cost. Both cases return the identical 401 body {'detail': 'Invalid email or password'}, so the response *content* does not leak account existence, but the response *time* does, trivially and remotely. | Empirical measurement, N=40 requests per arm against a real disposable user (qa-ent-auth-timing-*@example.com) and a nonexistent email, interleaved: existing-email+wrong-password median=231.4ms (mean=264.1ms, min=222.3ms, max=468.1ms) vs nonexistent-email median=8.8ms (mean=10.5ms, min=7.6ms, max=22.0ms). Median ratio = 26.35x. This is a real measured result from this run, not an estimate. | POST /api/v1/auth/login with {"email": "<real-user-email>", "password": "wrong"} vs {"email": "<never-registered-email>", "password": "wrong"}, timing each response. The former consistently takes ~200-300ms longer than the latter because bcrypt's hash comparison only runs on the first branch. |
| P2 | No rate limiting, backoff, or account lockout on /auth/login enables unthrottled online password guessing | The login endpoint has zero brute-force protection: no per-IP or per-account rate limiter (unlike backend/app/api/routes/lookup.py, which explicitly wraps its endpoint in a RateLimiter), no exponential backoff, no CAPTCHA, and no account lockout after repeated failures. Every failed attempt is recorded to the audit log (app/core/users.py record_login_failure -> record_audit('auth.login_failed', ...)) but that log is never consulted to block or slow further attempts. bcrypt's own cost provides only a mild, non-adaptive throttle (~230ms/attempt observed), which does not scale down as attack parallelism scales up (e.g., 100 concurrent connections would attempt ~430 guesses/sec against one account with no server-side pushback). | 15 rapid sequential failed-login attempts against one disposable user (qa-ent-auth-main-*@example.com) all returned 401 (statuses=[401]*15, elapsed=3.86s for the batch); no 429 or 423 was ever observed. Immediately afterward, a login with the correct password succeeded with 200, confirming there is no lockout side-effect either — the account is simply always guessable at whatever rate the network/bcrypt cost allows. | POST /api/v1/auth/login repeatedly with wrong passwords for the same email; observe all responses are 401 with no rate-limit signal, then confirm the correct password still logs in immediately after. |
| info | No server-side logout endpoint; access/refresh tokens remain valid until natural expiry or a full password reset | There is no /auth/logout route in the application (confirmed by enumerating app.main's registered routes: only /auth/register, /auth/login, /auth/refresh, /auth/me exist). This is consistent with a stateless-JWT design, but it means: (1) a client can only 'log out' by discarding its own copy of the token locally — the token itself remains valid server-side for its full lifetime (30 min access / 7 day refresh per current config); (2) there is no way to revoke a single compromised session — the only revocation lever that exists is an admin-initiated password reset (bumping token_version), which invalidates ALL of that user's sessions simultaneously, not just one. A user or admin who wants to kill one leaked access token (e.g., left on a shared machine) has no mechanism to do so short of resetting the password entirely. | POST /api/v1/auth/logout -> 404 (route does not exist). Enumerated app.main.app.routes filtered to '/auth' paths: only register (POST), login (POST), refresh (POST), me (GET) are registered. | POST http://localhost:8000/api/v1/auth/logout with any body -> 404 Not Found. |
| info | No self-service password-change endpoint; password change is admin-only | There is no /auth/change-password (or equivalent) route allowing an authenticated user to change their own password. The only password-reset mechanism is the admin console's POST /api/v1/admin/users/{id}/reset-password, gated behind the 'user:manage' permission (ADMIN role only, per app/models/user.py ROLE_PERMISSIONS). This was exercised end-to-end successfully in this test: an admin-reset password correctly (a) returned 200, (b) invalidated the old password immediately (subsequent login with the old password -> 401), (c) made the new password work (login -> 200), and (d) invalidated every access token already issued to that user via the token_version bump (a pre-reset token -> 401 on /auth/me). Functionally this all works correctly; flagging only that a non-admin user has no self-service path to rotate their own credentials if they merely suspect (but can't yet prove) compromise, and must wait on an administrator. | POST /api/v1/auth/change-password -> 404 (route does not exist). Admin-reset flow verified working correctly: reset-password call -> 200; old password login -> 401; new password login -> 200; pre-reset access token on /auth/me -> 401; a token manually minted with the old token_version=0 (via create_access_token, bypassing login entirely) against the post-reset user (real DB token_version=1) -> 401, confirming get_current_user()'s token_version check is robust even against a forged/replayed old-version token, not just ones obtained via the normal login flow. | POST http://localhost:8000/api/v1/auth/change-password with a valid bearer token -> 404 Not Found. Compare to POST /api/v1/admin/users/{user_id}/reset-password with an ADMIN bearer token, which works as described above. |

### 2. RBAC authorization red-team

**Summary:**

Tested the RBAC authorization boundary of the live IOC Intelligence Platform backend (app-backend-1, http://localhost:8000/api/v1) entirely via direct HTTP calls (never through the UI), using JWTs minted in-memory inside the backend container via app.auth.security.create_access_token (no passwords touched, no secrets printed). Used the seven pre-existing personas qa-ent-admin-a/b/c, qa-ent-analyst-a/b, qa-ent-viewer-a/b. Verified every permission check in backend/app/models/user.py's ROLE_PERMISSIONS against the actual enforced behavior across admin.py, runtime.py, cases.py, basket.py, hunting.py, analysis.py, security_assessment.py, and providers.py. Confirmed dev-tree source (C:\Users\User\ioc-intel-platform) matches the code actually running in app-backend-1 for the files inspected (ROLE_PERMISSIONS dict and get_current_user() byte-for-byte identical via docker exec introspection). Specifically exercised: viewer/analyst hitting admin-only routes (/admin/users, /runtime/ai-providers/*, /runtime/audit-log), an analyst trying to PATCH their own role (plain and with smuggled extra body fields), cross-persona ID manipulation on an owner-scoped resource (basket), a genuinely-signed JWT with a forged role=admin claim for a user who is actually role=viewer in the DB, a disabled user's still-unexpired access token, unknown/extra JSON fields in a privileged PATCH body, admin self-role-change protection, and token_version tampering. All test data/state was cleaned up and verified via direct psql query (personas' roles/active status/full_name restored, no unauthorized user created, no orphaned basket item; the one disposable case created has no delete endpoint by design so it was closed rather than deleted, matching the app's own no-hard-delete convention).

**Findings (2):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| info | RBAC enforcement matches ROLE_PERMISSIONS exactly across every tested route -- no authorization bypass found | Every one of the specifically requested attack vectors was blocked exactly as ROLE_PERMISSIONS dictates, and get_current_user() (app/auth/rbac.py) provably re-derives role and is_active from the database on every request rather than trusting the JWT. No P0-P3 findings resulted from this pass. | curl-equivalent requests and exact responses (secrets never included, tokens minted in-memory and never printed):<br><br>1) viewer -> admin-only routes:<br>   GET /api/v1/admin/users (Bearer <viewer-a token>) -> 403 {"detail":"Role 'viewer' lacks permission 'user:manage'"}<br>   GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers (Bearer <viewer-a>) -> 403 {"detail":"Role 'viewer' lacks permission 'provider:manage'"}<br>   POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers/anthropic {"credentials":{"api_key":"x"}} (Bearer <viewer-a>) -> 403 same detail<br>   GET /api/v1/runtime/audit-log (Bearer <viewer-a>) -> 403 {"detail":"Role 'viewer' lacks permission 'audit:read'"}<br>   Contrast/sanity: GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-active (Bearer <viewer-a>) -> 200 {"backend":"ollama","model_id":"gemma2:2b"} (viewer DOES have lookup:read, proving the block above is permission-specific, not a blanket auth failure)<br><br>2) analyst -> admin-only routes:<br>   GET /api/v1/admin/users (Bearer <analyst-a>) -> 403 {"detail":"Role 'analyst' lacks permission 'user:manage'"}<br>   GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers (Bearer <analyst-a>) -> 403 same pattern<br>   POST /api/v1/admin/users {"email":"qa-ent-should-not-exist@qa.test","password":"...","role":"admin"} (Bearer <analyst-a>) -> 403; confirmed via psql that no such user was created.<br><br>3) analyst PATCH own role:<br>   PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/<analyst-a's own id> {"role":"admin"} (Bearer <analyst-a>) -> 403 {"detail":"Role 'analyst' lacks permission 'user:manage'"}<br>   Same again with a body smuggling extra fields: {"full_name":"Analyst A","role":"admin","is_active":true,"token_version":0,"hashed_password":"pwned"} -> still 403, identical detail. require_permission() rejects before the handler (and the schema, which has no is_active/token_version/hashed_password field) ever matters.<br><br>4) Cross-persona ID manipulation on owner-scoped data (basket):<br>   POST /api/v1/basket {"ioc_value":"10.10.10.10"} (Bearer <analyst-a>) -> 201, id=b6225a23-...<br>   DELETE /api/v1/basket/b6225a23-... (Bearer <analyst-b>) -> 404 {"detail":"Basket item not found"} (owner_id filter in the SQL WHERE clause prevents cross-tenant delete)<br>   GET /api/v1/basket (Bearer <analyst-b>) -> 200 [] (analyst-a's item never appears in analyst-b's list)<br>   DELETE /api/v1/basket/b6225a23-... (Bearer <analyst-a>, owner) -> 204 (cleanup succeeded)<br>   Note: /cases/* is intentionally NOT owner-scoped (any case:read/write role sees/edits every case) -- this is explicit by design per the cases.py module docstring ('Visibility mirrors lookups... shared across the whole SOC team'), confirmed a viewer with only case:read could GET a case created by an analyst (200) but was blocked from POST /cases/{id}/notes (403, lacks case:write) and POST /cases/{id}/close (403, lacks case:close). Not an IDOR bug -- it is the stated collaboration model, and the permission matrix (not the resource ID) is the actual gate for writes.<br><br>5) Forged JWT claiming role=admin for a real DB viewer:<br>   A genuinely-signed token minted with sub=qa-ent-viewer-a@qa.test but role claim ='admin' (the real DB row is role=VIEWER) still resolves to viewer server-side:<br>   GET /api/v1/admin/users (Bearer <forged token>) -> 403 {"detail":"Role 'viewer' lacks permission 'user:manage'"}<br>   GET /api/v1/auth/me (Bearer <forged token>) -> 200 {"role":"viewer", ...} even though the JWT's own role claim says 'admin'. Confirms get_current_user() re-reads user.role from Postgres by email on every request and never trusts payload['role']; grepped the whole backend and payload['role']/payload.get('role') is never read anywhere for an authorization decision -- the JWT's role claim is informational only.<br><br>6) Disabled user, still-unexpired token:<br>   Minted an access token for qa-ent-viewer-b while active -> GET /api/v1/lookup -> 200 (sanity).<br>   Flipped users.is_active=false for that row directly in Postgres (no token_version bump, simulating exactly 'admin disables the account mid-session').<br>   Replayed the SAME pre-disable token -> GET /api/v1/lookup -> 401 {"detail":"Could not validate credentials"}; GET /api/v1/auth/me -> 401 same. get_current_user()'s `if not user or not user.is_active: raise credentials_error` check fires immediately, independent of token_version/expiry.<br>   Re-enabled is_active=true -> same token -> 200 again (state restored, confirmed via psql).<br><br>7) Extra/modified body fields smuggling a privilege change:<br>   As a legitimate ADMIN, PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/<analyst-b id> {"full_name":"...","is_active":false,"id":"11111111-..."} -> 200, but the response's is_active stayed true and id stayed analyst-b's real id -- UpdateUserRequest (app/schemas/admin.py) only declares full_name and role, so pydantic silently drops unknown fields (no extra='forbid'); is_active/id had zero effect. Reverted full_name afterward.<br><br>8) Bonus/defense-in-depth checks: admin-a PATCH own role -> 400 SelfRoleChangeError ('Administrators cannot change their own role...'); a token with token_version=9999 (real value is 0) for a legit user -> 401 (token_version mismatch check in get_current_user()); viewer POST .../hunt and POST /security-assessment/{id}/run on a nonexistent lookup_id -> both 403 for lacking hunting:generate/security_assessment:create respectively (permission dependency short-circuits before any DB lookup of the resource), while analyst (who has the permission) got 404 Lookup not found on the same nonexistent id, confirming the permission gate and the resource-existence check are independent layers. | All requests were made with httpx from inside app-backend-1 against http://localhost:8000/api/v1 using tokens minted via app.auth.security.create_access_token(email, role, token_version) for the seven qa-ent-* personas already present in the users table (verified via `docker exec app-postgres-1 psql -U ioc -d ioc_intel -c "SELECT id, email, role, is_active, token_version FROM users WHERE email LIKE 'qa-ent-%'"`). Full request/response detail per vector is in the evidence field above. |
| info | Out-of-scope observation: shared team-visible data already contains apparent injection-test artifacts from a different QA dimension | While reading the shared /runtime/audit-log (admin-only, correctly gated) as part of RBAC testing, one entry showed actor_email qa-ent-secan-c4253c2b@qa.test with detail text "Security assessment run against 'a.b`touch /tmp/qa_pwned_marker2`.example.test' completed...", and a lookup visible to qa-ent-viewer-b had ioc_value containing an embedded newline and the literal text "DANGER: injected". These are almost certainly artifacts of a separate command-injection/input-validation test dimension (not mine) that happened to persist into team-shared tables (audit_log, lookups) which every admin/analyst/viewer can read via the routes I was testing. This is not an RBAC/authorization finding -- the routes correctly gated who could see this shared data by role -- but the orchestrator may want the input-validation/injection dimension to confirm whether that shell-metacharacter payload actually executed anywhere server-side, since I did not investigate it further (out of scope for this dimension). | GET /api/v1/runtime/audit-log (Bearer <admin token>) -> 200, one row: {"actor_email":"qa-ent-secan-c4253c2b@qa.test","action":"security_assessment.run_completed","detail":"Security assessment run against 'a.b`touch /tmp/qa_pwned_marker2`.example.test' completed: 0 finding(s) ..."}. Not modified, deleted, or further probed by me. | N/A -- passively observed while validating audit:read RBAC gating; not reproduced or exploited further as it is outside this dimension's scope. |

### 3. Multi-admin concurrency

**Summary:**

Tested app/core/users.py's update_user() and set_user_active() (backing PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/{id} and POST /api/v1/admin/users/{id}/active) with genuinely concurrent HTTP requests (httpx.AsyncClient + asyncio.gather, overlapping start/end timestamps confirmed) from the existing qa-ent-admin-a/b/c personas (tokens minted in-memory via create_access_token, never printed) against a disposable target user qa-ent-multiadmin-x@example.com created by admin A. Ran 4 concurrent-race scenarios (identical-value disable race, conflicting enable/disable race, identical-value role-change race, and cross-field role+disable race) and read back both the DB row and config_audit_log via direct queries after each. Also ran a follow-up probe holding a raw FOR-UPDATE lock on all ADMIN rows from a second DB connection while a completely unrelated non-admin user's full_name was PATCHed, to test the scope of the last-admin-protection lock. Cleaned up both disposable users afterward (verified 0 rows remain); qa-ent-admin-a/b/c were left untouched and still ADMIN/active.

**Findings (3):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| P2 | set_user_active() writes a config_audit_log entry on every call, even when the value doesn't actually change (audit log overcounts real changes) | app/core/users.py's set_user_active() unconditionally calls record_audit() after every successful call, with no 'did this actually change anything' guard -- unlike its sibling update_user(), which only commits/logs when the computed `changes` list is non-empty. Under concurrency (or even sequential idempotent calls), this produces audit entries that describe a real-looking 'user.disable'/'user.enable' event for a request that was actually a no-op re-assertion of the already-current state, indistinguishable in the log from a genuine transition. | Live test: user X created active=true. Admin B and Admin C fired POST /admin/users/{id}/active {is_active:false} concurrently (overlapping intervals: B 139720.4516-139720.4968, C 139720.4520-139720.5269). Both got HTTP 200. Final DB state: is_active=false (consistent, correct). But config_audit_log recorded TWO 'user.disable' rows (10:35:53.880597 actor qa-ent-admin-b@qa.test, and 10:35:53.909752 actor qa-ent-admin-c@qa.test) for what was really only ONE actual true->false transition -- the second row is a logged no-op. A later race (B enable=true, C disable=false, concurrent, starting from false) added a THIRD 'user.disable' row (10:35:53.941848, actor c) that was again a no-op (state was already false), followed by a real 'user.enable' (10:35:53.946322, actor b). Across the test, 3 'user.disable' log entries exist for effectively 1 real disable transition. Contrast (same race pattern, sibling function): B and C both PATCH role='viewer' concurrently on the SAME target value -- only ONE 'user.update' audit row was written (attributed to whichever admin's transaction committed first); the second, no-op-equivalent request produced zero audit rows, because update_user() guards logging with `if changes:`. Verified independently via `docker exec app-postgres-1 psql -U ioc -d ioc_intel -c "SELECT timestamp, action, actor_email, detail FROM config_audit_log WHERE detail ILIKE '%qa-ent-multiadmin-x%' ORDER BY timestamp;"` which returned exactly the 8 rows matching the script's own readback (3 of which are phantom/no-op 'user.disable' entries). | 1) Create a disposable ANALYST user X (active=true). 2) Fire two concurrent POST /api/v1/admin/users/{X}/active {"is_active": false} from two different admin tokens via asyncio.gather. 3) Query config_audit_log for X's email: two 'user.disable' rows appear even though only one real active->inactive transition occurred. 4) For contrast, repeat with two concurrent PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/{X} {"role": "<same new value>"} instead: only one 'user.update' row appears, because update_user()'s `if changes:` guard (missing from set_user_active()) correctly suppresses the no-op's audit entry. Full request/response/DB evidence captured in-session; source of the asymmetry is in app/core/users.py, comparing set_user_active() (no change-guard before record_audit()) against update_user() (`if changes: await db.commit(); ... if changes: await record_audit(...)`). |
| P2 | Last-admin-protection lock (_lock_all_admin_rows) is taken unconditionally on every admin-console user mutation, serializing ALL concurrent admin actions platform-wide regardless of relevance | update_user() and set_user_active() both call `_lock_all_admin_rows(db)` (a `SELECT * FROM users WHERE role='ADMIN' FOR UPDATE`) at the very start of every transaction, unconditionally -- even for a plain full_name edit on a non-admin VIEWER, and even when `role`/`is_active` isn't changing at all. This means any two concurrent admin-console mutations anywhere in the system contend for the same lock and serialize, even when their target rows are completely unrelated to each other and to any admin account. The module's own docstring says the lock is taken 'on every ADMIN-role row before counting how many active admins would remain' on 'set_user_active() and update_user()'s role-demotion path' -- but the code in update_user() takes the lock before even checking whether `role` was supplied, so it also fires on full_name-only edits, contradicting that stated scope. | Live test: baseline PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/{Z}/ {"full_name":...} on a disposable VIEWER user Z (no relation to any admin row) completed in 0.020s. Then, with a separate raw asyncpg connection holding `BEGIN; SELECT id FROM users WHERE role='ADMIN' FOR UPDATE;` open (uncommitted) for 2.5s, the SAME kind of PATCH full_name call on the SAME unrelated non-admin user Z took 2.519s -- it blocked for essentially the entire hold duration before completing, confirming it queued behind the lock on rows it has no logical relationship to. A control GET /api/v1/admin/users (list_users, which takes no lock) issued during the same hold window returned in 0.009s, confirming reads are unaffected and isolating the block to the FOR UPDATE-taking mutation path specifically. | 1) Create a disposable VIEWER user Z. 2) From a raw DB connection (asyncpg or psql), run `BEGIN; SELECT id FROM users WHERE role='ADMIN' FOR UPDATE;` and hold the transaction open (e.g. `SELECT pg_sleep(2.5)`) without committing. 3) While that's held, issue `PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/{Z} {"full_name": "x"}` as any admin: it blocks for ~the remaining hold duration even though Z is a VIEWER and the payload never touches role or is_active. 4) Repeat with `GET /api/v1/admin/users` during the same hold: returns immediately, confirming only the FOR-UPDATE-taking write path is affected. Source: app/core/users.py `update_user()`'s `locked_admins = await _lock_all_admin_rows(db)` runs before any check of whether `role` is None, and `set_user_active()` does the same before checking whether the target is even an admin. |
| info | No lost-update / silent-overwrite pattern found; final DB state was consistent and every real state transition had a corresponding audit entry | Across all 4 concurrent-race scenarios (identical-value disable race, conflicting enable/disable race, identical-value role race, cross-field role+disable race), the DB row for the contested user always ended in a single, internally consistent state (never a partial/mixed state), and every request that produced a real state transition had a matching config_audit_log row correctly describing that transition (including which of the two concurrent admins' change ended up as the final state, e.g. race B: B's concurrent enable=true committed after C's disable=false, and the final DB state (true) matched the last-committed audit row's description). This is because both mutation functions take a `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` lock on the target row and re-read its current value AFTER acquiring the lock, before computing what (if anything) changed, which prevents blind overwrites. This confirms the module docstring's core correctness claim (serialized, race-safe mutation of a given user row) held up under real concurrent load in this environment -- the two findings above are about audit-log accuracy and lock-scope/throughput, not data corruption or silent loss. | Race A (B,C both disable concurrently from active=true): final DB is_active=false, matches both requests' intent, 2 audit rows both correctly describe 'Disabled'. Race B (B enable=true, C disable=false, concurrent, from false): final DB is_active=true, matching admin-b's request, and the last-committed audit row ('Enabled...', actor b, timestamp .946322) matches the final state; C's earlier-committed 'Disabled' row (.941848) accurately reflects that C's own transaction really did (transiently) set it false before B's commit. Race D (B changes role viewer->analyst, C disables, different columns, concurrent): final DB state role=analyst AND is_active=false -- both concurrent changes to different columns on the same row were correctly merged, neither was lost. | See the 4-race script output captured in-session; independently re-verified via `docker exec app-postgres-1 psql -U ioc -d ioc_intel -c "SELECT timestamp, action, actor_email, detail FROM config_audit_log WHERE detail ILIKE '%qa-ent-multiadmin-x%' ORDER BY timestamp;"`, which matches the script's own readback exactly (8 rows, ordering and content as reported). |

### 4. Last-administrator protection (demote/disable guard on the LAST active admin)

**Summary:**

Read the actual guard implementation in backend/app/core/users.py (update_user's role-demotion path and set_user_active) and its wiring in backend/app/api/routes/admin.py. Ran the codebase's existing pure-logic unit tests (app/tests/unit/test_users_service.py, 4/4 pass, no DB writes) as live evidence for the counting helper's boundary conditions. Live-drove the real HTTP API (PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/{id} and POST /api/v1/admin/users/{id}/active) inside app-backend-1 using two disposable qa-ent-* ADMIN personas created via the same create_user() service call the admin console uses, and a JWT minted in-memory via create_access_token() (never printed) for the acting persona -- confirming end-to-end that the guard correctly does NOT fire when real global admins remain (both calls returned 200), i.e. it doesn't over-block. Confirmed 'user:manage' is ADMIN-only in ROLE_PERMISSIONS (defense in depth). Did NOT trigger the actual LastAdminError condition live, and did NOT touch kokos@gmail.com / final-rtp-admin@example.com (verified untouched, both still ADMIN/active after the test). Both disposable QA users and the temporary probe script were fully cleaned up (verified 0 matching rows in DB afterward).

**Findings (2):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| info | Code-review verdict: last-admin guard logic is correct by inspection (role demotion + disable, race-safe) | backend/app/core/users.py implements the guard in two call sites. In update_user() (lines 203-213): when role != user.role and user.role == Role.ADMIN and role != Role.ADMIN and user.is_active, it calls _remaining_active_admins(locked_admins, excluding=user.id) and raises LastAdminError if 0. In set_user_active() (lines 236-238): when not is_active and user.role == Role.ADMIN and user.is_active, same check before disabling. Both are preceded by _lock_all_admin_rows(db) (lines 73-78), which takes `SELECT ... WHERE role = ADMIN FOR UPDATE` on every ADMIN-role row up front (not just the target row), before counting. This is what makes the two-concurrent-disables race safe: two transactions each targeting a DIFFERENT one of the last two admins will both try to lock the *same overlapping ADMIN row set*, so Postgres serializes them -- the second transaction blocks until the first commits, then re-evaluates and correctly sees 0 remaining. A bare per-target row lock would not catch this (each transaction would see the other admin still active and both would succeed, leaving zero admins). The _remaining_active_admins() helper (lines 81-82) correctly excludes the target by id and counts only rows with is_active=True. Its 4 boundary-condition unit tests (excludes target / zero-when-only-active-one / counts-only-active-rows / empty-list) were run live in this session and all pass. The route layer (api/routes/admin.py lines 65-88) correctly maps LastAdminError to HTTP 409 for both the PATCH role and POST active endpoints, and require_permission('user:manage') gates both to ADMIN-only per ROLE_PERMISSIONS in models/user.py. SelfRoleChangeError is checked before the last-admin check in update_user, which does not weaken the guard (self-demotion is blocked unconditionally regardless of admin count). By inspection this logic is correct and matches its own docstring's race-safety claim. | backend/app/core/users.py lines 73-82 (_lock_all_admin_rows, _remaining_active_admins), 203-213 (update_user role-demotion guard), 236-238 (set_user_active guard); backend/app/api/routes/admin.py lines 65-88 (409 mapping, require_permission('user:manage')); backend/app/models/user.py lines 42-48 (ROLE_PERMISSIONS -- user:manage is ADMIN-only). Live run: `docker exec app-backend-1 python -m pytest app/tests/unit/test_users_service.py -v` -> 4 passed, 0 failed. | Not applicable -- this is a static code-review finding, informational. |
| not_tested | Live trigger of LastAdminError itself -- NOT TESTED -- ENVIRONMENT LIMITATION | The guard's count is deliberately global (every ADMIN-role row in the whole users table, not scoped to any subset), which is precisely what makes it correct -- but it also means the actual 'last admin' boundary (_remaining_active_admins(...) == 0) cannot be forced to fire in this shared environment without first driving the count of active ADMIN rows platform-wide down to exactly the one disposable target, i.e. temporarily disabling every real admin account (there were 7 active ADMIN rows at test time, including kokos@gmail.com and final-rtp-admin@example.com). The task's own constraints explicitly prohibit disabling the real admins, so this specific branch (the actual raise LastAdminError line, and the true 409 response) was not exercised live. What WAS exercised live: the same guarded HTTP endpoints, same permission path, same audit logging, and same service functions, but with the count intentionally left non-zero (7 active admins), which correctly produced the non-blocked path (two 200 responses) -- this confirms the guard doesn't over-fire and that the plumbing (JWT auth, RBAC, service call, response serialization) works end-to-end, but it is not equivalent to observing the 409 rejection itself. Note for completeness: the dev tree already contains an integration-test fixture, app/tests/integration/test_admin_users.py's `_isolated_admin_set`, that does force this exact boundary by temporarily setting is_active=False on every pre-existing active admin inside a try/finally that restores each one's exact prior value even on failure -- and test_cannot_demote_the_last_active_admin / test_cannot_disable_the_last_active_admin / test_concurrent_disable_of_both_last_admins_only_one_succeeds all exercise the real 409/LastAdminError path against Postgres this way. This is a legitimate pattern for an isolated test database, but was deliberately NOT invoked in this session because it requires transiently disabling kokos@gmail.com and final-rtp-admin@example.com, which the task explicitly instructed must not be done in this shared, live environment. Verified after the session that both real admin accounts remain role=ADMIN, is_active=true, untouched. | docker exec app-postgres-1 psql query at test time: 7 active ADMIN rows platform-wide. Post-test verification query: kokos@gmail.com and final-rtp-admin@example.com both role=ADMIN, is_active=t (unchanged). Existing fixture reference: backend/app/tests/integration/test_admin_users.py lines 74-100 (_isolated_admin_set) and lines 148-229 (the three tests that use it to force and verify the real 409 path, including the concurrency race test). | Would require: (1) recording exact is_active for every currently-active ADMIN row, (2) setting all to is_active=false inside a try/finally guaranteeing restoration, (3) creating one disposable ADMIN, (4) attempting PATCH role=analyst and POST active=false on it and confirming HTTP 409 with a LastAdminError-derived detail message, (5) restoring every real admin's original is_active in the finally block regardless of outcome. Not run here per explicit task instruction not to disable real admin accounts, even transiently. |

### 5. General concurrency

**Summary:**

Fired real concurrent HTTP requests (asyncio.gather, real network calls to http://localhost:8000 from inside app-backend-1, not sequential/mocked) against the live single-uvicorn-process backend for three scenarios: (1) two admins (qa-ent-admin-a/b) PATCHing the same disposable target user simultaneously -- both a pure full_name race and a role-conflict race; (2) five disposable personas (qa-ent-analyst-a/b, admin-a/b/c) each POSTing /lookup/stream for a distinct benign public IP (1.1.1.1, 9.9.9.9, 8.8.8.8, 8.8.4.4, 208.67.222.222) at the same instant, consuming the full real SSE pipeline (real providers, real AI backend); (3) two admins concurrently PATCHing the disposable/unconfigured 'gemini' AI-provider credential slot with conflicting values, then racing an active-backend switch (gemini vs bedrock). All test users/lookups/credentials were disposable (qa-ent-*@qa.test personas already seeded from a prior session, reused per instructions) and cleaned up afterward (race-target user hard-deleted with FK-safe order; gemini credentials wiped and active AI backend restored to the pre-test baseline 'ollama'; the 5 test IOC lookups and their dependent provider_results/ai_summaries/correlation_edges/final_assessment_records deleted). Notably, this shared live backend had at least one other concurrently-running QA test process (qa-ent-chaos-* persona) hammering the same /runtime/ai-active endpoint ~387 times during my test window, which is disclosed below as a confound on the latency numbers.

**Findings (3):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| info | Concurrent same-user admin edits: no lost updates, no corruption, correctly serialized | Two admins (admin-a, admin-b) fired truly concurrent PATCH /admin/users/{id} at a shared disposable target: (a) both changed full_name to different values in the same instant, (b) both changed role to different values ('viewer' vs 'admin') in the same instant. Both requests in every pair returned HTTP 200, the final DB state was always exactly one of the two written values (never a merged/corrupted state), and all 4 edits produced 4 distinct config_audit_log rows (no lost audit entries). This confirms app/core/users.py's update_user() with_for_update() lock on the admin-rows set (plus the target row) correctly serializes concurrent writers rather than racing. No defect found. | PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/{target_id} fired via asyncio.gather from admin-a (full_name='Edited-By-A') and admin-b (full_name='Edited-By-B') both returned 200; DB row.full_name ended as exactly 'Edited-By-A'. Follow-up role race (viewer vs admin) both returned 200; DB row.role ended as exactly 'viewer'. config_audit_log showed 4 distinct 'user.update' rows for the target, one per PATCH, none dropped. | Mint ADMIN JWTs for two personas via create_access_token(); create a disposable target user; fire two concurrent httpx PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/{id} calls via asyncio.gather with different full_name (then different role) payloads; assert both 200 and that config_audit_log has one row per call. |
| info | Concurrent AI-provider credential/activation writes on a disposable slot ('gemini'/'bedrock'): correctly serialized, no split-brain state | Two admins concurrently POSTed conflicting credentials to the same unconfigured 'gemini' provider slot (api_key A vs B), and then concurrently activated 'gemini' (admin-a) vs 'bedrock' (admin-b) as the platform's active AI backend. Both requests in each pair returned 200. After the credential race, the row held exactly one of the two values (masked as '************AAAA' or '************BBBB', not a merge of both). After the activation race, querying provider_runtime_configs showed exactly one AI-backend row with is_active=true at all times -- never zero, never two -- confirming app/core/runtime_config.py's with_for_update()-protected upsert (for credentials) and the full-table-rewrite pattern in set_active_ai_backend() correctly avoid split-brain state under concurrency. This held even though a separate, unrelated QA process was simultaneously hammering the same /runtime/ai-active endpoint ~387 times/2min during the test window (persona qa-ent-chaos-6e19d35b@qa.test, not part of this test) -- the single-active-backend invariant never broke under that combined load either. No defect found. | POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers/gemini from admin-a (api_key=qa-test-key-AAAA) and admin-b (api_key=qa-test-key-BBBB) via asyncio.gather both returned 200; response masked_credentials showed '************AAAA' and '************BBBB' respectively (never a mixed value). Concurrent POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-active with backend='gemini' (admin-a) and backend='bedrock' (admin-b) both returned 200; SELECT provider_id FROM provider_runtime_configs WHERE kind='AI' AND is_active=true immediately after returned exactly one row ('gemini'). | Mint two ADMIN JWTs; asyncio.gather two POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers/gemini calls with different credentials; asyncio.gather two POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-active calls with different backends; query provider_runtime_configs to confirm exactly one active row and a non-mixed credential value. |
| P3 | Concurrent IOC investigations complete correctly (no cross-contamination) but degrade sharply in latency -- 3 of 5 simultaneous pipelines took 87-160s vs. the ~20-40s single-run baseline documented in the test suite | Five disposable users (2 analysts, 3 admins) each submitted POST /lookup/stream for a distinct benign public IP at the same instant. Correctness held perfectly: all 5 reached final status COMPLETED, each lookup_id's persisted ioc_value and requested_by matched exactly the submitting user's own IP/id (zero cross-user data bleed), and no duplicate lookup_ids were created. However, completion latency was 100.1s, 87.0s, 89.4s, 125.6s, and 160.6s respectively -- 3-8x the ~20-40s baseline the codebase's own integration-test docstring (test_security_assessment_api.py) describes for a single unstubbed run. An initial run with a 120s client timeout actually cut off 3 of the 5 requests before they finished (server correctly marked them FAILED via the documented GeneratorExit->mark-failed path, itself evidence the disconnect-handling code works correctly), and only completed once the timeout was raised to 300s. The backend runs as a single uvicorn process (no --workers), and the platform's active AI backend during this run was 'ollama' (a local model), which is a plausible single-instance-inference bottleneck for the per-provider summarize_provider + final generate_final_assessment AI calls that every concurrent investigation funnels through -- no application-level lock/semaphore was found gating this (grepped app/ai/ and app/providers/orchestrator.py), so the degradation looks like genuine resource contention rather than an app bug. CONFOUND TO DISCLOSE: a separate, unrelated QA test process (persona qa-ent-chaos-*) was simultaneously flipping the active AI backend ~387 times over roughly the same 2-minute window on this shared live instance, which may have added extra AI-backend churn/latency on top of my own 5-way load; I cannot cleanly separate how much of the slowdown is from my 5 concurrent requests alone versus this sibling load. Recommend re-running this specific latency measurement in an isolated window with no other test traffic before treating the exact multiplier as authoritative -- the *fact* of significant degradation under concurrent load and the *absence* of any data-integrity fault are both solid findings from this run. | ioc_lookups rows for this run: 1.1.1.1/qa-ent-analyst-a 100.1s COMPLETED, 9.9.9.9/qa-ent-analyst-b 87.0s COMPLETED, 8.8.8.8/qa-ent-admin-a 89.4s COMPLETED, 8.8.4.4/qa-ent-admin-b 125.6s COMPLETED, 208.67.222.222/qa-ent-admin-c 160.6s COMPLETED -- all with ioc_value and requested_by matching the submitting persona exactly. config_audit_log showed 387 ai_provider.activate rows from actor 92647808-050f-44da-bf12-82340cb6ba61 (qa-ent-chaos-6e19d35b@qa.test) interleaved throughout the same window at ~270ms intervals. | Mint 5 JWTs (2 ANALYST, 3 ADMIN) for existing qa-ent-analyst-a/b and qa-ent-admin-a/b/c personas; asyncio.gather 5 POST /api/v1/lookup/stream calls each with a distinct benign public IP body {'value': ip}, consuming the SSE stream to its 'done' event; time each; cross-check ioc_lookups.ioc_value/requested_by against the submitting persona per lookup_id. |

### 6. Data isolation between users (basket / case / lookup ownership scoping)

**Summary:**

Using disposable personas qa-ent-analyst-a@qa.test and qa-ent-analyst-b@qa.test (both role=analyst, already present in the DB), minted in-memory JWTs via app.auth.security.create_access_token inside app-backend-1 (no passwords touched, no tokens printed) and drove the live API at localhost:8000. As analyst A: created a basket item (POST /basket, 201), a case (POST /cases, 201), and a lookup/investigation (POST /lookup/stream, captured lookup_id from the SSE 'detected' event). As analyst B: attempted to read/delete A's basket item, read/PATCH/add-a-note to A's case, read A's lookup, and probed random-UUID enumeration and unauthenticated access. Cross-checked every result against docs/API_DOCUMENTATION.md and the actual route source (backend/app/api/routes/basket.py, cases.py, lookup.py) inside the running container. All test data was cleaned up (basket item hard-deleted by its owner A; test case closed and re-titled since no case-delete endpoint exists; the test lookup has no delete endpoint and references TEST-NET-2 documentation IPs 198.51.100.77/198.51.100.200, so it was left in place as harmless).

**Findings (5):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| info | Basket isolation: enforced exactly as documented (no defect) | docs/API_DOCUMENTATION.md §10 states the basket is 'per-analyst private scratch space (scoped to BasketItem.owner_id == user.id)'. Code confirms every basket route (list/add/delete/clear) filters by `BasketItem.owner_id == user.id`. Live test: B's `GET /basket` returned `200 []` and did not contain A's item; B's `DELETE /basket/{A's item_id}` returned `404 {"detail":"Basket item not found"}` -- identical to the response for a random non-existent UUID, so there is no oracle distinguishing 'exists but not yours' from 'does not exist'. There is also no `GET /basket/{id}` route at all (only list, add, delete-by-id, clear, compare) -- attempting one returns `405 Method Not Allowed` for anyone, including the owner -- so 'read a single basket item by ID' is not an operation the API exposes at all, not even to its own owner. | B GET /basket -> 200, body []. B DELETE /basket/{e4e7dc60-e144-49ce-851b-0b4db3c17829} (A's real item) -> 404 {"detail":"Basket item not found"}. B DELETE /basket/{random-uuid} -> identical 404 body. B GET /basket/{item_id} -> 405 Method Not Allowed. | — |
| info | Case isolation: none exists, exactly as documented (no defect) | docs/API_DOCUMENTATION.md §11 and the cases.py module docstring both explicitly state case visibility 'mirrors lookups -- shared across the whole SOC team for anyone with the relevant permission ... not a private-per-analyst construct like the basket.' Code confirms `_load_case()` and every case route (`GET/PATCH /cases/{id}`, `POST /cases/{id}/close`, `POST /cases/{id}/iocs`, `POST /cases/{id}/notes`) query only by `Case.id`, with zero `analyst_id`/owner check -- gated purely by role-based `case:read`/`case:write`/`case:close` permission, which both ANALYST and ADMIN hold. Live test: analyst B, with no relationship to A's case, successfully read it (`GET /cases/{id}` -> 200 full body including A's notes/IOCs/reports), rewrote its title and status (`PATCH /cases/{id}` -> 200, title changed to 'qa-ent-MODIFIED-BY-B', status open->investigating), and added a note to it (`POST /cases/{id}/notes` -> 201) -- all without any authorization error. This is the actual, intentional design per the docs' own claim, not a discovered vulnerability -- but it does mean, concretely, that any analyst can silently take over, retitle, re-triage (change severity/status), or inject notes into any other analyst's case with no per-case ACL, assignment concept, or B-side error to signal the action was cross-analyst. | B GET /cases/{31e7e85c-...} -> 200, full case body (analyst_id = A's UUID, not B's). B PATCH /cases/{same id} {title, status} -> 200, title now 'qa-ent-MODIFIED-BY-B', status 'investigating'. B POST /cases/{same id}/notes -> 201. | — |
| info | Lookup isolation: none exists, exactly as documented (no defect) | The basket docs explicitly call out that lookups, like cases, 'are shared team-wide' unlike the basket. lookup.py's `GET /lookup/{lookup_id}` route (and its sibling `/assessments`, `/export`, `/pivots` routes) gate only on the `lookup:read` permission via `require_permission`, with no `requested_by == user.id` filter anywhere in the query. Live test: B successfully fetched A's in-progress lookup by ID. | B GET /lookup/{294ecdcb-e15b-41a1-9992-34f4d75fbb19} (A's lookup, requested_by = A) -> 200, full lookup body returned to B. | — |
| info | ID enumeration / adjacent-ID guessing: not a viable attack surface (no defect) | All resource IDs observed (basket items, cases, lookups) are server-generated random UUIDv4s, not sequential integers, so 'guess adjacent IDs' has no practical foothold. Randomly generated UUIDs against both `/cases/{id}` and `/basket/{id}` consistently returned clean, generic 404s with no information leakage (same message whether the ID belongs to another user's real resource or doesn't exist at all, for the basket case). | B GET /cases/{random-uuid} -> 404 {"detail":"Case not found"}. B DELETE /basket/{random-uuid} -> 404 {"detail":"Basket item not found"} (same shape as the real-but-not-owned case). | — |
| info | Unauthenticated access blocked on all tested routes (no defect) | Removing the Authorization header entirely on both a case-read and a basket-list request produced a clean 401, confirming no anonymous fallback access to either resource type. | No-auth GET /cases/{id} -> 401 {"detail":"Could not validate credentials"}. No-auth GET /basket -> 401 {"detail":"Could not validate credentials"}. | — |

*Note: finding #1 in this dimension ("Basket isolation: enforced exactly as documented (no defect)") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `failure_scenario`: "N/A -- isolation held. No input caused B to see, delete, or otherwise infer the existence of A's basket item.".*

*Note: finding #2 in this dimension ("Case isolation: none exists, exactly as documented (no defect)") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `failure_scenario`: "Any authenticated analyst account (not just admins) can modify or annotate a case they never created or were assigned to, with the response looking identical to a legitimate same-team edit -- e.g. a compromised or malicious low-privilege analyst account could quietly downgrade a case's severity/status or plant misleading notes on another analyst's active investigation.".*

*Note: finding #3 in this dimension ("Lookup isolation: none exists, exactly as documented (no defect)") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `failure_scenario`: "N/A -- matches documented/intended shared-team model.".*

*Note: finding #4 in this dimension ("ID enumeration / adjacent-ID guessing: not a viable attack surface (no defect)") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `failure_scenario`: "N/A -- tested and found not exploitable in this environment.".*

*Note: finding #5 in this dimension ("Unauthenticated access blocked on all tested routes (no defect)") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `failure_scenario`: "N/A.".*

### 7. Provider credential lifecycle mechanics (AI provider config: save/edit/mask/connection-test/reset, concurrent-write safety)

**Summary:**

Tested the full AI-provider credential lifecycle in app.core.runtime_config / app.ai.connection_test directly inside app-backend-1, using the disposable "gemini" slot (confirmed configured=False beforehand, alongside anthropic and bedrock; the live groq and ollama credentials were never touched). Verified: (1) svc.upsert_ai_provider saves a fake credential and list output only ever exposes mask_secret() output, never the raw value or a substring of it; (2) editing to a second fake value replaces the stored value server-side and updates the mask; (3) app.ai.connection_test.test_ai_connection with an obviously-invalid key makes a real request to Google's Gemini endpoint and correctly returns ok=False with a generic "Authentication failed" message (no key leakage), and svc.record_ai_test_result persists that failure (last_test_ok/last_test_at/last_test_message) without touching the stored credentials; (4) fired 12 concurrent svc.upsert_ai_provider calls at the same "gemini" row, each adding a distinct credential field, and confirmed the with_for_update() row lock in upsert_ai_provider prevented any lost update -- all 12 fields plus the prior api_key survived; (5) reset the slot back to empty/unconfigured and confirmed via both the service layer and a direct read-only Postgres check that gemini is back to its exact baseline (configured=false, masked_credentials={}, model_id unchanged) and that anthropic/bedrock/groq/ollama rows were unaffected. All temporary scripts were deleted from the container and host afterward. No secret values (real or fake) were printed at any point -- only booleans, masked strings, and non-sensitive metadata.

**Findings (2):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| info | No 'clear credential' path exists in upsert_ai_provider by design | runtime_config.py's _merge_credentials() intentionally merges incoming fields onto the existing stored credentials and never removes a key (documented in the code as fixing a prior bug where an empty save wiped a working credential). This is correct behavior for the settings-UI use case, but it means there is no supported API-level way to blank/reset a provider slot back to a truly empty masked_credentials={} state -- QA had to reset the disposable test row directly via the ORM (setting encrypted_credentials='' on the row) rather than through upsert_ai_provider. Not a bug given the documented intent, but worth flagging: if an operator ever needs to genuinely revoke/clear a stored AI credential (e.g. compromised key) rather than replace it with a new one, today's UI/API has no direct affordance for that -- only overwrite-with-a-new-value. | app/core/runtime_config.py _merge_credentials(): 'if not incoming: return _decrypt_credentials(row)' and 'There is no "clear a credential" affordance in the UI today, so this doesn't remove any reachable capability.' Confirmed empirically: calling upsert_ai_provider('gemini', {}, None) leaves prior credentials fully intact; only a direct DB write (row.encrypted_credentials = '') achieved a true reset in this test. | Inside app-backend-1: await upsert_ai_provider('gemini', {'api_key': 'X'}, None); await upsert_ai_provider('gemini', {}, None); list_ai_providers() still shows configured=True with the same masked api_key -- confirms an empty-dict save cannot clear a credential. |
| not_tested | RBAC-gated HTTP surface (POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers/{backend}, POST /api/v1/ai/test) not exercised end-to-end | This dimension was scoped and executed at the service-layer (app.core.runtime_config / app.ai.connection_test), per the task's explicit instruction to use svc.upsert_ai_provider(...) directly. Doing the same lifecycle through the real HTTP endpoints (app/api/routes/runtime.py, app/api/routes/ai_config.py) would additionally require a real DB-backed user row for require_permission('provider:manage') to resolve (get_current_user in app/auth/rbac.py does a live `select(User).where(User.email == ...)` DB lookup and rejects tokens for emails with no matching active user row -- contrary to the task brief's premise that a minted JWT needs no DB lookup). Creating a disposable admin user was judged out of scope for a credential-lifecycle-mechanics test and was not done, so the RBAC/route wiring itself (as opposed to the underlying service functions it calls) is NOT TESTED here. | app/auth/rbac.py get_current_user(): 'result = await db.execute(select(User).where(User.email == payload["sub"])); user = result.scalar_one_or_none(); if not user or not user.is_active: raise credentials_error' -- a minted token for a non-existent qa-ent-*@qa.test email would get 401, not reach the route handler. | NOT TESTED -- ENVIRONMENT/SCOPE LIMITATION. Would require creating a disposable admin-role user row, minting a token for it, then calling POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers/gemini and POST /api/v1/ai/test over HTTP, then cleaning up the user via the FK-safe delete pattern. |

### 8. AI backend chaos (IOC Intelligence Platform)

**Summary:**

Tested against the live installed stack (app-backend-1, ollama active backend = gemma2:2b) using a disposable qa-ent-chaos admin persona minted via create_access_token + create_user (no passwords/secrets ever printed). Ran three real chaos scenarios via the actual /api/v1/lookup/stream SSE endpoint and /api/v1/runtime/ai-* endpoints: (1) an oversized (~2010-char) IOC value fed through a real investigation, (2) ~400 rapid POST /runtime/ai-active calls flipping ollama<->an unconfigured disposable "bedrock" slot while a real investigation was mid-flight, and (3) pointing ollama's base_url at an unreachable port (http://host.docker.internal:19999) mid-run to simulate a total AI-backend outage. All DB/log evidence was captured directly (docker logs, psql). Ollama's base_url/model were restored to the original values afterward (verified byte-for-byte equal via in-process comparison, never printed) and the active backend confirmed back to ollama/gemma2:2b, with a final real investigation showing a genuine non-fallback AI-generated assessment (ai_backend=ollama, ai_model=gemma2:2b, verdict=benign) proving restoration succeeded. All disposable lookups, the qa-ent-chaos user, and the FK rows it touched (config_audit_log.actor_user_id, provider_runtime_configs.updated_by) were cleaned up and verified removed at the end.

**Findings (4):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| P1 | Oversized IOC value crashes the /lookup/stream generator and leaves the investigation permanently stuck in RUNNING status | OTX's provider client builds `source_url=f"https://otx.alienvault.com/indicator/{section}/{ioc_value}"` with no length cap (backend/app/providers/otx.py lines 61/79/93), and this is written verbatim into `provider_results.source_url`, a `VARCHAR(2048)` column. Any ioc_value long enough that the OTX URL exceeds 2048 chars causes Postgres to raise `asyncpg.exceptions.StringDataRightTruncationError` during the per-provider `await stream_db.commit()` in `stream_lookup()`'s event generator (backend/app/api/routes/lookup.py, ~line 150). That first exception IS caught by the generator's `except Exception as exc:` block, which sets `stream_lookup_row.status = LookupStatus.FAILED` in memory and then calls `await stream_db.commit()` a second time on the SAME session (~line 264) -- but because the prior flush failure already rolled the session back internally, this second commit raises an uncaught `sqlalchemy.exc.PendingRollbackError` (SQLAlchemy requires an explicit `.rollback()` first). That second exception is never caught, so it escapes the async generator entirely, killing the SSE stream mid-response (the client sees a raw dropped connection, not a clean `error` SSE event). The `finally` block's own documented safety net (`if stream_lookup_row.status == LookupStatus.RUNNING: mark failed via a fresh session`) is a no-op here because the in-memory `.status` attribute had already been (uncommitted) set to FAILED one step earlier -- so the safety net believes recovery already happened, when in fact nothing was ever persisted. Net result, confirmed directly against Postgres several minutes later: the `ioc_lookups` row is permanently stuck at `status='running'` and will never transition to completed or failed. This is exactly the 'corrupted lookup left permanently stuck in a running state' failure mode this test dimension was designed to catch, and it is triggerable by any user with the ordinary `lookup:create` permission (every analyst) simply by pasting an unusually long URL/value into the normal search box -- no admin action or AI-backend manipulation needed. | Repro request: POST /api/v1/lookup/stream {"value": "https://example.com/" + "A"*1990} (2010-char URL) as an authenticated user with lookup:create. Client-side: httpx.RemoteProtocolError: peer closed connection without sending complete message body (incomplete chunked read). Server logs (docker logs app-backend-1): `sqlalchemy.exc.DBAPIError: ... StringDataRightTruncationError: value too long for type character varying(2048)` on the provider_results INSERT for provider_id='otx', immediately followed by an unhandled ExceptionGroup ending in `sqlalchemy.exc.PendingRollbackError: This Session's transaction has been rolled back due to a previous exception during flush...`. DB confirmation: `SELECT id, status FROM ioc_lookups WHERE id='f81daa89-76a3-470a-a96c-4730bcc7f0be'` returned `status='running'` (queried well after the stream had terminated and the HTTP connection was closed). Row and its FK dependents were removed during my own test-data cleanup at the end of this session. | 1) Auth as any user with lookup:create. 2) POST /api/v1/lookup/stream with value = 'https://example.com/' + 'A'*1990 (or any URL whose length pushes 'https://otx.alienvault.com/indicator/url/' + value past 2048 chars, with the otx IOC provider enabled/configured). 3) Observe the SSE stream die abruptly after the otx provider_result event (no error/done event ever arrives). 4) Query the lookup's row in ioc_lookups by the id from the earlier 'detected' event -- status remains 'running' indefinitely. |
| P2 | Final-assessment generation can exhaust its one retry and fall back to 'unavailable' on a fully healthy AI backend, indistinguishable from a real outage | generate_final_assessment() (app/ai/service.py) retries exactly once, and only for pydantic ValidationError (self-contradictory verdict/risk pairs from the model). Live against IOC '1.0.0.1' with a fully healthy, correctly-restored ollama/gemma2:2b backend and real mixed-signal evidence (OTX flagged it malicious/associated with malware families while Spamhaus/WHOIS showed it clean), gemma2:2b produced two DIFFERENT self-contradictory outputs across its two attempts: attempt 1 gave final_verdict='benign' with risk.malicious_probability=90.0 (rejected by the model's own consistency validator), attempt 2 gave final_verdict='malicious' with risk.malicious_probability=1.0 (also rejected). Both attempts exhausted, so the pipeline correctly avoided fabricating a verdict and fell back to the generic 'AI-generated assessment unavailable (generation error)' text -- even though all 4 per-provider AI summaries for the same lookup had generated successfully moments earlier. This is not data corruption (the anti-hallucination validator worked exactly as designed) and the lookup still completed cleanly, but from the API/UI's perspective this failure mode renders identically to a total AI-backend outage (same generic exec_summary string), even though the root cause and remediation are completely different (transient small-model sampling flakiness on mixed-signal evidence vs. genuine infra/config failure). A second, immediate reproduction attempt on a low-signal domain succeeded on the first try, confirming this is stochastic model flakiness rather than a deterministic bug. | docker logs app-backend-1 (~10:5x UTC 2026-08-15): 'Final assessment attempt 1 failed for 1.0.0.1: 1 validation error for FinalAssessment ... final_verdict="benign" contradicts risk.malicious_probability=90.0 ... -- retrying once' followed immediately by 'Final assessment generation failed for 1.0.0.1: 1 validation error for FinalAssessment ... final_verdict="malicious" contradicts risk.malicious_probability=1.0 ...'. Corresponding SSE final_assessment event returned ai_backend=None, final_verdict='unknown', executive_summary='AI-generated assessment unavailable (generation error). See individual provider results and summaries below for raw findings.' despite 4 real per-provider summaries (spamhaus/whois_rdap/otx/internet_intelligence) having already been generated successfully in the same run. | 1) With ollama/gemma2:2b active and healthy, POST /api/v1/lookup/stream {"value": "1.0.0.1"} (or any IOC likely to produce genuinely conflicting provider signals, e.g. an IP OTX flags maliciously-associated but Spamhaus/WHOIS show clean). 2) Inspect server logs for 'Final assessment attempt 1 failed' / 'Final assessment generation failed' pydantic ValidationError messages, and confirm the SSE final_assessment event's executive_summary is the generic 'unavailable' fallback rather than a real assessment, even though no AI backend was ever actually down. |
| info | Active-AI-backend switching under chaos degrades gracefully and does not corrupt data (positive confirmation), but backend attribution within one investigation is non-atomic | Confirmed live, not a defect: issuing ~400 rapid-fire POST /runtime/ai-active calls (alternating ollama <-> an unconfigured disposable 'bedrock' slot every 0.25s) throughout a single ~127s investigation produced zero HTTP errors on the switch endpoint itself (no IntegrityError/deadlock from concurrent writes to the same provider_runtime_configs rows) and the investigation still reached status=COMPLETED. Because app/ai/service.py's _get_ai_client() does a fresh DB read of the active backend on every single AI call (by design, to support 'switch AI, no restart'), a single investigation's per-provider summaries can land on different backend states than its own final assessment: in this run, 2 of 3 AI-summarized providers landed on the healthy ollama backend and got real summaries, while 1 (whois_rdap) and the final assessment call both happened to land on the disposable unconfigured 'bedrock' slot at that instant and degraded cleanly to the documented 'AI backend failed to generate a summary...' / 'AI-generated assessment unavailable...' fallback text -- no crash, no hang, no fabricated content, no stuck lookup. Simulating a total outage (ollama base_url pointed at an unreachable port) behaved identically: fast ConnectError-based failures (~18s total, no long timeout/hang, no retry loop since the retry logic only fires for ValidationError), clean per-call fallback text, lookup still reached COMPLETED. The only minor observability gap: the persisted FinalAssessmentRecord.ai_backend/ai_model only capture which backend served the FINAL call, not which backend(s) served each per-provider summary, so an investigation that is a mix of real and degraded content has no field indicating that mix beyond reading each summary's caveats text. | Rapid-switch run: lookup 46d1ada2-... completed with status=COMPLETED; ai_summaries.caveats for whois_rdap = 'The AI backend failed to generate a summary for this provider's data (see server logs for details).' while spamhaus/internet_intelligence for the same lookup have empty caveats (real summaries); final_assessment executive_summary was the generic unavailable fallback; 400 config_audit_log 'ai_provider.activate' rows recorded in the ~5-minute test window with zero non-200 responses among the 397 switch calls made during the stream itself. Outage-simulation run: elapsed 18.1s total, both provider summaries and the final assessment degraded to the documented fallback text, lookup still reached COMPLETED (confirmed via DB). | See Test 2 and Test 3 methodology described in the summary; no further action needed -- this documents expected, correctly-handled behavior under the requested chaos conditions. |
| P3 | Stale 'last test: failed' status can persist on the AI Providers panel for a backend that is actually healthy and actively serving investigations | At the start of this session, before any action of mine, the 'ollama' row in provider_runtime_configs already showed last_test_ok=false / last_test_message='Ollama base URL and model are both required.' (timestamped 2026-08-15 04:53 UTC, actor 'claude-code-test' in config_audit_log -- not an @qa.test disposable identity, suggesting a prior automated session already exercised this exact base_url-outage scenario and restored the credentials but never re-ran a successful /api/v1/ai/test to refresh the recorded test status). I independently confirmed during this session that ollama/gemma2:2b was, in fact, fully functional at that time (real per-provider AI summaries were generated correctly). Because upsert_ai_provider() (used both to break and to restore the base_url) never calls record_ai_test_result(), reconfiguring credentials back to a working value does not clear or refresh the previous failure recorded on the Manage AI Providers panel. An admin looking at that panel after a transient outage-and-recovery cycle (exactly the scenario this dimension simulates) would see a persistent 'last tested: failed' badge on the currently-active, currently-working backend until someone manually clicks 'Test connection' again. | GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers (admin token) at session start returned for provider_id='ollama': is_active=true, configured=true, model_id='gemma2:2b', last_test_ok=false, last_test_message='Ollama base URL and model are both required.', last_test_at='2026-08-12T14:29:39' (3 days stale) while updated_at='2026-08-15T04:53:03' (same-day, from a configure call) -- i.e. the credentials were touched/restored same-day but the test result was never refreshed. I did not attempt to fix this pre-existing state since it predates my session; noting it as inherited/NOT something I introduced or resolved. | GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers as a provider:manage user and inspect the 'ollama' entry's last_test_ok/last_test_message/last_test_at vs. updated_at/configured/is_active fields for this same staleness pattern. |

### 9. IOC provider chaos (backend/app/providers registry + orchestrator resilience, malformed-input handling)

**Summary:**

Read backend/app/providers/registry.py, orchestrator.py, and base.py to understand the fan-out/timeout/retry architecture, then exercised it live against the running Docker stack. Created one disposable analyst persona (qa-ent-chaos-125bba29@qa.test, minted a JWT in-memory via app.auth.security.create_access_token, never printed) and used it to call GET /api/v1/providers/health and run a real POST /api/v1/lookup/stream investigation against 8.8.8.8, observing every provider's live status. Cross-checked live results against read-only Postgres queries of provider_results/ioc_lookups for historical corroboration. Ran 4 malformed-value investigations (empty string, 50,000-char string, emoji/unicode, SQL-metacharacter string "1' OR '1'='1") through the real endpoint. Cleaned up my own test lookup (deleting final_assessment_records first, since it has no FK cascade from ioc_lookups) and test user; verified both are gone.

**Findings (6):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| info | Confirmed live: single-provider failures degrade gracefully, whole investigation still completes | Ran POST /api/v1/lookup/stream for 8.8.8.8 as the qa-ent-chaos analyst persona. Live provider_result statuses observed over SSE: virustotal=error (real HTTP 401 Unauthorized from https://www.virustotal.com/api/v3/ip_addresses/8.8.8.8, live right now), abuseipdb=error (real HTTP 401 from https://api.abuseipdb.com/api/v2/check...), censys/threatfox/urlhaus=not_configured, otx=no_data (call succeeded, no data), internet_intelligence/spamhaus/whois_rdap=ok. The stream still reached the 'done' SSE event and the DB row (id a617f345-dfd5-4624-b29d-c29ee0a5b6d8, now deleted) ended with status=COMPLETED, final_verdict=BENIGN, risk_score=50, confidence_score=80 -- i.e. 2 real, currently-broken provider credentials did not sink the investigation. Code-level cause confirmed in app/providers/base.py's BaseProvider.run() (every fetch() wrapped in try/except httpx.HTTPStatusError -> RATE_LIMITED/ERROR, plus a catch-all Exception -> ERROR, both normalized to a ProviderResult rather than raised) and app/providers/orchestrator.py's run_all_providers() (each provider is an independent asyncio task; asyncio.as_completed yields each with its own try/except so one task's exception can't cancel or block the others). | SSE events from the live run (provider_id, status, error_message): ["virustotal","error","HTTP 401: ... virustotal.com/api/v3/ip_addresses/8.8.8.8"], ["abuseipdb","error","HTTP 401: ... api.abuseipdb.com/api/v2/check?ipAddress=8.8.8.8..."], final SSE event ("done", {"lookup_id":"a617f345-..."}); DB row before cleanup: status=COMPLETED, final_verdict=BENIGN, risk_score=50, confidence_score=80. Historical corroboration via read-only query: `SELECT l.status, pr.provider_id, pr.status, count(*) FROM provider_results pr JOIN ioc_lookups l ON l.id=pr.lookup_id WHERE pr.status IN ('error','rate_limited','timeout') GROUP BY 1,2,3` shows 11 abuseipdb 'error', 8 censys 'rate_limited', 9 virustotal 'error' all inside lookups whose overall status is COMPLETED. | Mint an analyst JWT via app.auth.security.create_access_token for a real DB user; POST {"value":"8.8.8.8"} to /api/v1/lookup/stream with that bearer token; read the SSE stream to 'done'; GET the lookup row and observe status=COMPLETED despite 2 provider_result rows with status=error. |
| info | Malformed IOC values are rejected with clean 422s before touching the DB or any provider | detect_ioc_type() (app/ioc/detector.py) always returns a value (defaulting to UNKNOWN, never raises), and stream_lookup() (app/api/routes/lookup.py) checks `if ioc_type == IOCType.UNKNOWN: raise HTTPException(422, ...)` BEFORE creating the IOCLookup DB row or calling run_all_providers -- so a rejected value is never persisted and never reaches any provider's fetch(). Confirmed live for all 4 malformed inputs tried: empty string, a 50,000-character string, an emoji/unicode string, and a SQL-metacharacter string ("1' OR '1'='1"). All 4 returned HTTP 422 with body {"detail":"Could not determine IOC type; pass ioc_type_hint."}, 0 SSE events, and no lookup_id was ever created. The regexes involved (_DOMAIN_RE etc.) use a length-bounded lookahead ((?=.{1,253}$)) so the long-string case fails fast rather than risking ReDoS. | Live responses: empty_string -> {"http_status":422,"body":"{\"detail\":\"Could not determine IOC type; pass ioc_type_hint.\"}"}; extremely_long (50k chars) -> same 422; unicode_emoji -> same 422; sql_metachar ("1' OR '1'='1") -> same 422. All 4 had lookup_id_created=null. Read-only DB check after the run showed no ioc_lookups row for any of these values. | POST {"value":""} (or a 50000-char string, or "\U0001F480\U0001F525", or "1' OR '1'='1") to /api/v1/lookup/stream with a valid analyst bearer token; observe HTTP 422 with detail 'Could not determine IOC type; pass ioc_type_hint.' and zero SSE events. |
| P3 | GET /api/v1/providers/health's `configured` field is stale/wrong for several providers vs. the real runtime-DB-backed state actually used by investigations | registry.py's get_provider_health() reports `configured` straight from each provider singleton's class-level `.configured` attribute, which for most providers is set once at process import time from app.core.config.Settings (itself sourced from the container's .env-derived env vars). Separately, app/core/runtime_config.py maintains a DB-backed provider_runtime_configs table (encrypted credentials, seeded from the same env vars but editable at runtime) which is what app/providers/orchestrator.py's get_ioc_provider_snapshot() + app/core/runtime_context.py's per-request override actually use to decide whether a provider's fetch() is attempted. These two notions of 'configured' have diverged live: GET /providers/health reports configured=false for virustotal, abuseipdb, and otx, yet the live investigation above actually dispatched real outbound calls to all three (virustotal/abuseipdb got real 401s from the live third-party APIs; otx succeeded with no_data) -- proving effective_configured was true for those three at request time. Conversely /providers/health reports censys as configured=false and the runtime DB genuinely didn't attempt it either (matches), but note ABUSECH_AUTH_KEY, CENSYS_PERSONAL_ACCESS_TOKEN and CENSYS_ORGANIZATION_ID are all present as container env vars yet threatfox/urlhaus/malwarebazaar/censys all show not_configured in both the static and runtime views -- the .env-to-runtime-DB seed step (app/core/runtime_config.py's _ENV_SEED_MAP) appears to not have populated/activated those.
