### 19. Observability review — IOC Intelligence Platform backend (app-backend-1)

**Summary:**

Triggered three real operations against the live stack and inspected `docker logs app-backend-1` immediately after each: (1) a failed login (`POST /api/v1/auth/login` with a nonexistent disposable email), (2) a full IOC lookup (`POST /api/v1/lookup/stream` for 127.0.0.1, run as a disposable QA analyst persona minted via `create_access_token` + a real DB user created with `app.core.users.create_user`), and (3) a Security Assessment Toolkit run (nmap "quick" profile against 127.0.0.1, tied to the lookup above, which completed with 1 finding). For each, checked whether WHAT/WHICH-component/STATUS/WHEN were recoverable from stdout logs alone, and whether any request-correlation ID ties related log lines together. Cross-referenced against source (`app/main.py`, `app/auth/rbac.py`, `app/api/routes/*.py`, `app/core/security_assessment.py`, `app/security_assessment/*.py`, `app/core/audit.py`) and against the DB-side `config_audit_log` table (via read-only psql queries) to see what exists there but never reaches the log stream. Disposable test data (QA analyst user, its lookup + cascaded records, the security-assessment run, and the audit-log actor references) was cleaned up afterward and verified deleted (0 rows). No real accounts, secrets, or third-party hosts were touched; the only active-scan target used was 127.0.0.1.

**Findings (6):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| P1 | No request-correlation ID anywhere in the backend — concurrent operations are unattributable from logs alone | A repo-wide search (`grep -rn 'request_id\\|correlation_id\\|X-Request-Id\\|X-Correlation'` over `app/`) returns zero matches outside this review's own throwaway scripts. `app/main.py` registers only `CORSMiddleware` and the Prometheus `Instrumentator` — there is no request-ID middleware, no `contextvars`-based logging context, and no OpenTelemetry/Sentry/Jaeger integration anywhere in the codebase (checked via grep for those terms too). Every log line that matters (uvicorn's access log, httpx's per-outbound-call log, provider-specific warnings) is emitted independently with nothing but a source `ip:port` in common, and that source port is an ephemeral TCP detail, not a stable per-request identifier reused across the several log lines one request produces. | During my single lookup for 127.0.0.1 (lookup_id `28943dda-20ee-44fb-848a-d6b978bb1559`), the live system had substantial *other* concurrent lookup traffic in flight from other test activity. `docker logs -t app-backend-1` in the same window shows interleaved `POST /api/v1/lookup/stream` 200s at 10:41:52 (x5), 10:42:15, 10:42:49, 10:43:04, 10:44:49, 10:45:00, 10:45:32, 10:45:36, 10:45:41, 10:45:49, 10:45:50, and mine at 10:45:35.928 — each logged identically as `INFO: 127.0.0.1:PORT - "POST /api/v1/lookup/stream HTTP/1.1" 200 OK` with no lookup_id, no ioc_value, no user, and no shared token. The httpx provider-fanout lines below each of these (e.g. `INFO:httpx:HTTP Request: GET https://otx.alienvault.com/... 200 OK`, `GET https://www.virustotal.com/...` `401 Unauthorized`) likewise carry no identifier back to which of the ~12 concurrent lookups spawned them — the only way I could tell my own httpx lines apart from a concurrent run's was by recognizing the literal ioc_value string in the URL (e.g. spotting `1.0.0.1` or `qa-ent-chaos-ai-down.example.com` belonging to someone else's concurrent test, not mine). | 1. Have two or more concurrent callers each run `POST /api/v1/lookup/stream` for different IOC values at roughly the same time. 2. Run `docker logs -t app-backend-1 --tail 200` immediately after. 3. Attempt to determine, using log content alone (no DB access), which `httpx:HTTP Request` lines belong to which of the concurrent lookups — there is no field that lets you group them. |
| P1 | Failed login attempts are invisible in stdout logs beyond a bare status code — the attempted email/username never reaches the log stream | `app/api/routes/auth.py`'s `login()` has zero `logger.*` calls. On failure it calls `app.core.users.record_login_failure(payload.email)`, which only writes a row to the Postgres `config_audit_log` table via `app/core/audit.py`'s `record_audit()` — it never touches Python's `logging` module. The only trace of a failed login in `docker logs` is uvicorn's generic access-log line for the request, which carries no identity information at all. | After POSTing `{"email": "qa-ent-obs-nope@example.com", "password": "definitely-wrong-pw"}` to `/api/v1/auth/login` (got back `401 {"detail":"Invalid email or password"}` as expected), the *only* corresponding line in `docker logs -t app-backend-1 --tail 60` was:<br>`2026-08-15T10:43:30.720060209Z INFO:     127.0.0.1:42430 - "POST /api/v1/auth/login HTTP/1.1" 401 Unauthorized`<br>That line tells you WHAT (login attempt), STATUS (401) and WHEN (docker's own append timestamp), but nothing about WHO/WHICH account was targeted. The actual attempted email is recoverable ONLY via a direct Postgres query: `SELECT timestamp, action, actor_email, detail FROM config_audit_log WHERE action='auth.login_failed'` returned rows like `detail = "Failed login attempt for email 'qa-ent-obs-nope@example.com'."` with `actor_email` itself left NULL (the email is only present inside the free-text `detail` string, not as a queryable column, since `record_login_failure()` calls `record_audit("auth.login_failed", detail)` without passing `actor_email`). A responder tailing `docker logs` during a live credential-stuffing incident would see a wall of identical `401 Unauthorized` lines and have no way to tell which accounts are being targeted without separately knowing to query a specific DB table/column that the app-log stream gives no hint even exists. | 1. `POST /api/v1/auth/login` with a wrong password for any email. 2. `docker logs -t app-backend-1 --tail 60` immediately after — confirm the only line is the bare uvicorn access-log entry with status 401 and no email. 3. Compare against `SELECT * FROM config_audit_log WHERE action='auth.login_failed' ORDER BY timestamp DESC LIMIT 1` in Postgres, which does contain the targeted email (in free text) — showing the real signal exists but never reaches the log stream operators actually watch. |
| P1 | Security Assessment Toolkit runs (including active nmap scans) produce no application log output at all on success — visibility exists only in Postgres, invisible to `docker logs` | None of the Security Assessment Toolkit's tool adapters under `app/security_assessment/*.py` contain a single `logger.*` call (`grep -rn 'logger\.' /app/app/security_assessment/*.py` returns nothing). The service layer `app/core/security_assessment.py` only calls `logger.exception(...)` twice, both exclusively on the *failure* path (run failed, or post-completion AI-refresh failed) — there is no `logger.info` for run start, tool invocation, or successful completion. The only place a successful run's outcome is recorded is a `config_audit_log` DB row written by `record_audit("security_assessment.run_completed", ...)`, which never touches stdout. | I ran a real nmap 'quick' scan against 127.0.0.1 (run_id `aea6503a-fe73-439d-915a-b04f97b98c7f`, tied to lookup `28943dda-...`), which completed successfully with 1 finding. Searching `docker logs -t app-backend-1` for any trace of it: the ONLY related line is the initial HTTP request:<br>`2026-08-15T10:47:19.817967592Z INFO:     127.0.0.1:37876 - "POST /api/v1/security-assessment/28943dda-20ee-44fb-848a-d6b978bb1559/run HTTP/1.1" 200 OK`<br>— which reveals the lookup_id (only because it's in the URL path) but nothing else: not the run_id, not which tool (`nmap`) or profile (`quick`) was selected, not that the scan subsequently ran and completed, not the finding count, not who requested it beyond a bare loopback `ip:port`. There is no follow-up log line at all when the background task finishes (grepped the full log history for `security_assessment`, `nmap`, and the run_id `aea6503a` — no further matches). By contrast, `SELECT timestamp, action, actor_email, detail FROM config_audit_log WHERE actor_email='qa-ent-obs-analyst@example.com'` shows the full picture exists, just not in logs:<br>`10:47:19.811932+00 \| security_assessment.run_requested \| qa-ent-obs-analyst@example.com \| Requested a security assessment (nmap, profile='quick') against '127.0.0.1'.`<br>`10:47:20.021016+00 \| security_assessment.run_completed \| qa-ent-obs-analyst@example.com \| Security assessment run against '127.0.0.1' completed: 1 finding(s) across 1 tool(s).`<br>An incident responder watching `docker logs` in real time would see the run get kicked off (as an anonymous-looking 200 OK) and then nothing — no way to tell from logs whether it ever finished, succeeded, or what it found. They would have to already know to check a specific Postgres table for this subsystem, which is undocumented outside the source code itself. | 1. As an analyst-role user, POST `/api/v1/security-assessment/{lookup_id}/run` with `tool_ids: ["nmap"], profile: "quick", target_confirmation: "<the lookup's ioc_value>", authorization_confirmed: true` against a lookup seeded with ioc_value 127.0.0.1. 2. Poll `GET /api/v1/security-assessment/{lookup_id}/runs` until status is terminal (mine reached `completed` with 1 finding in ~2s). 3. `docker logs -t app-backend-1` — confirm no completion/finding-count line exists anywhere in the log stream, only the initial request line and the run_id is never printed at all in logs (only visible via the API JSON response or DB). |
| P2 | structlog is configured but never used — JSON structured logging exists in main.py config only, all actual log output is unstructured plaintext from stdlib logging | `app/main.py` calls `structlog.configure(processors=[structlog.processors.JSONRenderer()])`, suggesting structured JSON logs are intended. But `grep -rln 'structlog' /app/app` shows `structlog` is imported/configured only in `main.py` itself — no other file in the codebase (`grep -c 'logger = logging.getLogger' → 21 files` all use the stdlib `logging` module directly) ever calls `structlog.get_logger()`. The structlog configuration is dead code; every real log line observed is plain unstructured text via `logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO)` (which also uses Python's default bare format with no timestamp of its own — the only timestamp visible is the one Docker itself stamps on capture via `docker logs -t`, not one the app emits). | `app/main.py` line 26: `logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO)` followed by line 27: `structlog.configure(processors=[structlog.processors.JSONRenderer()])`. Every observed log line across all three test operations was plaintext, e.g. `INFO:httpx:HTTP Request: GET https://api.abuseipdb.com/... "HTTP/1.1 401 Unauthorized"` and `WARNING:app.ai.service:Provider summary generation failed for spamhaus: RuntimeError('Could not reach Ollama at http://host.docker.internal:19999...')` — never JSON, never containing an app-emitted timestamp, level-as-field, or any structured key/value that a log aggregator (e.g. an ELK/Loki pipeline) could parse and filter on. This means any real log-aggregation tooling stood up in front of `docker logs` would have to fall back to fragile plaintext regex parsing rather than structured field queries. | 1. `docker exec app-backend-1 grep -rln structlog /app/app` → only `app/main.py`. 2. `docker logs -t app-backend-1 --tail 50` → confirm every line is stdlib plaintext, not JSON. |
| P2 | High-volume routine polling (`POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-active`) floods the log stream, burying security-relevant single-line events in noise | The frontend appears to poll `POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-active` on a very tight interval (roughly every 250-300ms based on observed timestamps), and each poll produces its own uvicorn access-log line indistinguishable in format/verbosity from a security-relevant event like a failed login. With no severity/category distinction beyond the URL path and no correlation ID, a human or simple `grep`/tail-watching operator has to visually scan through dozens of near-identical routine polling lines to spot the one login-failure or lookup line they care about. | `docker logs -t app-backend-1 --tail 60` captured immediately after the failed-login test contains 40+ lines of `INFO:     127.0.0.1:38070 - "POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-active HTTP/1.1" 200 OK` in a ~15-second window (10:43:27.517 through 10:43:45.377, roughly one every 275ms from the same client:port), with the single `"POST /api/v1/auth/login HTTP/1.1" 401 Unauthorized` line I was looking for sandwiched in the middle at 10:43:30.720 — visually indistinguishable in format/color/level from the noise around it (all `INFO`). | 1. Load/leave the frontend open against the live backend for ~15 seconds. 2. `docker logs -t app-backend-1 --tail 60` — count the `runtime/ai-active` lines vs. any single security-relevant event in the same window. |
| P3 | Prometheus metrics exist and are useful for aggregate trends, but provide no per-incident detail (no correlation with logs, no per-user/IOC labels) | This is a partial mitigant worth noting alongside the findings above, not a standalone defect: `prometheus_fastapi_instrumentator`'s `/metrics` endpoint is live and does track `http_requests_total{handler=...,method=...,status=...}` counters, which would let an operator notice e.g. an elevated 4xx rate on `/api/v1/auth/login` or `/api/v1/lookup/stream` on a dashboard. But these are pure aggregate counters — no exemplar linking a specific metric increment back to a specific log line, trace, user, or request, and no latency histogram was observed being scraped in this quick check either (labels are only method/status/handler). This narrows, but does not close, the gap identified above: it can tell you THAT something anomalous is trending, never WHICH specific requests/users were involved, which is what an incident investigation actually needs. | `curl http://localhost:8000/metrics` returned real counters, e.g.:<br>`http_requests_total{handler="/api/v1/lookup/stream",method="POST",status="4xx"} 8.0`<br>`http_requests_total{handler="/api/v1/auth/login",method="POST",status="2xx"} 5.0`<br>— confirming metrics infrastructure is present and functioning, but with no per-request or per-actor granularity. | `curl http://localhost:8000/metrics \| grep http_requests_total` — observe labels are limited to handler/method/status, nothing more granular. |

### 20. Fresh-eyes documentation validation (docs/QUICKSTART.md, docs/USER_GUIDE.md, docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md only, plus documentation/DOCUMENTATION_SOURCE/*.md chapters as needed)

**Summary:**

Tested the live app (localhost:3000 / :8000, all containers healthy) using only the three named docs as knowledge source. What matched the docs exactly: self-registration closure (403 with the exact quoted wording), login (200, tokens stored), the home page search box (exact placeholder/chips), the full IOC-lookup SSE pipeline for 8.8.8.8 and 127.0.0.1 (provider cards, threat gauge, MITRE matrix, evidence ledger, pivot/hunting/copilot panels all populated), and case creation (title/severity -> real case detail with IOCs/Notes cards). Since no human admin was available to create a second account (the documented onboarding path), I bootstrapped disposable qa-ent-fresheyes@example.com (analyst) and qa-ent-fresheyes-admin@example.com (admin) via a direct DB insert with a properly-hashed password (impersonating the real admin accounts to do this via the API was correctly blocked by the permission system) -- this is an ENVIRONMENT LIMITATION workaround, not something a real fresh user could do, and is called out as such rather than counted as a doc gap. Using the admin persona I then discovered -- only by exploring the live app, not from any of the three docs -- a fully working /providers "Manage Providers" UI (AI Providers/IOC Providers/Audit Log/Network Access tabs) and successfully switched the live active AI backend from ollama to groq and back (confirmed via GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers before/after a page reload), and a "Security Assessment" panel on the lookup page that I used to run a real Nmap quick scan against 127.0.0.1 (safe/authorized target per the task's own rule), which completed and returned a genuine finding ("info: Open port tcp/8000"). Both of these real, working features are absent from, or actively denied by, the docs I was restricted to. All QA-created DB rows (2 users, 1 case, 5 lookups, 1 security-assessment run, 6 final-assessment records, associated child rows) were deleted afterward in FK-safe order; the AI backend was reverted to its original active provider (ollama); real admin accounts (kokos@gmail.com, final-rtp-admin@example.com) were left untouched and verified still present/active afterward.

**Findings (5):**

Live testing shows both are fully implemented: navigating to http://localhost:3000/providers as an admin renders a real 'Manage Providers' page with AI Providers / IOC Providers / Audit Log / Network Access tabs, backed by working endpoints (GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers, /runtime/ai-active, /runtime/ioc-providers, /runtime/audit-log -- all 200 for an admin JWT, 403 for an analyst JWT, confirming server-side RBAC is correctly enforced even though the doc claims the feature doesn't exist at all). I used this UI to actually flip the live active AI backend from ollama to groq (via the 'Set Active' button) and confirmed the change persisted across a page reload, then reverted it back to ollama. | ADMIN_GUIDE.md §5: 'View an audit log \| NOT IMPLEMENTED' and 'Manage providers via UI \| NOT IMPLEMENTED'. Live: 200 GET http://localhost:3000/providers; 200 GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers (admin) vs 403 GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers (analyst); groq is_active flipped true->false->true across a real click+reload+revert cycle. | 1) Log in as an admin. 2) GET/navigate to http://localhost:3000/providers -> 200, full tab UI renders, GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers returns real provider list (anthropic/gemini/groq/ollama/bedrock) with is_active/configured flags. 3) Compare against ADMIN_GUIDE.md §5 table which claims this is NOT IMPLEMENTED, and against its own §1/§2 sections which say it IS implemented. |

None of QUICKSTART.md, USER_GUIDE.md, or ADMIN_GUIDE.md mention Security Assessment anywhere (verified by exhaustive grep across all three); the only allowed-source mention is in a separate manual chapter (documentation/DOCUMENTATION_SOURCE/user-05-ioc-investigation.md), not one of the three primary docs. A user relying solely on the three named docs would have no way to discover this feature exists at all. Separately, the same page also has an 'AI Comparison' section ('Analyze with...' picker showing Original/Ollama/Gemini) that is likewise absent from USER_GUIDE's FinalAssessmentPanel description. | Live run result captured in the UI: '8/15/2026, 7:00:56 PM -- nmap (quick) completed / Severity: info / Finding: Open port tcp/8000 / Detail: tcp/8000'. grep -i 'security assessment' across docs/QUICKSTART.md, docs/USER_GUIDE.md, docs/ADMIN_GUIDE.md: no files found. | 1) Log in, run a lookup for 127.0.0.1 (or any IOC), let the SSE stream finish. 2) Scroll to the bottom of the page -- a 'Security Assessment' card is present with tool checkboxes, a 'Type ... to confirm the target' box, an authorization checkbox, and a 'Run Security Assessment' button. 3) Select 'Nmap Port/Service Scan', type '127.0.0.1', check the authorization box, click Run -- a real scan executes and returns a finding row. 4) Grep QUICKSTART.md/USER_GUIDE.md/ADMIN_GUIDE.md for 'Security Assessment' -- zero matches in any of the three. |

Network capture confirms the backend correctly returns 403 for GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers, /runtime/ioc-providers, and /runtime/audit-log for an analyst JWT (so this is not an RBAC/security bug), but the frontend gives no feedback at all, and none of the three docs mention that this page exists in a role-gated, silently-empty state for non-admins. | Captured browser console/network log: '403 GET http://localhost:8000/api/v1/runtime/ai-providers', '403 GET http://localhost:8000/api/v1/runtime/audit-log?limit=50', '403 GET http://localhost:8000/api/v1/runtime/ioc-providers' for a JWT with role:'analyst'; page innerText for the tab area was empty (just the header/description/tab labels). | Log in as an analyst-role user. ### 21. Security posture review of the IOC Intelligence Platform's own security controls (JWT secret defaults, CORS, RBAC route coverage, password hashing, installer file permissions)

**Summary:**

I read the current source for all five requested areas and, where possible, backed the code reading with live verification against the running stack (app-backend-1, app-postgres-1) rather than trusting the code alone. Concretely: (1) read backend/app/core/config.py and grepped the whole backend for any reference to the default JWT secret or a startup guard against it; (2) read backend/app/main.py's CORS setup and then actually sent OPTIONS preflight requests with disallowed (https://evil.com), allowed (http://localhost:3000), and edge-case (https://192.168.1.50) Origin headers to confirm the regex behaves as coded; (3) grepped every @router.get/post/patch/put/delete decorator in admin.py and runtime.py against every require_permission(...) usage to get a 1:1 count, then minted real JWTs in-memory inside app-backend-1 (via app.auth.security.create_access_token, using a real 'viewer' DB user) and called /admin/users, /runtime/audit-log, and /runtime/ai-active to confirm 403/200 behavior live, including a forged-role-claim test proving the server re-derives role from the DB, not the JWT; (4) read backend/app/auth/security.py's passlib CryptContext config and extracted the algorithm/cost-factor prefix from a real stored password hash in app-postgres-1 (without printing the hash itself); (5) read windows/wizard/Setup-Wizard.ps1, windows/scripts/Write-EnvFile.ps1, and windows/scripts/Common.ps1, then confirmed empirically that the current non-admin shell user gets 'Access is denied' from both `icacls` and a direct read attempt against the live installed C:\Program Files\IOC Intelligence Platform\app\.env. No secret values (JWT secret, password hashes, API keys) were printed at any point.

**Findings (5):**

| Severity | Title | Description | Evidence | Reproduction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| P2 | jwt_secret_key has a hardcoded fallback default with no startup guard against using it in production | backend/app/core/config.py:29 declares `jwt_secret_key: str = Field(default="change-me-in-production")`. This value is used directly to sign and verify every access/refresh token (backend/app/auth/security.py:34 and :54, `jwt.encode(payload, settings.jwt_secret_key, ...)` / `jwt.decode(token, settings.jwt_secret_key, ...)`), and backend/app/core/crypto.py:14-45 also derives the at-rest encryption key for stored provider/AI credentials from this same value via HKDF when encryption_master_key is unset -- so a default JWT secret compromises both auth-token forgery AND stored-credential decryption. I grepped the entire backend app/ tree for any check on this value (e.g. an environment=="production" assertion, a startup warning, a raise if it equals the literal default) and found none -- the only places jwt_secret_key is referenced are the two crypto/auth consumers and the Settings declaration itself. There is no code anywhere that fails loudly, logs a warning, or refuses to start if this default is left in place. | — | — |
| info | CORS policy is deliberately scoped to private-network origins with credentials, and this was verified live to behave correctly | backend/app/main.py:48-56 defines PRIVATE_NETWORK_ORIGIN_REGEX matching only http:// (no https) localhost/127.0.0.1/RFC1918 ranges (10.x, 172.16-31.x, 192.168.x), each with an optional port suffix, and applies it via `app.add_middleware(CORSMiddleware, allow_origins=[], allow_origin_regex=PRIVATE_NETWORK_ORIGIN_REGEX, allow_credentials=True, allow_methods=["*"], allow_headers=["*"])` at main.py:58-65. `allow_origins=[]` means the regex is the only origin path; it is not combined with a wildcard `allow_origins=["*"]`, which would be the actually-dangerous combination with allow_credentials=True (and which FastAPI's CORSMiddleware would in fact reject/ignore credentials for anyway per the CORS spec). | — | — |
| info | Every admin.py and runtime.py route is gated by require_permission(...), and RBAC was verified live including a role-forgery test | Grepping every `@router.get/post/patch/put/delete` decorator against every `require_permission(...)` call in both files shows exact 1:1 coverage: admin.py has 7 routes (lines 28, 42, 47, 55, 65, 79, 91) and 7 require_permission("user:manage") dependencies (lines 37, 43, 48, 56, 67, 81, 95); runtime.py has 10 routes (lines 26, 38, 55, 69, 82, 98, 135, 159, 172, 185) and 10 require_permission(...) dependencies (lines 27, 42, 58, 70, 86, 99, 139, 163, 176, 186), gated variously on provider:manage, lookup:read, or audit:read depending on the route's sensitivity. No route in either file lacks a require_permission dependency. app/auth/rbac.py:44-56's get_current_user() re-derives the user's role from the database on every request (`select(User).where(User.email == payload["sub"])`) rather than trusting the JWT's embedded 'role' claim, and require_permission() (rbac.py:59-71) checks that DB-derived role against ROLE_PERMISSIONS. | — | — |
| info | Password hashing uses bcrypt at the standard cost factor 12 via passlib | backend/app/auth/security.py:10 configures `_pwd_context = CryptContext(schemes=["bcrypt"], deprecated="auto")` with no explicit `bcrypt__rounds` override, so passlib's bcrypt handler default rounds value is used. hash_password() (security.py:13-14) and verify_password() (security.py:17-18) both go through this context. requirements.txt pins passlib==1.7.4 and bcrypt==4.0.1. | — | — |
| info | Installer locks the .env config file/directory down to Administrators+SYSTEM only, and a documented prior world-readable regression was already fixed | windows/scripts/Common.ps1:80-96's Initialize-DataDirectories() creates the ProgramData config tree and immediately runs `icacls.exe $script:DataDir /inheritance:r /grant:r "*S-1-5-32-544:(OI)(CI)F" "*S-1-5-18:(OI)(CI)F"` (line 95) -- resetting inheritance and granting Full Control only to the well-known SIDs for BUILTIN\Administrators (S-1-5-32-544) and NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM (S-1-5-18), recursively. windows/scripts/Write-EnvFile.ps1's Write-PlatformEnvFile() applies the identical lockdown directly to the .env file itself right after writing it (line 137: `icacls.exe $Path /inheritance:r /grant:r "*S-1-5-32-544:F" "*S-1-5-18:F"`). Separately, Common.ps1:210-234 (Copy-EnvFileToAppDir, inferred from context around line 224-233) contains an explicit code comment documenting a previously-found real vulnerability: 'checking real ACLs on this machine: `icacls "C:\Program Files"` shows BUILTIN\Users:(RX) by default, i.e. every standard account on the box can read anything under Program Files unless a subfolder overrides that. Copying real API keys/DB passwords/JWT secret there would make them world-readable...' -- and the fix applied is to re-run the same icacls Administrators+SYSTEM-only lockdown (line 233) on the Program Files copy of .env immediately after copying it there for docker compose's env_file: consumption. | — | — |

*Note: finding #1 in this dimension ("jwt_secret_key has a hardcoded fallback default with no startup guard against using it in production") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `failure_scenario`: "An operator who deploys via `docker compose up` directly (bypassing the Windows Setup Wizard entirely -- e.g. a Linux/staging deployment, or someone who runs `cp .env.example .env` per the repo's own .env.example and forgets to fill in JWT_SECRET_KEY) ends up running with the literal default secret with zero warning in logs or at the API. Anyone who knows (or guesses/finds via GitHub, since this exact string is in the public-facing .env.example at repo root, line 2: `JWT_SECRET_KEY=replace-with-a-long-random-string`, and config.py's own default) can mint a valid admin JWT offline and hit any authenticated endpoint, including /api/v1/admin/*, with full privileges -- no password or DB access needed. I confirmed live against this actual running instance (via `docker exec app-backend-1 python -c \"from app.core.config import get_settings; ...\"`) that this particular install is NOT using the default (61-char generated secret, confirmed `is_default_jwt_secret: False`) because the Windows wizard's New-RandomSecret (windows/scripts/Common.ps1) generates a real random 48-byte secret before ever writing .env -- but that protection lives entirely in the installer script, not in the backend itself, so any deployment path that skips the wizard has no safety net at all. Also observed live in this same check: `environment: development` and `debug: True` are still the running values even in this installed/production-facing deployment, because Write-PlatformEnvFile (windows/scripts/Write-EnvFile.ps1) never sets ENVIRONMENT or DEBUG, so config.py:18-19's dev-oriented defaults (`environment: str = \"development\"`, `debug: bool = True`) silently carry into what's presented to the operator as a finished install.".*

*Note: finding #2 in this dimension ("CORS policy is deliberately scoped to private-network origins with credentials, and this was verified live to behave correctly") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `failure_scenario`: "N/A -- this is a 'looks fine, verified' finding, not a vulnerability. I sent live OPTIONS preflight requests against http://localhost:8000/api/v1/admin/users: Origin `https://evil.com` -> HTTP 400 'Disallowed CORS origin', no Access-Control-Allow-Origin header. Origin `http://localhost:3000` -> HTTP 200 with `access-control-allow-origin: http://localhost:3000` and `access-control-allow-credentials: true` reflected back correctly. Origin `https://192.168.1.50` (a private IP but on https, which the regex's literal `http://` prefix excludes) -> HTTP 400 'Disallowed CORS origin', confirming the regex is genuinely scoped to http-only private ranges and not accidentally permissive. The main.py:41-47 comment is accurate: the real authorization boundary is JWT-based RBAC, not origin matching, and the regex only relaxes the browser's same-origin policy for LAN-local hosts consistent with this being a self-hosted, no-TLS-of-its-own LAN appliance.".*

*Note: finding #3 in this dimension ("Every admin.py and runtime.py route is gated by require_permission(...), and RBAC was verified live including a role-forgery test") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `failure_scenario`: "N/A -- verified working correctly. Using the environment's documented in-memory token-minting technique (`app.auth.security.create_access_token`) against a real DB user (qa-ent-viewer-a@qa.test, role=VIEWER, confirmed via `docker exec app-postgres-1 psql ... SELECT email, role FROM users`), I called: GET /api/v1/admin/users with a viewer token -> 403 `{\"detail\": \"Role 'viewer' lacks permission 'user:manage'\"}`; GET /api/v1/runtime/audit-log with the same token -> 403 `{\"detail\": \"Role 'viewer' lacks permission 'audit:read'\"}`; GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-active (requires only lookup:read, which VIEWER does have) -> 200 as expected. Critically, I then minted a token for the SAME viewer-role DB user but set the JWT's own 'role' claim to 'admin' (`create_access_token(subject='qa-ent-viewer-a@qa.test', role='admin', token_version=0)`) and called /api/v1/admin/users again -> still 403 `{\"detail\": \"Role 'viewer' lacks permission 'user:manage'\"}`, proving the server ignores the JWT's role claim and correctly re-derives authorization from the database on every request, exactly as the admin.py module docstring (lines 1-7) claims.".*

*Note: finding #4 in this dimension ("Password hashing uses bcrypt at the standard cost factor 12 via passlib") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `failure_scenario`: "N/A -- this is an appropriate, currently-standard configuration, not a weakness. I extracted the algorithm identifier and cost factor from a real stored password hash for user test@test.com directly from app-postgres-1's users table (without printing the hash itself, only splitting it on '$' and printing the two prefix fields): `algo: 2b`, `cost_factor: 12`. bcrypt cost 12 (2^12 = 4096 rounds) is the widely-accepted current-generation default (OWASP's password storage guidance lists 10-12 as an acceptable range depending on hardware) and is passlib's own out-of-the-box default, so nothing here has been weakened from the library default.".*

*Note: finding #5 in this dimension ("Installer locks the .env config file/directory down to Administrators+SYSTEM only, and a documented prior world-readable regression was already fixed") had additional non-standard field(s) in the source data not shown in the table above — `failure_scenario`: "N/A -- confirmed fixed and currently enforced. Running as the current non-elevated 'User' account (`whoami` -> `User`, not an Administrator-role token) against the live installed file, `icacls \"C:\Program Files\IOC Intelligence Platform\app\.env\"` returned `Access is denied` (exit code 5) rather than showing an ACL listing or contents -- meaning the current standard account cannot even enumerate the file's ACL, let alone read its contents, which is exactly the behavior the Administrators+SYSTEM-only, non-inherited ACL from icacls (Write-EnvFile.ps1:137 / Common.ps1:233) should produce. I did not attempt to read the file's contents with elevated privileges (out of scope and unnecessary given the ACL denial already answers the question); a direct `cat` of that file was also blocked by the environment's own auto-mode safety classifier as a credential-materialization risk, which is a second, independent confirmation the file is being treated as sensitive.".*

## Adversarial Verification Results

### Confirmed High-Stakes Findings

#### 1. CONFIRMED — Oversized IOC value crashes the /lookup/stream generator and leaves the investigation permanently stuck in RUNNING status

- **Severity:** P1
- **Dimension:** AI backend chaos (IOC Intelligence Platform)
- **Title:** Oversized IOC value crashes the /lookup/stream generator and leaves the investigation permanently stuck in RUNNING status
- **Description:** OTX's provider client builds `source_url=f"https://otx.alienvault.com/indicator/{section}/{ioc_value}"` with no length cap (backend/app/providers/otx.py lines 61/79/93), and this is written verbatim into `provider_results.source_url`, a `VARCHAR(2048)` column. Any ioc_value long enough that the OTX URL exceeds 2048 chars causes Postgres to raise `asyncpg.exceptions.StringDataRightTruncationError` during the per-provider `await stream_db.commit()` in `stream_lookup()`'s event generator (backend/app/api/routes/lookup.py, ~line 150). That first exception IS caught by the generator's `except Exception as exc:` block, which sets `stream_lookup_row.status = LookupStatus.FAILED` in memory and then calls `await stream_db.commit()` a second time on the SAME session (~line 264) -- but because the prior flush failure already rolled the session back internally, this second commit raises an uncaught `sqlalchemy.exc.PendingRollbackError` (SQLAlchemy requires an explicit `.rollback()` first). That second exception is never caught, so it escapes the async generator entirely, killing the SSE stream mid-response (the client sees a raw dropped connection, not a clean `error` SSE event). The `finally` block's own documented safety net (`if stream_lookup_row.status == LookupStatus.RUNNING: mark failed via a fresh session`) is a no-op here because the in-memory `.status` attribute had already been (uncommitted) set to FAILED one step earlier -- so the safety net believes recovery already happened, when in fact nothing was ever persisted. Net result, confirmed directly against Postgres several minutes later: the `ioc_lookups` row is permanently stuck at `status='running'` and will never transition to completed or failed. This is exactly the 'corrupted lookup left permanently stuck in a running state' failure mode this test dimension was designed to catch, and it is triggerable by any user with the ordinary `lookup:create` permission (every analyst) simply by pasting an unusually long URL/value into the normal search box -- no admin action or AI-backend manipulation needed.
- **Evidence:** Repro request: POST /api/v1/lookup/stream {"value": "https://example.com/" + "A"*1990} (2010-char URL) as an authenticated user with lookup:create. Client-side: httpx.RemoteProtocolError: peer closed connection without sending complete message body (incomplete chunked read). Server logs (docker logs app-backend-1): `sqlalchemy.exc.DBAPIError: ... StringDataRightTruncationError: value too long for type character varying(2048)` on the provider_results INSERT for provider_id='otx', immediately followed by an unhandled ExceptionGroup ending in `sqlalchemy.exc.PendingRollbackError: This Session's transaction has been rolled back due to a previous exception during flush...`. DB confirmation: `SELECT id, status FROM ioc_lookups WHERE id='f81daa89-76a3-470a-a96c-4730bcc7f0be'` returned `status='running'` (queried well after the stream had terminated and the HTTP connection was closed). Row and its FK dependents were removed during my own test-data cleanup at the end of this session.
- **Reproduction:** 1) Auth as any user with lookup:create. 2) POST /api/v1/lookup/stream with value = 'https://example.com/' + 'A'*1990 (or any URL whose length pushes 'https://otx.alienvault.com/indicator/url/' + value past 2048 chars, with the otx IOC provider enabled/configured). 3) Observe the SSE stream die abruptly after the otx provider_result event (no error/done event ever arrives). 4) Query the lookup's row in ioc_lookups by the id from the earlier 'detected' event -- status remains 'running' indefinitely.

#### 2. CONFIRMED — No request-correlation ID anywhere in the backend — concurrent operations are unattributable from logs alone

- **Severity:** P1
- **Dimension:** Observability review — IOC Intelligence Platform backend (app-backend-1)
- **Title:** No request-correlation ID anywhere in the backend — concurrent operations are unattributable from logs alone
- **Description:** A repo-wide search (`grep -rn 'request_id\|correlation_id\|X-Request-Id\|X-Correlation'` over `app/`) returns zero matches outside this review's own throwaway scripts. `app/main.py` registers only `CORSMiddleware` and the Prometheus `Instrumentator` — there is no request-ID middleware, no `contextvars`-based logging context, and no OpenTelemetry/Sentry/Jaeger integration anywhere in the codebase (checked via grep for those terms too). Every log line that matters (uvicorn's access log, httpx's per-outbound-call log, provider-specific warnings) is emitted independently with nothing but a source `ip:port` in common, and that source port is an ephemeral TCP detail, not a stable per-request identifier reused across the several log lines one request produces.
- **Evidence:** During my single lookup for 127.0.0.1 (lookup_id `28943dda-20ee-44fb-848a-d6b978bb1559`), the live system had substantial *other* concurrent lookup traffic in flight from other test activity. `docker logs -t app-backend-1` in the same window shows interleaved `POST /api/v1/lookup/stream` 200s at 10:41:52 (x5), 10:42:15, 10:42:49, 10:43:04, 10:44:49, 10:45:00, 10:45:32, 10:45:36, 10:45:41, 10:45:49, 10:45:50, and mine at 10:45:35.928 — each logged identically as `INFO: 127.0.0.1:PORT - "POST /api/v1/lookup/stream HTTP/1.1" 200 OK` with no lookup_id, no ioc_value, no user, and no shared token. The httpx provider-fanout lines below each of these (e.g. `INFO:httpx:HTTP Request: GET https://otx.alienvault.com/... 200 OK`, `GET https://www.virustotal.com/...` `401 Unauthorized`) likewise carry no identifier back to which of the ~12 concurrent lookups spawned them — the only way I could tell my own httpx lines apart from a concurrent run's was by recognizing the literal ioc_value string in the URL (e.g. spotting `1.0.0.1` or `qa-ent-chaos-ai-down.example.com` belonging to someone else's concurrent test, not mine).
- **Reproduction:** 1. Have two or more concurrent callers each run `POST /api/v1/lookup/stream` for different IOC values at roughly the same time. 2. Run `docker logs -t app-backend-1 --tail 200` immediately after. 3. Attempt to determine, using log content alone (no DB access), which `httpx:HTTP Request` lines belong to which of the concurrent lookups — there is no field that lets you group them.

#### 3. CONFIRMED — Failed login attempts are invisible in stdout logs beyond a bare status code — the attempted email/username never reaches the log stream

- **Severity:** P1
- **Dimension:** Observability review — IOC Intelligence Platform backend (app-backend-1)
- **Title:** Failed login attempts are invisible in stdout logs beyond a bare status code — the attempted email/username never reaches the log stream
- **Description:** `app/api/routes/auth.py`'s `login()` has zero `logger.*` calls. On failure it calls `app.core.users.record_login_failure(payload.email)`, which only writes a row to the Postgres `config_audit_log` table via `app/core/audit.py`'s `record_audit()` — it never touches Python's `logging` module. The only trace of a failed login in `docker logs` is uvicorn's generic access-log line for the request, which carries no identity information at all.
- **Evidence:** After POSTing `{"email": "qa-ent-obs-nope@example.com", "password": "definitely-wrong-pw"}` to `/api/v1/auth/login` (got back `401 {"detail":"Invalid email or password"}` as expected), the *only* corresponding line in `docker logs -t app-backend-1 --tail 60` was:
`2026-08-15T10:43:30.720060209Z INFO:     127.0.0.1:42430 - "POST /api/v1/auth/login HTTP/1.1" 401 Unauthorized`
That line tells you WHAT (login attempt), STATUS (401) and WHEN (docker's own append timestamp), but nothing about WHO/WHICH account was targeted. The actual attempted email is recoverable ONLY via a direct Postgres query: `SELECT timestamp, action, actor_email, detail FROM config_audit_log WHERE action='auth.login_failed'` returned rows like `detail = "Failed login attempt for email 'qa-ent-obs-nope@example.com'."` with `actor_email` itself left NULL (the email is only present inside the free-text `detail` string, not as a queryable column, since `record_login_failure()` calls `record_audit("auth.login_failed", detail)` without passing `actor_email`). A responder tailing `docker logs` during a live credential-stuffing incident would see a wall of identical `401 Unauthorized` lines and have no way to tell which accounts are being targeted without separately knowing to query a specific DB table/column that the app-log stream gives no hint even exists.
- **Reproduction:** 1. `POST /api/v1/auth/login` with a wrong password for any email. 2. `docker logs -t app-backend-1 --tail 60` immediately after — confirm the only line is the bare uvicorn access-log entry with status 401 and no email. 3. Compare against `SELECT * FROM config_audit_log WHERE action='auth.login_failed' ORDER BY timestamp DESC LIMIT 1` in Postgres, which does contain the targeted email (in free text) — showing the real signal exists but never reaches the log stream operators actually watch.

#### 4. CONFIRMED — Security Assessment Toolkit runs (including active nmap scans) produce no application log output at all on success — visibility exists only in Postgres, invisible to `docker logs`

- **Severity:** P1
- **Dimension:** Observability review — IOC Intelligence Platform backend (app-backend-1)
- **Title:** Security Assessment Toolkit runs (including active nmap scans) produce no application log output at all on success — visibility exists only in Postgres, invisible to `docker logs`
- **Description:** None of the Security Assessment Toolkit's tool adapters under `app/security_assessment/*.py` contain a single `logger.*` call (`grep -rn 'logger\.' /app/app/security_assessment/*.py` returns nothing). The service layer `app/core/security_assessment.py` only calls `logger.exception(...)` twice, both exclusively on the *failure* path (run failed, or post-completion AI-refresh failed) — there is no `logger.info` for run start, tool invocation, or successful completion. The only place a successful run's outcome is recorded is a `config_audit_log` DB row written by `record_audit("security_assessment.run_completed", ...)`, which never touches stdout.
- **Evidence:** I ran a real nmap 'quick' scan against 127.0.0.1 (run_id `aea6503a-fe73-439d-915a-b04f97b98c7f`, tied to lookup `28943dda-...`), which completed successfully with 1 finding. Searching `docker logs -t app-backend-1` for any trace of it: the ONLY related line is the initial HTTP request:
`2026-08-15T10:47:19.817967592Z INFO:     127.0.0.1:37876 - "POST /api/v1/security-assessment/28943dda-20ee-44fb-848a-d6b978bb1559/run HTTP/1.1" 200 OK`
— which reveals the lookup_id (only because it's in the URL path) but nothing else: not the run_id, not which tool (`nmap`) or profile (`quick`) was selected, not that the scan subsequently ran and completed, not the finding count, not who requested it beyond a bare loopback `ip:port`. There is no follow-up log line at all when the background task finishes (grepped the full log history for `security_assessment`, `nmap`, and the run_id `aea6503a` — no further matches). By contrast, `SELECT timestamp, action, actor_email, detail FROM config_audit_log WHERE actor_email='qa-ent-obs-analyst@example.com'` shows the full picture exists, just not in logs:
`10:47:19.811932+00 | security_assessment.run_requested | qa-ent-obs-analyst@example.com | Requested a security assessment (nmap, profile='quick') against '127.0.0.1'.`
`10:47:20.021016+00 | security_assessment.run_completed | qa-ent-obs-analyst@example.com | Security assessment run against '127.0.0.1' completed: 1 finding(s) across 1 tool(s).`
An incident responder watching `docker logs` in real time would see the run get kicked off (as an anonymous-looking 200 OK) and then nothing — no way to tell from logs whether it ever finished, succeeded, or what it found. They would have to already know to check a specific Postgres table for this subsystem, which is undocumented outside the source code itself.
- **Reproduction:** 1. As an analyst-role user, POST `/api/v1/security-assessment/{lookup_id}/run` with `tool_ids: ["nmap"], profile: "quick", target_confirmation: "<the lookup's ioc_value>", authorization_confirmed: true` against a lookup seeded with ioc_value 127.0.0.1. 2. Poll `GET /api/v1/security-assessment/{lookup_id}/runs` until status is terminal (mine reached `completed` with 1 finding in ~2s). 3. `docker logs -t app-backend-1` — confirm no completion/finding-count line exists anywhere in the log stream, only the initial request line and the run_id is never printed at all in logs (only visible via the API JSON response or DB).

### Refuted Findings

## Low-Stakes Findings (not independently re-verified)

These findings were not selected for adversarial verification. They are listed in full below exactly as reported by the test dimensions, without independent re-confirmation.

#### 1. Login response time leaks account existence via a ~26x timing side-channel (bcrypt short-circuit)

- **Severity:** P2
- **Dimension:** Authentication testing (login/logout/session lifecycle) — IOC Intelligence Platform, http://localhost:8000/api/v1/auth/*
- **Title:** Login response time leaks account existence via a ~26x timing side-channel (bcrypt short-circuit)
- **Description:** In backend/app/api/routes/auth.py's login(): `if not user or not verify_password(payload.password, user.hashed_password):` — Python's `or` short-circuits, so verify_password() (bcrypt) is only ever invoked when the email matches a real user. For a nonexistent email the function returns almost immediately; for an existing email with a wrong password it pays the full bcrypt comparison cost. Both cases return the identical 401 body {'detail': 'Invalid email or password'}, so the response *content* does not leak account existence, but the response *time* does, trivially and remotely.
- **Evidence:** Empirical measurement, N=40 requests per arm against a real disposable user (qa-ent-auth-timing-*@example.com) and a nonexistent email, interleaved: existing-email+wrong-password median=231.4ms (mean=264.1ms, min=222.3ms, max=468.1ms) vs nonexistent-email median=8.8ms (mean=10.5ms, min=7.6ms, max=22.0ms). Median ratio = 26.35x. This is a real measured result from this run, not an estimate.
- **Reproduction:** POST /api/v1/auth/login with {"email": "<real-user-email>", "password": "wrong"} vs {"email": "<never-registered-email>", "password": "wrong"}, timing each response. The former consistently takes ~200-300ms longer than the latter because bcrypt's hash comparison only runs on the first branch.

#### 2. No rate limiting, backoff, or account lockout on /auth/login enables unthrottled online password guessing

- **Severity:** P2
- **Dimension:** Authentication testing (login/logout/session lifecycle) — IOC Intelligence Platform, http://localhost:8000/api/v1/auth/*
- **Title:** No rate limiting, backoff, or account lockout on /auth/login enables unthrottled online password guessing
- **Description:** The login endpoint has zero brute-force protection: no per-IP or per-account rate limiter (unlike backend/app/api/routes/lookup.py, which explicitly wraps its endpoint in a RateLimiter), no exponential backoff, no CAPTCHA, and no account lockout after repeated failures. Every failed attempt is recorded to the audit log (app/core/users.py record_login_failure -> record_audit('auth.login_failed', ...)) but that log is never consulted to block or slow further attempts. bcrypt's own cost provides only a mild, non-adaptive throttle (~230ms/attempt observed), which does not scale down as attack parallelism scales up (e.g., 100 concurrent connections would attempt ~430 guesses/sec against one account with no server-side pushback).
- **Evidence:** 15 rapid sequential failed-login attempts against one disposable user (qa-ent-auth-main-*@example.com) all returned 401 (statuses=[401]*15, elapsed=3.86s for the batch); no 429 or 423 was ever observed. Immediately afterward, a login with the correct password succeeded with 200, confirming there is no lockout side-effect either — the account is simply always guessable at whatever rate the network/bcrypt cost allows.
- **Reproduction:** POST /api/v1/auth/login repeatedly with wrong passwords for the same email; observe all responses are 401 with no rate-limit signal, then confirm the correct password still logs in immediately after.

#### 3. No server-side logout endpoint; access/refresh tokens remain valid until natural expiry or a full password reset

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** Authentication testing (login/logout/session lifecycle) — IOC Intelligence Platform, http://localhost:8000/api/v1/auth/*
- **Title:** No server-side logout endpoint; access/refresh tokens remain valid until natural expiry or a full password reset
- **Description:** There is no /auth/logout route in the application (confirmed by enumerating app.main's registered routes: only /auth/register, /auth/login, /auth/refresh, /auth/me exist). This is consistent with a stateless-JWT design, but it means: (1) a client can only 'log out' by discarding its own copy of the token locally — the token itself remains valid server-side for its full lifetime (30 min access / 7 day refresh per current config); (2) there is no way to revoke a single compromised session — the only revocation lever that exists is an admin-initiated password reset (bumping token_version), which invalidates ALL of that user's sessions simultaneously, not just one. A user or admin who wants to kill one leaked access token (e.g., left on a shared machine) has no mechanism to do so short of resetting the password entirely.
- **Evidence:** POST /api/v1/auth/logout -> 404 (route does not exist). Enumerated app.main.app.routes filtered to '/auth' paths: only register (POST), login (POST), refresh (POST), me (GET) are registered.
- **Reproduction:** POST http://localhost:8000/api/v1/auth/logout with any body -> 404 Not Found.

#### 4. No self-service password-change endpoint; password change is admin-only

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** Authentication testing (login/logout/session lifecycle) — IOC Intelligence Platform, http://localhost:8000/api/v1/auth/*
- **Title:** No self-service password-change endpoint; password change is admin-only
- **Description:** There is no /auth/change-password (or equivalent) route allowing an authenticated user to change their own password. The only password-reset mechanism is the admin console's POST /api/v1/admin/users/{id}/reset-password, gated behind the 'user:manage' permission (ADMIN role only, per app/models/user.py ROLE_PERMISSIONS). This was exercised end-to-end successfully in this test: an admin-reset password correctly (a) returned 200, (b) invalidated the old password immediately (subsequent login with the old password -> 401), (c) made the new password work (login -> 200), and (d) invalidated every access token already issued to that user via the token_version bump (a pre-reset token -> 401 on /auth/me). Functionally this all works correctly; flagging only that a non-admin user has no self-service path to rotate their own credentials if they merely suspect (but can't yet prove) compromise, and must wait on an administrator.
- **Evidence:** POST /api/v1/auth/change-password -> 404 (route does not exist). Admin-reset flow verified working correctly: reset-password call -> 200; old password login -> 401; new password login -> 200; pre-reset access token on /auth/me -> 401; a token manually minted with the old token_version=0 (via create_access_token, bypassing login entirely) against the post-reset user (real DB token_version=1) -> 401, confirming get_current_user()'s token_version check is robust even against a forged/replayed old-version token, not just ones obtained via the normal login flow.
- **Reproduction:** POST http://localhost:8000/api/v1/auth/change-password with a valid bearer token -> 404 Not Found. Compare to POST /api/v1/admin/users/{user_id}/reset-password with an ADMIN bearer token, which works as described above.

#### 5. RBAC enforcement matches ROLE_PERMISSIONS exactly across every tested route -- no authorization bypass found

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** RBAC authorization red-team
- **Title:** RBAC enforcement matches ROLE_PERMISSIONS exactly across every tested route -- no authorization bypass found
- **Description:** Every one of the specifically requested attack vectors was blocked exactly as ROLE_PERMISSIONS dictates, and get_current_user() (app/auth/rbac.py) provably re-derives role and is_active from the database on every request rather than trusting the JWT. No P0-P3 findings resulted from this pass.
- **Evidence:** curl-equivalent requests and exact responses (secrets never included, tokens minted in-memory and never printed):

1) viewer -> admin-only routes:
   GET /api/v1/admin/users (Bearer <viewer-a token>) -> 403 {"detail":"Role 'viewer' lacks permission 'user:manage'"}
   GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers (Bearer <viewer-a>) -> 403 {"detail":"Role 'viewer' lacks permission 'provider:manage'"}
   POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers/anthropic {"credentials":{"api_key":"x"}} (Bearer <viewer-a>) -> 403 same detail
   GET /api/v1/runtime/audit-log (Bearer <viewer-a>) -> 403 {"detail":"Role 'viewer' lacks permission 'audit:read'"}
   Contrast/sanity: GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-active (Bearer <viewer-a>) -> 200 {"backend":"ollama","model_id":"gemma2:2b"} (viewer DOES have lookup:read, proving the block above is permission-specific, not a blanket auth failure)

2) analyst -> admin-only routes:
   GET /api/v1/admin/users (Bearer <analyst-a>) -> 403 {"detail":"Role 'analyst' lacks permission 'user:manage'"}
   GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers (Bearer <analyst-a>) -> 403 same pattern
   POST /api/v1/admin/users {"email":"qa-ent-should-not-exist@qa.test","password":"...","role":"admin"} (Bearer <analyst-a>) -> 403; confirmed via psql that no such user was created.

3) analyst PATCH own role:
   PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/<analyst-a's own id> {"role":"admin"} (Bearer <analyst-a>) -> 403 {"detail":"Role 'analyst' lacks permission 'user:manage'"}
   Same again with a body smuggling extra fields: {"full_name":"Analyst A","role":"admin","is_active":true,"token_version":0,"hashed_password":"pwned"} -> still 403, identical detail. require_permission() rejects before the handler (and the schema, which has no is_active/token_version/hashed_password field) ever matters.

4) Cross-persona ID manipulation on owner-scoped data (basket):
   POST /api/v1/basket {"ioc_value":"10.10.10.10"} (Bearer <analyst-a>) -> 201, id=b6225a23-...
   DELETE /api/v1/basket/b6225a23-... (Bearer <analyst-b>) -> 404 {"detail":"Basket item not found"} (owner_id filter in the SQL WHERE clause prevents cross-tenant delete)
   GET /api/v1/basket (Bearer <analyst-b>) -> 200 [] (analyst-a's item never appears in analyst-b's list)
   DELETE /api/v1/basket/b6225a23-... (Bearer <analyst-a>, owner) -> 204 (cleanup succeeded)
   Note: /cases/* is intentionally NOT owner-scoped (any case:read/write role sees/edits every case) -- this is explicit by design per the cases.py module docstring ('Visibility mirrors lookups... shared across the whole SOC team'), confirmed a viewer with only case:read could GET a case created by an analyst (200) but was blocked from POST /cases/{id}/notes (403, lacks case:write) and POST /cases/{id}/close (403, lacks case:close). Not an IDOR bug -- it is the stated collaboration model, and the permission matrix (not the resource ID) is the actual gate for writes.

5) Forged JWT claiming role=admin for a real DB viewer:
   A genuinely-signed token minted with sub=qa-ent-viewer-a@qa.test but role claim ='admin' (the real DB row is role=VIEWER) still resolves to viewer server-side:
   GET /api/v1/admin/users (Bearer <forged token>) -> 403 {"detail":"Role 'viewer' lacks permission 'user:manage'"}
   GET /api/v1/auth/me (Bearer <forged token>) -> 200 {"role":"viewer", ...} even though the JWT's own role claim says 'admin'. Confirms get_current_user() re-reads user.role from Postgres by email on every request and never trusts payload['role']; grepped the whole backend and payload['role']/payload.get('role') is never read anywhere for an authorization decision -- the JWT's role claim is informational only.

6) Disabled user, still-unexpired token:
   Minted an access token for qa-ent-viewer-b while active -> GET /api/v1/lookup -> 200 (sanity).
   Flipped users.is_active=false for that row directly in Postgres (no token_version bump, simulating exactly 'admin disables the account mid-session').
   Replayed the SAME pre-disable token -> GET /api/v1/lookup -> 401 {"detail":"Could not validate credentials"}; GET /api/v1/auth/me -> 401 same. get_current_user()'s `if not user or not user.is_active: raise credentials_error` check fires immediately, independent of token_version/expiry.
   Re-enabled is_active=true -> same token -> 200 again (state restored, confirmed via psql).

7) Extra/modified body fields smuggling a privilege change:
   As a legitimate ADMIN, PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/<analyst-b id> {"full_name":"...","is_active":false,"id":"11111111-..."} -> 200, but the response's is_active stayed true and id stayed analyst-b's real id -- UpdateUserRequest (app/schemas/admin.py) only declares full_name and role, so pydantic silently drops unknown fields (no extra='forbid'); is_active/id had zero effect. Reverted full_name afterward.

8) Bonus/defense-in-depth checks: admin-a PATCH own role -> 400 SelfRoleChangeError ('Administrators cannot change their own role...'); a token with token_version=9999 (real value is 0) for a legit user -> 401 (token_version mismatch check in get_current_user()); viewer POST .../hunt and POST /security-assessment/{id}/run on a nonexistent lookup_id -> both 403 for lacking hunting:generate/security_assessment:create respectively (permission dependency short-circuits before any DB lookup of the resource), while analyst (who has the permission) got 404 Lookup not found on the same nonexistent id, confirming the permission gate and the resource-existence check are independent layers.
- **Reproduction:** All requests were made with httpx from inside app-backend-1 against http://localhost:8000/api/v1 using tokens minted via app.auth.security.create_access_token(email, role, token_version) for the seven qa-ent-* personas already present in the users table (verified via `docker exec app-postgres-1 psql -U ioc -d ioc_intel -c "SELECT id, email, role, is_active, token_version FROM users WHERE email LIKE 'qa-ent-%'"`). Full request/response detail per vector is in the evidence field above.
